## THÈSE POUR OBTENIR LE GRADE DE DOCTEUR DE L’UNIVERSITÉ DE MONTPELLIER

En Ecophysiologie et Adaptation des Plantes [SOURCE: Page_2]

École doctorale GAIA [SOURCE: Page_2]

Unité de recherche Plant Health Institute of Montpellier (PHIM) [SOURCE: Page_2]

REPONSES PHENOTYPIQUES ET MOLECULAIRES DU RIZ AU COURS D’INTERACTIONS AVEC DES MICROORGANISMES DU SOL [SOURCE: Page_2]

Présentée par Ludivine GUIGARD Le 24 novembre 2023 [SOURCE: Page_2]

Sous la direction de Lionel MOULIN Et Pierre CZERNIC [SOURCE: Page_2]

Devant le jury composé de Benoît LEFEBVRE, Directeur de Recherche, INRAE                                          Président du Jury Alia DELLAGI, Professeure des Universités, AgroParisTech                                Rapportrice Claire PRIGENT-COMBARET, Directrice de Recherche, CNRS                                  Examinatrice Maryline MAGNIN-ROBERT, Maître de Conférences, Université du Littoral de Côte d’Opale   Examinatrice Lionel MOULIN, Directeur de Recherche, iRD                                              Directeur de thèse Pierre CZERNIC, Professeur des Universités, Université de Montpellier                   Co-Directeur de thèse [SOURCE: Page_2]

« Cherchez toujours l’émerveillement dans la vie Considérez votre passé sans crainte, appréciez le présent sans mensonges et craignez un futur insensé » Tout, finit toujours, par passer [SOURCE: Page_4]

Il est l’heure. Il me tardait d’enfin commencer à écrire ces lignes. C’est drôle, c’est maintenant que j’en suis là, à taper ces quelques (non) mots, que je me rends compte que c’est la fin. Trois années de thèse, trois années de vie, trois années qui ont eu parfois des airs de trois siècles et maintenant seulement trois petites semaines. Je me sens un peu émue. Un peu triste que tout ce chapitre se termine, même si j’ai pu répéter l’inverse pendant des jours et des jours. Mais je me sens surtout très reconnaissante et il est l’heure de remercier. [SOURCE: Page_5]

Tout d’abord, je tiens à remercier chaleureusement mes directeurs de thèse, Lionel MOULIN et Pierre CZERNIC. Le directeur musical et le professeur number one. Elle me paraît lointaine, l’époque de notre toute première rencontre en Zoom, à cause de ce fichu Covid, en fin d’année scolaire 2020. Je devais paraître bien frêle derrière mon ordinateur et encore plus timide à ma première visite du labo. Je me souviendrais toujours de ton appel le soir des résultats Lionel, alors que j’avais passé toute la journée à rafraîchir la page des résultats du concours en vain. « Je ne veux rien spoiler, mais tu ferais bien de regarder le site de l’Ecole Doc et puis de me rappeler, je pense qu’on doit un peu s’organiser ! » Merci à vous de m’avoir fait confiance tout le long de cette thèse, merci à toi Pierre, qui était toujours si content de m’épauler pendant les manips (parce que « vive les bibis plantouilles » et les contrôles clippés à l’eau), merci pour ces après midi de brainstormings qui partaient toujours trop loin, pour votre aide pendant les gros rushs et les deadlines jamais loupés (parce qu’on travaille toujours mieux sous la pression). Merci pour tout ce que vous m’avez transmis, croyez-moi que je cultiverai cette curiosité et que j’en prendrais grand soin. [SOURCE: Page_5]

Je remercie également les membres de mon jury, pour avoir accepté d’évaluer mes travaux, que ce soit pour la lecture de ce manuscrit ou pour votre attention et nos échanges le jour de ma soutenance. Merci à mes rapporteurs, Alia DELLAGI et Benoît LEFEBVRE, ainsi qu’à mes examinatrices Claire PRIGENT-COMBARET et Maryline MAGNIN-ROBERT. Je souhaite également remercier les membres de mes comités de thèse, Jean-Benoît MOREL, Antony CHAMPION, Elsa BALLINI et Pierre Marc DELAUX, pour votre écoute et vos conseils avisés, ainsi que votre bienveillance pendant nos échanges. Un second remerciement à Jean-Benoît pour avoir accueilli ma thèse au sein de la nouvelle unité PHIM, et pour m’avoir écouté râler en tant que représentante des doctorants de l’unité. [SOURCE: Page_5]

Il est maintenant l’heure des merci encore plus personnels, des merci qui rendent un peu larmoyants, des merci qui serrent le cœur mais des jolis sentiments. Comme dirait un grand sage, allons-y Alonso. [SOURCE: Page_5]

Merci à mon équipe caféinée préférée, merci au trio qui a su écouter mes râlages, rire à mes blagues (pas si) drôles, péter des câbles au même moment que moi, que ce soit de larmes de joie ou de frustration, on était toujours là les uns pour les autres. Merci à Nicolas qui est rapidement devenu mon « partner in crime », pour le soutien indéfectible dont tu as fait preuve, pour les fous rires, les soirées sushis, les petites bières après le taff et les belles conversations qu’on a eues, surtout dans les moments difficiles, qui sont autant de choses que je garderai toujours dans un coin spécial de mon petit cœur tout mou. Merci à Mathilde, qui fait partie de l’équipe, même si ce n’est pas vraiment inscrit sur l’annuaire de PHIM. A nos cafés qui, de premier abord léger, tournaient invariablement à des leçons de vies ou des séances de thérapie pour l’une comme pour l’autre. J’espère que là où tu es, tu n’es que plus heureuse (ouais d’accord j’abuse, mais j’espère que les débuts à Mada ne sont pas trop rudes !!). Merci à Léa, d’abord pour ton énergie, ton inventivité et ta curiosité lorsque tu étais ma stagiaire (officieusement), et ensuite pour l’amitié qui s’est créée au fil des jours. Merci pour ces fous rires, pour ces questions existentielles, pour ce soutien indéfectible dont tu fais preuve avec tes amis. Merci d’avoir rendu la vie à Montpellier bien plus facile, plus douce, et simplement bien meilleure. [SOURCE: Page_6]

Merci à Gilles pour ton attention et ta bienveillance quand ça allait moins bien, merci d’avoir toujours eu le mot qu’il fallait (même s’ils se transforment parfois en blagues qui devraient être classées sur une fameuse échelle du vert au rouge …). Merci au reste de l’équipe, à Stéphane (C. comme B.), à Agnieszka, à Monique, à Isabelle, à Aurore, Sophie et Audrey. Merci à Jamel et à Emilie pour tous vos conseils quand mon riz n’était pas en forme et pour votre aide en serre. Merci à Malo, même si t’en fais plus parti, pour les mots que tu as su avoir quand il le fallait (mais pas merci pour la grippe à Aussois). Merci à Gabriel pour ton énergie toute nouvelle à la toute fin, elle est si douce et rafraîchissante qu’il me devait d’en parler ! Courage pour les CFU, et on croise les doigts pour ce concours ! Merci aux stagiaires que nous avons vu passer, et surtout à Marine, pour m’avoir écouté et montré que j’adorais la transmission de savoir. Merci à la nouvelle génération de thésards, merci à Sarah et Kakada pour la première relève des Briotes, merci à Lila pour tes astuces de pâtisserie et ton amitié qui fait tant de bien, merci à Emma de supporter notre énergie débordante avec Léa dans le bureau (même si tu n’es pas en reste sur les pétages de câbles, avoue-le). Merci à Michel pour ces soirées de détente au Titty (même si sur le coup c’est plus des traquenards qu’autre chose, tu nous manques quand même), merci à François pour ta motivation à boxer (sinon je n’aurai jamais eu le culot d’y aller). [SOURCE: Page_6]

Merci à l’ancienne génération, à Adrian pour m’avoir accueillie, que ce soit à Montpellier ou avec de bonnes bières à Vienna city, à Eoghan pour ton aide précieuse pour mes analyses et ces longues discussions qui redonnent la ferveur et la passion scientifique, à Moussa pour ta joie de vivre, à Marlène pour ton soutien et ta compréhension dans les moments difficiles. [SOURCE: Page_7]

Merci à mes amis qui sont tout de suite beaucoup plus loin. Merci au Pitres crew, et en particulier à mon petit Bob, qui a su m’épauler à distance et voir de loin la chercheuse mais également la femme que j’étais entrain de devenir, même dans les moments les plus difficiles de cette thèse. J’espère que tu es aussi fière de moi que je le suis de toi. Merci à Mhel, pour avoir su calmer mes crises d’angoisses et fait relativiser, merci pour nos discussions. Merci à Bib également pour cette même dernière raison. Merci au Lyon gang, merci à HW, à Marion, à JD, à Coco, à Théo, à Baty, à Marwin et Balou, à Guillaume, à Flo, à Fredoudou, à Aymeric aussi. Merci de votre soutien à distance, merci d’être là, de m’avoir soutenue de loin et toujours dispos quand je passe sur Lyon. Merci à mes amies de toujours, Laurie et Aude-Marie, pour nos soirées rattrapage de vie biannuelles qui me montrent ce à quoi ressemble la vie d’adulte de l’autre côté de la barrière. Merci aux copines d’Edinburgh, Alix, Lulu et Cassou, pour nos soirées jeux de société et binouze qui ont toujours la même saveur (même après 6 ans). Merci à Axel de m’avoir accueillie dans ta ville, je regrette un peu de ne pas t’avoir vu aussi souvent que je le voulais. Mais j’espère que ta nouvelle vie de papa te comblera de bonheur. Merci à Clarice d’avoir cru en moi jusqu’au bout, cette thèse c’est pour nous deux mon vieux. Merci aussi à Oscar pour ces joutes de jeux de mots endiablées, surtout quand il fallait rire de mes résultats (ou de leur absence finalement …) [SOURCE: Page_7]

Comment ne pas remercier mon Charles ? Merci d’avoir fait irruption dans ma vie lorsque je m’y attendais le moins, mais que j’en avais le plus besoin. Je déteste les gens qui disent des trucs du style « tu sais déjà tout », et j’adore être rébarbative. Merci de toujours savoir quoi faire avec moi, que ce soit me faire relativiser sur ma situtation, ou me motiver pour tout donner, à grand coups de fou rire et de tendresse. Merci pour ces instants de flottement dans le temps, merci de créer cette bulle protectrice autour de nous quand nous en avons besoin. Merci de m’avoir refait croire en tant de belles choses, merci de m’épauler chaque jour que Dieu fait. [SOURCE: Page_7]

Merci évidemment à mes parents et mes frères et sœur, pour leur soutien sans faille, même à distance. Merci plus généralement à ma famille, pour toutes les valeurs de résilience, de patience et de courage qu’elle a su m’inculquer. J’espère vous rendre un peu fiers aujourd’hui. [SOURCE: Page_7]

J’aimerai remercier François, Aude et Mylène, pour cette ferveur scientifique qu’ils ont fait naitre en moi. Vous ne le savez peut-être pas, mais ce manuscrit, il est aussi grâce à vous. [SOURCE: Page_8]

Merci à tous les gens qui, de près ou de loin, ont contribué à faire de ma vie montpelliéraine ce qu’elle est devenue. Merci pour les coups de cœur, merci pour les fous rires, merci pour les larmes aussi. Merci à l’entité tout là-haut, quelle qu’elle soit, Dieu, le Destin, ou Cthulhu pour certains (je tairais les noms mais ça démange), d’avoir guidé mes pas jusque-là. [SOURCE: Page_8]

Je n’oublierai jamais ces trois ans de thèse. A bien des égards, elle aura su me faire vivre pleinement. Rire, pleurer, ressentir de la fierté. M’enseigner que les erreurs font partie du deal, les échecs ne sont que ce qui arrive avant le succès. Chaque problème a une solution, il suffit de l’inventer. [SOURCE: Page_8]

Il ne me reste plus qu’à vous souhaiter bonne lecture, de ce joli pavé dans lequel j’ai distillé des petits bouts de mon cœur et ma passion pour les interactions entre plantes et petites bébêtes microscopiques (cf ma magnifique figure qui a fait sensation à Aussois). Vous y retrouverez aussi ma propension à toujours trop écrire (et quelques larmes çà et là, il faut bien le dire). Ce savoir ne m’appartient plus, il est à vous. Amusez-vous. [SOURCE: Page_8]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_18]

The agronomic transition from conventional chemical fertiliser and pesticide use to sustainable organic farming practices is a pressing concern, especially as global feed demand continues to rise. This transition requires the study of the plant microbiota associated with crop roots, as it holds the key to formulating effective organic inoculation strategies such as bioinoculants. The plant microbiota, comprising a diversity of microorganisms residing in plant organs, plays a crucial role in soil nutrient cycling, root and whole plant development, and mediation of pathogen pressure. Among these beneficial microbes, plant growth promoting rhizobacteria (PGPR) and arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF) are of particular interest, but their effectiveness in the field depends on soil conditions and plant genetics. This work focuses on rice as a model crop, which is of particular economic importance in East and South Asia. Rice production, predominantly in flooded fields, consumes a significant proportion of the world's freshwater resources and contributes to greenhouse gas emissions. The transition to water-efficient irrigation methods is a satisfactory alternative, allowing the use of bioinoculants containing PGPR and AMF, but it will significantly alter the interactions between rice and naturally occurring microbes. Current bioinoculant formulations often lack resilience and adaptation to specific environments, highlighting the need to identify and test naturally occurring microbiota candidates in crop fields. Co-inoculation studies with PGPR and AMF show different effects on plant growth, ranging from synergistic to antagonistic. Understanding the underlying mechanisms of these interactions is crucial to accurately predict plant phenotypes. Plant perception of soil-dwelling microbial diversity involves defined molecular dialogues between the two partners. However, the mechanisms underlying plant discrimination between beneficial and pathogenic microorganisms and the subsequent activation of symbiotic or immune pathways remain unclear. It is important to define the drivers that lead to mutualism or parasitism in a variety of rice-microbe interactions, as they drive the establishment of symbiotic associations. Comprehensive studies of rice interactions with different bacterial and fungal species are essential to unravel the molecular networks involved and gain insights into rice-microbe interactions. This work investigates the phenotypic and molecular responses of rice during interactions with soil microorganisms, both individually and in co-inoculation scenarios. Several PGPR and AMF species, based on recent literature claiming their benefits for rice health were investigated. This work aims to uncover common and specific rice responses to beneficial microorganisms, of bacterial or fungal origin, to establish links between improved growth, biotic stress tolerance and transcriptional regulation of physiological pathways. [SOURCE: Page_18]

General Introduction Achieving the transition from chemical fertiliser and pesticide use to more sustainable organic practices, while maintaining crop yield increases due to ever-increasing feed demand, is of paramount interest (Prasad et al., 2017). Current studies on microbiota associated with crop roots represent a gold mine for organic inoculation formulations. In natural environments and fields, there is a constant interaction and dynamics between plants and a wide variety of microorganisms. The cohort of microbes (pathogens, commensals or beneficials) that thrive and are specifically associated with plant organs and are stable over time is called the plant microbiota (Trivedi et al., 2020). There has been much interest in the root-associated microbiota due to its role in soil nutrient cycling, root anchoring and development, or antagonistic effect on pathogenic environmental strains (Berendsen et al., 2015, Vannier et al., 2019). Within this pool of beneficial microbes, plant-growth promoting rhizobacteria (PGPR) and arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF) are of most interest. Several of these microorganisms have been formulated as bioinoculants, but their beneficial effects in the field depend on soil characteristics and crop genotypes. Large disparities have been observed compared to in vitro or greenhouse experiments, highlighting the need to better understand the molecular basis and compatibility between PGPR, AMF and crops in diverse environments (Schlaeppi & Bulgarelli, 2015, Haskett et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_20]

Furthermore, there are still gaps in our knowledge of how plants discriminate between mutualistic and pathogenic microbes in their microbiota. Plants perceive their environment through microbe-associated molecular patterns (MAMPs) produced by either pathogenic, beneficial or unadapted bacteria (Jones & Dangl, 2006, Henry et al., 2012). How plants discriminate beneficial bacteria or fungi from pathogens or commensals, and trigger pathways related to symbiosis or immunity, is still unclear. In-depth studies of the general or specific responses of rice during interaction with a range and diversity of bacterial and fungal species are needed to characterise the different gene networks involved and to better understand rice- microbe interactions at an integrative level. [SOURCE: Page_20]

In this work, rice is used as a crop model. This crop is of great economic interest to the developing countries of East and South Asia, which produce more than 90% of the world's rice (Ngalimat et al., 2021). As the majority of rice fields are flooded, its cultivation currently requires one third of the world's available freshwater: an average of 2,500 litres of freshwater to produce 1 kg of rice grain (Chialva et al., 2020). Flooding produces high levels of [SOURCE: Page_20]

1 [SOURCE: Page_20]

greenhouse gases such as methane: flooded rice fields contribute up to 20% of anthropogenic methane emissions (Redeker et al., 2000, Richards & Sander, 2015, Prasad et al., 2017). Recent meta-analyses suggest that 92% of paddy fields have the potential to switch to water- saving irrigation methods, such as alternate wetting and drying (AWD) solutions (Richards & Sander, 2015). This change in irrigation mode, which creates more aerobic conditions in the soil and roots of rice, could drastically alter the interactions this cereal has with its microbiota. This switch also allows the use of bioinoculants formulated with both PGPR and AMF (Chialva et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_21]

The beneficial effects of several PGPR genera are well documented in the literature. These rhizobacteria improve the health of their hosts through several mechanisms. Through their enzymatic activity, they have been reported to have biostimulating effects in the soil, increasing its fertility and the percentage of bioavailable nutrients for their plant host. Some nodulating, endophytic or free-living bacteria of the genera Rhizobium, Bradyrhizobium, Azospirillum or Paraburkholderia are able to fix free nitrogen available in the soil (Baldani et al., 2000). Representatives of the genera Bacillus or Pseudomonas solubilise phosphate, a nutrient that is known to form complexes with certain soil substances and is one of the most poorly bioavailable nutrients in the root environment (Lugtenberg & Kamilova, 2009). Some others are able to directly induce their host's growth through the production of phytohormonal substances (Zamioudis et al., 2013, Esmaeel et al., 2018, Ngalimat et al., 2021). PGPR have also been reported to have bioremediation effects by rendering their host more tolerant to abiotic stress or reducing its detrimental effects (Backer et al., 2018, Dias et al., 2019, Sagar et al., 2021). A recent review also reported the different potential biological control agents among the known PGPR for rice infection by different pathogens, mentioning the genera Pseudomonas, Enterobacter, Streptomyces, Bacillus (Ngalimat et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_21]

The same beneficial effects on soil fertility, induction of plant growth and improvement of abiotic and biotic stress tolerance are recorded in the studies that investigated AMF inoculation on a variety of plant hosts (Paszkowski & Boller, 2002, Gutjahr et al., 2009, Begum et al., 2019, Liu et al., 2007, Fiorilli et al., 2018, Campo et al., 2020a). These beneficial fungi were initially thought to be completely absent from flooded rice fields, but fungal propagules naturally present in anaerobic soils are able to develop new functional individuals when dry conditions occur (Lumini et al., 2011, Vallino et al., 2014). Under AWD irrigation conditions, rice could then form AMF symbioses in paddy fields (Chialva et al., 2020). Under controlled and greenhouse conditions, the first reports of rice mycorrhization with three major AMF species, Funneliformis mosseae, Rhizophagus intraradices and R. irregularis, were reported to induce both rice growth and tolerance to abiotic and biotic stresses. However, they depend 2 [SOURCE: Page_21]

on the combination of rice cultivar and AMF species studied (Suzuki et al., 2015, Li et al., 2016, Chen et al., 2019, Campo et al., 2020a). Furthermore, the symbiotic compatibility between rice and AMF in particular, as well as the local and systemic molecular responses of rice to mycorrhization, have been poorly studied. [SOURCE: Page_22]

The main drawback of current bioinoculants is their lack of resilience and adaptation to the environment in which they are used. Studying the naturally occurring microbiota in crop fields, deciphering the best microbial candidates among them, and then testing them in single and co-inoculation is, in theory, an excellent way to increase crop productivity by biological means. Studies interested in co-inoculation between PGPR and AMF currently select their microbial candidates thanks to their in vitro and in planta effects rather than their natural presence in fields (Hart et al., 2018). The literature tends to point out that co-inoculation has contrasting effects on plant growth, from synergistic to antagonistic, depending on the AMF, PGPR and plant model chosen (Pérez-de-Luque et al., 2017, Bernaola et al., 2018). Further work should address how AMF and PGPR interact in planta, in order to understand the random plant phenotypes induced. [SOURCE: Page_22]

In this context, this work presents the phenotypic and molecular responses of rice during interaction with a variety of soil microorganisms, during single or co-inoculation. I focused on interactions with previously reported PGPR and AMF species from different genera. I aimed to unravel common and specific rice responses to beneficial microorganisms, linking beneficial effects on rice growth and tolerance to biotic stress with transcriptional modulation of physiological pathways. [SOURCE: Page_22]

This thesis begins with a bibliographic study in Chapter 1, highlighting the agronomic importance of both rice research and the interactions it establishes with its biotic environment. The benefits that plant microbiota can bring to the health and yield of their hosts are listed. This cohort of beneficial microorganisms is a key focus of agronomic research that seeks to replace chemical pest control with biological inoculation. Within the root-associated microbiota, plant growth promoting bacteria (PGPR) and arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF) are mainly studied for their biostimulatory and biocontrol potential. The various benefits they provide to a plant host, as well as rice-specific interactors, are listed in this chapter. Finally, as the most interesting approach for the development of more resilient bioinoculants is the inoculation of multiple microbial species, the study of their effect on plants, and more specifically on rice, has been addressed. [SOURCE: Page_22]

3 [SOURCE: Page_22]

Figure 1: Schematic representation of the structure of the results obtained during my thesis, organised in my manuscript (created on BioRender.com) [SOURCE: Page_23]

4 [SOURCE: Page_23]

Chapter 2 is focused on the long-term interaction between Nipponbare, a japonica model cultivar and Paraburkholderia kururiensis, a native endophyte having PGPR effects, on crops. The assessment of its effect on phenotypic and molecular parameters of rice health will be compared between two mode of inoculation. [SOURCE: Page_24]

Chapter 3 unravel the common and specific response signature of rice inoculated with a diversity of bacteria from different ecological niches, both on short (seven days, RNAseq, colonisation characterisation) and long (several weeks, months, phenotyping) term interactions. [SOURCE: Page_24]

Chapter 4 discuss on the symbiotic compatibility between six rice cultivars from two subspecies backgrounds with three arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi model species. Rice mycorrhizal colonisation intensities, growth and defence against a bacterial phytopathogen are selected proxies of this symbiotic compatibility. The effect of mycorrhization on systemic molecular responses is also investigated in two rice cultivars of interest, IR64 and Nipponbare. [SOURCE: Page_24]

Chapter 5 deals with the phenotypic and molecular responses of rice during single or co-inoculation with PGPR and AMF species. Common as well as specific responses of rice (both phenotypic and molecular) are investigated with ISR-inducer P. kururiensis and MIR- triggering Rhizophagus irregularis in Nipponbare. [SOURCE: Page_24]

Chapter 1: Bibliographic study Préambule au chapitre 1 : [SOURCE: Page_30]

Ce premier chapitre, rédigé en anglais, est une synthèse bibliographique présentant l'état actuel des connaissances concernant l'étude du microbiote des plantes et les bénéfices agronomiques qu'il peut leur procurer. Tout au long de ce chapitre, j'ai axé mon attention sur le riz, qui m’a servi de modèle végétal. Mon objectif était de recenser les interactions entre le riz et divers microorganismes du sol, ainsi que les réponses induites chez le riz (phénotypiques et moléculaires). [SOURCE: Page_30]

Pour commencer, cette synthèse met en lumière l’importance agronomique, à la fois de la recherche sur le riz et sur les interactions qu’il met en place avec son environnement biotique. J’ai énuméré ensuite les bénéfices que le microbiote des plantes peut apporter à la santé et au rendement de leurs hôtes. Cet ensemble de microorganismes bénéfiques constitue un point central dans la recherche agronomique de réduction de l’utilisation de produits de la lutte chimique par l’application de bioinoculations. Dans ce microbiote, les rhizobactéries améliorant la croissance des plantes (PGPR) et les champignons mycorhiziens à arbuscules (AMF) sont principalement étudiés pour leur potentiel de biostimulation et biocontrôle. Les bénéfices variés qu’ils apportent à un hôte végétal, ainsi que les différents taxons en interaction spécifique avec le riz sont recensés dans ce chapitre. Enfin, la piste la plus intéressante à la mise au point de bioinoculants plus résilients étant l’inoculation d’espèces microbiennes multiples, l’étude de leur effet sur les plantes, et plus particulièrement sur le riz, a été traitée. [SOURCE: Page_30]

11 [SOURCE: Page_30]

Figure 1: Main differences between Flooding and Alternate Wet-Drying irrigation methods (adapted from LaHue et al., 2016) While flooding rice fields has been reported to result in the best yield in lowland cultivars, it has some environmental impacts that are detrimental on the long run (methane (CH$_4$) emissions, inputs runoffs). Alternate wetting and drying alleviates both disadvantages while having no dramatic effects on rice yield. This figure was created with BioRender.com. [SOURCE: Page_31]

12 [SOURCE: Page_31]

How agronomy benefits from microbiome studies to harness new agroecological practices [SOURCE: Page_32]

By 2050, the world's ever-growing population is expected to reach 10 billion people, leading to an increase in global food demand of between 35% and 56% (Poveda & Eugui, 2022). This demand can be met by increasing overall agricultural productivity by 70% (Haskett et al., 2021). At the Green Revolution time, its radical methods (crop intensification and massive use of agrochemicals) fed an exponentially growing population, while making agriculture one of the main causes of global environmental degradation (Schlaeppi & Bulgarelli, 2015; Poveda & Eugui, 2022). The goal of agronomic research is to transform current farming systems and practices to ensure sufficient quantity and quality of produce while minimising their negative environmental impacts (expansion of cropland or polluted soils) and farmer insecurity (Freed et al., 2020, Haskett et al., 2021). To replace the narrow productivity focus that dominates current agronomic systems, it is interesting to implement agroecological principles and practices to build resilience and sustainability of crop fields (Schlaeppi & Bulgarelli, 2015, Freed et al., 2020). Sustainable agriculture needs to address three major issues: nutrient supply to crops, abiotic stress tolerance, and reducing yield losses due to pathogens and pests in the field and in post-harvest management, with abiotic stress, pathogen and pest pressure accounting for 50%, 15% and 25% of annual yield losses, respectively (Poveda & Eugui, 2022). [SOURCE: Page_32]

Oryza sativa: rice, a plant of agronomic interest, with current changes in irrigation mode [SOURCE: Page_32]

Rice (Oryza sativa) is a major crop grown and consumed worldwide, with global production estimated at just over 500 million tonnes (Prasad et al., 2017, Nawaz et al., 2022). Rice, in all its different varieties, is a staple food for more than half of the world's population (Prasad et al., 2017). More than 90% of this cereal is produced in the Asian region, where it then occupies a central position in the export economy (Ngalimat et al., 2021). Today, its production, harvest and consumption are threatened by a variety of factors ranging from abiotic, biotic and environmental issues (Nawaz et al., 2022). In many rice regions, nursery planting, transplanting, weeding and harvesting are still done manually by paid labourers or the farmer's family, with an average of 200 person-days per hectare in South Asia (Prasad et al., 2017). Industrialisation and preference for non-agricultural work by the main labour force increases labour costs and reduces farmers' margins due to reduced paddy yield caused by [SOURCE: Page_32]

13 [SOURCE: Page_32]

Figure 2: Beneficial effects of the plant-associated microbiota (from Trivedi et al., 2020) The plant-associated microbiota (fungi, bacteria, etc.) provide many benefits to their host, from both abiotic and biotic perspectives. These include growth enhancement (blue), abiotic stress control (green) and defence against pathogens and pests (red). Soil and rhizospheric microbiota help release essential nutrients such as inorganic phosphate (Pi), iron (Fe$_2$-), potassium (K+) and others through solubilisation, oxidation or siderophore chelation. These beneficial microorganisms also directly enhance nutrient uptake through interaction and exchange with their host (arbuscules for AMF, nodules for rhizobia). They stimulate plant growth by modulating phytohormones and osmoprotectants, or by acting on ACC deaminase or detoxifying enzymes. Finally, they can protect the plant against pathogens through direct (competition, antibiosis and lytic enzymes) and indirect effects (recognition of microbial structures such as pili, flagella and secretion systems). [SOURCE: Page_33]

14 [SOURCE: Page_33]

fewer and less diligent transplanting workers (Nawaz et al., 2022). A difficult solution to avoid these costs is to revise agricultural practices in specific fields by mechanising some agricultural steps or replacing transplanting with dry direct seeding where possible, a high initial investment resulting in better yield in the long run (Nawaz et al., 2022). [SOURCE: Page_34]

Rice fields irrigation is the most widespread crisis that needs to adress rice production systems. Today, they are generally classified as lowland (flooded) or upland (irrigated), representing 92% and 8% of the world's available rice fields, respectively (Saito et al., 2018). This crop is very water-intensive, requiring an average of 2,500 litres of freshwater to produce 1 kg of rice grain: overall, rice production consumes one-third of the world's available freshwater for irrigation under flood conditions (Chialva et al., 2020). In Asia, 80% of available freshwater resources are used for irrigation, 50% of which is used for rice alone (Nawaz et al., 2022). As a result of global climate change, this freshwater supply is being affected by changes in rainfall patterns and distribution, which could lead to water shortages at low altitudes as early as 2025 (Nawaz et al., 2022). Moreover, the constant flooding of paddy soils triggers the anaerobic decomposition of organic matter, producing high levels of methane and methyl halides greenhouse gases, which contribute between 5% and 20% of global anthropogenic methane emissions (Redeker et al., 2000, Richards & Sander, 2015, Prasad et al., 2017). Conventional cropping methods combine flooding with continuous rice cultivation, which degrades soil health by compacting the soil without introducing new organic matter, reducing the efficiency of N and P fertiliser use, sometimes leading to polluting runoff and leaching, depleting some essential macro- and micronutrients, while reducing microbial diversity and enzymatic activities (Zhuang et al., 2019, Zhang et al., 2020b, Nawaz et al., 2022). All these detrimental effects on soil and environmental factors lead to a decrease in root branching and anchorage, nutrient bioavailability and ultimately rice production yield (Prasad et al., 2017, Nawaz et al., 2022). [SOURCE: Page_34]

A meta-analysis by Zhuang et al. (2019) found that 94% of Asian paddy fields are suitable for water-saving irrigation practices, which rice growers should consider. Shallow-wet irrigation, controlled irrigation, alternative wet-drying and rain-collecting irrigation are typical ways to avoid flooded, uncontrolled conditions, save up to 35% of water use and reduce pollution rate by up to 55% while maintaining yield (Zhuang et al., 2019, Zhang et al., 2020b). This latter statement depends on the rice variety studied, as lowland rice varieties show the lowest yields under controlled irrigation methods (Chialva et al., 2020). Alternate wetting and drying (AWD) irrigation mode is gaining interest as it has been reported to extensively reduce methane emissions, soil and rice methylmercury concentrations while maintaining high yields (Richards and Sander, 2015, Rothenberg et al., 2016, Figure 1). This change in irrigation [SOURCE: Page_34]

15 [SOURCE: Page_34]

mode has been reported to affect soil and root-associated microbiota and the susceptibility of rice varieties to pathogens, shifting from predominantly anaerobic to predominantly aerobic conditions (Chialva et al., 2020). They showed a shift in the microbial community between lowland and upland rice cropping systems: an enrichment of beneficial bacteria and fungal pathogens, as well as beneficial Trichoderma and arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF). Depending on the cultivar, rice yields can be severely affected by a variety of phytopathogens: the development of bacterial blight caused by Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae (Xoo), rice blast caused by Pyricularia oryzae and the rice root knot nematode Meloidogyne graminicola have the potential to decimate an entire field (Zhang & Wang, 2013, Liu & Wang, 2016, Masson et al., 2020). Flooded conditions are known to inhibit the development of some rice root phytopathogens such as M. graminicola or triticoryzae (Chandel et al., 2002, Masson et al., 2022). One solution to this increased vulnerability in upland conditions, due to new agricultural approaches aimed at optimising ecological processes and biotic interactions, is the use of bioinoculants to increase the proportion of rhizospheric beneficial microorganisms against saprotrophic, biotrophic or necrotrophic pathogens (Schlaeppi & Bulgarelli, 2015, Begum et al., 2019, Masson et al., 2022, Wu et al., 2022). The first step of this process is to characterise the residing ensemble of microorganisms living in the vicinity of plants roots and decipher via interaction studies and genome mining what would be in field interesting associations. [SOURCE: Page_35]

Benefits from plant-associated microbiota [SOURCE: Page_35]

The interactions established by plants with their surrounding microbiota, whether beneficial or detrimental, have always forged plant evolution and vice versa (Piromyou et al., 2015, Delaux & Schornack, 2021). The long-term established microbial community specifically associated with the roots of its host is known as the root microbiota (Trivedi et al., 2020, Figure 2). In plant-microbiota interactions studies, this one is preferred since it is more diverse and richer than the leaf or stem microbiota (Edwards et al., 2015). With microorganisms ranging from beneficial, commensal or pathogenic organisms to their vegetal host, from bacteria and fungi to archaea, protists and viruses, root microbiota plays a role in soil fertility (in the cycles of nutrients such as nitrogen, phosphate and carbon) and in anchoring and development of their host root system (Berendsen et al., 2012 & 2015, Michl et al., 2023). It also has a biological control effect on soil and root phytopathogens, either by direct competition, or by inducing local or systemic resistance in their host roots or shoots (Berendsen et al., 2012, Schlaeppi & Bulgarelli, 2015, Vannier et al., 2019). Microbiota diversity and composition depend on soil type and environment (land use, soil physical and chemical properties), plant genotype, age and compartment (Edwards et al., 2015, Wang et al., 2020, Babalola et al., [SOURCE: Page_35]

16 [SOURCE: Page_35]

2021). The plant host modify the composition of the microbiota through its exudates, with phenols, strigolactones as well as sugars, stimulating the recruitment of some microorganisms while restricting the growth of others (Badri et al., 2013, Edwards et al., 2015, Stringlis et al., 2018, Pascale et al., 2020). Bacteria, archaea and fungi that are ubiquitous in the community, associated with a particular host and have specific beneficial functions represent the core microbiota (Busby et al., 2017, Trivedi et al., 2020, Wang et al., 2020). Beneficial bacteria such as Pseudomonas spp in garlic, or rice-associated Bacillus spp, detoxifying-Sphingomonas as well as methanogenic bacteria, are key genera in the establishment of their respective root microbiota (Zhuang et al., 2020; Edwards et al., 2015, Wang et al., 2020). The identification of core microbiota beneficial microorganisms could serve as a starting point for the development of new multispecies bioinoculants (Trivedi et al., 2020). These synthetic communities could help restructure a dysbiosed microbial community, enriching it for beneficial functions and roles, while being resilient in the soil (Banerjee et al., 2018, Mitter et al., 2019). Based on previous knowledge, two types of beneficial microorganisms are of interest for our agronomic objective: plant-growth-promoting rhizobacteria (PGPRs) and arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMFs). [SOURCE: Page_36]

How plants recognise, differentiates and establish beneficial interaction between beneficial and pathogenic microorganisms? [SOURCE: Page_36]

Before delving into the many beneficial effects that some microorganisms can have on plant growth and tolerance to various stresses, it is important to assess how they initially interact and establish a lasting relationship with their host. The fate of the interaction between soil microorganisms and plant roots depends on a finely tuned molecular dialogue between them. Common signalling pathways are being uncovered that allow the recognition of a variety of different interactants with the plant. Besides, the interactions and molecular exchanges between different plant models and the pathogens inhabiting their microbiota have been widely described, enabling a better understanding and treatment of affected crops (see Liu & Wang, 2016 for rice). However, the dialogues that take place during commensal or mutualistic interactions are less well described, although they likely involve the same microbial recognition systems in the plant and modulations of its immune response (Carvalho et al., 2016, Yu et al., 2019a, Teixeira et al., 2021). There are still gaps in our knowledge of which recognition mechanisms allow plants to distinguish beneficial from pathogenic, opportunistic or commensal soil microorganisms and how they choose between symbiosis and immunity signalling pathways upon recognition of an interactant. [SOURCE: Page_36]

17 [SOURCE: Page_36]

Figure 3: Microbial recognition and immune evasion strategies in the molecular dialogue with their plant host (adapted from Chen et al., 2021) Microorganisms, either commensals, beneficials or pathogenic to plants, release microbe-associated molecular patterns (MAMPs) in their vicinity (membrane components, detoxifying molecules and so on). When invading plant cells, they also release some damage-associated molecular patterns (DAMPs) or effectors. Their recognition by plant’s extra or intracellular receptors leads to signal transduction and appropriate immune responses, known as microbe-triggered immunity (MTI). Plants will then release defence molecules with antimicrobial activity. Microbes can evade this MTI by preventing the recognition of DAMPs, MAMPs and effectors (specificity or modifications) or by blocking the transduction of immunity signals and acting on immune responses of their host. Green: plant-related actions. Blue & yellow: all microbe-related actions. Red: plant-adapted (pathogen or beneficial microbes) related actions (MTI reduction). Solid lines: linked with the action of the microbe (production of microbe-related molecules, immune evasion), eventually leading to an MTI. Dashed lines: perception of the microbe by the plant and defence response of the plant, triggered by the MTI. This figure was created with BioRender.com. [SOURCE: Page_37]

18 [SOURCE: Page_37]

It is known that plants, through their own metabolism and development, exude metabolites in the immediate vicinity of their root system to acquire essential nutrients or modulate their rhizospheric microbial communities, recruiting and establishing interactions with beneficial microorganisms while preventing the overgrowth of pathogenic ones (Bakker et al., 2020, Pascale et al., 2020, Stassen et al., 2021, Kim et al., 2022). Plants then excrete secondary metabolites such as organic acids, well-known carbon source for their microbiota, as well as generalist flavonoids or strigolactones, attracting beneficial microorganisms such as arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF) or beneficial bacteria via chemotaxis (Chiu et al., 2022, Stringlis et al., 2018, Stassen et al., 2021). Flavonoids are a family of metabolites derived from the phenylpropanoid pathway that play a role in the recruitment, establishment and functioning of the interaction between diverse plants and beneficial microorganisms, such as quercetin supporting the interaction between Serratia sp. and rice, while flavonols, flavone and anthocyanin are accumulated in Funneliformis mosseae-mycorrhized soybean roots (Balachandar et al., 2006, Lu et al., 2020). They also play a role in the establishment of nodulation between legumes and Rhizobium sp. or between actinorhizal plants and Frankia sp. (Stassen et al., 2021). Strigolactones are carotenoid-derived molecules that act both as plant hormones and as inducers of spore germination and hyphal branching, enhancing AMF root colonisation and establishment (Lanfranco et al., 2018). They have recently been shown to also modulate rice rhizosphere bacterial communities, with the Caballeronia - Burkholderia - Paraburkholderia complex, Granulicella and Dyella bacteria being more recruited in both root and rhizosphere compartments in the presence of the strigolactone orobanchol (Kim et al., 2022). Coumarins shares the same biosynthetic pathway as phenylpropanoid-derived flavonoids, acting as iron-chelating metabolites under iron-deficient soil conditions and as inhibitors of biofilm formation by various pathogenic microorganisms (e.g. for esculetin, ranging from human pathogens to Ralstonia solanacearum biofilms) (Stassen et al., 2021). Their antimicrobial function also makes plants more resistant to a wide range of microbial attacks, while beneficial bacteria such as P. simiae or P. capeferrum were found to be resistant to scopoletin, the most abundantly secreted form of coumarin (Stringlis et al., 2018, Stassen et al., 2021). Benzoxazinoids, triterpenes and camalexin are recently-discovered chemical actors in the interplay between plants and their root-associated microbiota (Pascale et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_38]

Microbe presence is revealed by Pathogen- or, more broadly, Microbe-Associated Molecular Pattern molecules (PAMPs or MAMPs), directly deriving from their microbial components or metabolism. Flagellins, chitins, peptidoglycans and lipopolysaccharides are typical MAMPs (Henry et al., 2012, Zipfel & Oldroyd, 2017). [SOURCE: Page_38]

19 [SOURCE: Page_38]

Figure 4: Effector-triggered immunity mechanisms (from Remick et al., 2023) Some pathogens interfere with Microoganism-Triggered Immunity (MTI) via multiple ways, that can be detected by the vegetal host and leads to Effector-triggered immunity (a) The modification of a plant protein, a target or guardee protein by a pathogen effector is detected by a sensor, or guard. (b) Enzymatic activity of the pathogen leads to destabilization or loss of a target protein, guarded by a guardian sensor. Pathogen-induced loss of this target activates the immune response. (c) The disruption of a host cell physiology by a pathogen can lead to ETI (d) Direct recognition of a pathogen effector by a host sensor (e) A sensor is inhibited by a decoy, which is a mimic of an immunity protein involved in host defense. A pathogen effector that evolved to inhibit the immunity protein also inadvertently disrupts the decoy, unleashing an immune response. Decoy domain(s) can be integrated within the sensor. [SOURCE: Page_39]

20 [SOURCE: Page_39]

On the other hand, when microorganisms colonise root epidermal cells, Damage- Associated Molecular Patterns (DAMPs), such as oligogalacturonides released by microbial enzymes or the extracellular release of plant ATP, are recognised by neighbouring cells (Henry et al., 2012, Thoms et al., 2021). Both MAMPs and DAMPs are recognised by Pattern Recognition Receptors (PRRs) with high affinity receptor-like kinases or proteins (RLK and RLP) (Buendia et al., 2018, Chen et al., 2021). Their recognition triggers local defence response associated with short term responses such as calcium influx, reactive oxygen species (ROS) accumulation and phosphorylation of mitogen-activated protein kinases (Chuberre et al., 2018). Within minutes, transcription factors of phytohormones such as ethylene (ET), salicylate (SA) and jasmonate (JA) are phosphorylated, leading to the activation of a broader host defence response (Nishad et al., 2020, Pascale et al., 2020). On the long term, callose deposition induced by this immunity response leads to cell wall thickening, controlling the spread of the pathogen (Chuberre et al., 2018). This Pathogen- or Microorganism-Triggered Immunity (PTI or MTI) is induced upon recognition of a "non-self" organism to control its infection, prevent further damage and place the plant host in a defence- ready state (Chen et al., 2021, Figure 3). [SOURCE: Page_40]

Some microorganisms interfere with this MTI by producing plant defence inhibitory effectors. This can lead to the establishment of disease, effector-triggered susceptibility (ETS) or effector-triggered immunity (ETI) if plants recognise and act against these effectors (Henry et al., 2012, Chen et al., 2021, Figure 4). The rapid evolution of pathogen acts on their effectors, modifying their conformations, rendering them invisible to common host immunity pathways that act as barriers to pathogen replication (actin cytoskeleton, autophagy, inflammatory signalling pathways or protein synthesis) (Remick et al., 2023). In addition to the general MAMPs-triggered immunity, plants have developed different ETI strategies (listed in Figure 4) to recognise and handle pathogenic effectors (Remick et al., 2023). One strategy is direct effector detection, thanks to resistance proteins (R proteins), the most studied being nucleotide-binding leucine-rich repeat proteins (NLRs) (Chen et al., 2021). They are classified into two major groups based on their non-conserved N-terminal domain: coiled coil (CC-NLR) or toll interleukin-1 receptor (TIR-NLR) (Nishad et al., 2020). There is growing evidence that the complexity of their structure, combined with the high number of different ramification of N- terminal domains, is part of the natural weaponry of plants against pathogens (Kourelis et al., 2021 & 2023). This strategy leading to ETI, transcriptional changes in defence mechanisms and a hypersensitive response associated with programmed cell death are triggered and control the spread of the pathogen in plant cells (Nishad et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_40]

21 [SOURCE: Page_40]

A [SOURCE: Page_41]

B [SOURCE: Page_41]

Figure 5: Schematic representation of rhizobia (A) and mycorrhizal (B) mutualism and plant local immunity modulations (from Nishad et al., 2020) Both sub-figures put in light how plant signals attract the microbes (A: flavonoid; B: strigolactones), how their recognition induces microbe-triggered immunity (MTI) pathways and how microbes are able to evade this MTI (A: effectors and recognition of Nod factors; B: Myc factor-induced abscisic acid production) and triggers a priming of induced systemic response (A: JA-mediated pathway; B: JA-ET- mediated pathway linked with SA repression) [SOURCE: Page_41]

22 [SOURCE: Page_41]

To successfully select beneficial microorganisms from commensal or pathogenic ones in highly diverse rhizosphere communities, plants must use this multi-layered detection system and reprogram their defence strategies accordingly (Yu et al., 2019a, Pascale et al., 2020). MAMPs can be of either beneficial, commensal or pathogenic origin, meaning that all types of microorganisms can trigger MTI (Jones & Dangl, 2006, Stringlis et al., 2018, Teixeira et al., 2021). To avoid a costly constant immune response, plant PRR are induced upon recognition only in cell layers deeper than the epidermis (Pascale et al., 2020). Some PGPR have evolved specific MAMPs that differ from pathogenic ones; rhizobia have for example evolved non- immunogenic flagellins (Zipfel & Oldroyd, 2017, Yu et al., 2019a). Commensals or beneficial microorganisms also have the ability to interfere with their host's immune signalling. In Arabidospis, bacteria such as the commensal Dyella japonica or the beneficial Pseudomonas simiae suppress root growth inhibition and flagellin 22-induced MTI by using the type 2 secretion system (T2SS) and gluconic acid production (Yu et al., 2019a & b, Teixeira et al., 2021). Still in this plant model, Piriformospora indica’s effector (PIIN_08944) suppress the expression of a transcription factor regulating the biosynthesis of SA (isochorismate synthase 1), normally triggered during the flagellin 22-induced SA response (Yu et al., 2019a). The inhibition of host defence responses is essential for the symbiosis between plant and rhizobia or arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi, following the reception of symbiosis signals such as Nod and Myc lipochitooligosaccharides (LCO) (Zipfel & Oldroyd, 2017). Beneficial rhizobacteria such as Paraburkholderia phytofirmans, P. kururiensis or Herbaspirillum seropedicae suppress MTI by secreting 1-aminocyclopropane-1-carboxylate (ACC) deaminase enzyme, which inhibits ethylene production and MTI-related defence responses (Pedrosa et al., 2011, Dias et al., 2019, Nishad et al., 2020, Figure 5A). Specific AMF effectors (not well described yet), are able to induce abscisic acid biosynthesis and suppress SA priming, simultaneously reducing MTI and ensuring proper root mycorrhization (Nishad et al., 2020, Figure 5B). [SOURCE: Page_42]

Another hypothesis for the distinction between symbiosis and immunity events lies in the combination of signals: a nematode root attack induces the production of numerous intracellular DAMPs and specific MAMPs, whereas a fungal or bacterial infection does not present the same types or quantity of molecular profiles (Thoms et al., 2021). Zhou et al., 2020 explained that both DAMPs and PAMPs should be recognised at the same time to elicit an immune local response. Receptor competition between OsCEBiP and OsMYR$_1$ for the binding of OsCERK$_1$ is at the origin of the choice between symbiosis or immunity in rice (Zhang et al., 2021). Moreover, since there is a high degree of overlap in the pathways regulated by pathogens, commensals or beneficials (ROS, ET, JA and SA, pathogenesis-related (PR) proteins), the combination of several signalling pathways associated with either symbiosis or immunity responses must be necessary (Plett & Martin, 2018). [SOURCE: Page_42]

23 [SOURCE: Page_42]

Figure 6: Rhizobacterial mutualism with rice and its benefits for growth and stress response [SOURCE: Page_43]

Beneficial rhizobacteria improve rice shoot and root growth through their effects on nutrient bioavailability and uptake (nitrogen (N 2) fixation, inorganic phosphate (Pi) solubilisation or siderophore utilisation) or by modulating phytohormones (decreasing ACC (1-aminocyclopropane-1-carboxylate, precursor of ethylene)) or by releasing similar molecules near the roots (IAA = auxin; ethylene; cytokinins; etc.). They also help their host to tolerate abiotic stresses by accumulating osmoprotectants (proline, betaine, etc.), alleviating damage induced by heavy metals (HM) or blocking their uptake (Cd: cadmium). They also help maintain the uptake of water and nutrients in the roots under drought stress. Finally, they also help their host against local and systemic pathogens by competing for nutrients or colonisation sites, by MTI induction, or by induced systemic resistance (ISR), a priming state induced by phytohormone modulation. This figure was created on Biorender.com [SOURCE: Page_43]

24 [SOURCE: Page_43]

If the beneficial microorganism in question is able to evade or suppress the host defence response, mutualistic interactions can take place. AMF will eventually colonise plant roots and form a symbiosis called mycorrhization, the beneficial effects of which will be discussed in detail below. Beneficial rhizobacteria have two alternative lifestyles, one as free- living microorganisms inhabiting soils and the rhizosphere and the other as plant endophytes, inducing the differentiation of a root organ (nodulation) or not (efficient intercellular colonisation of root cells) (Tonelli et al., 2020). The mechanisms used to induce plant growth, improve its health and defence against phytopathogens, and the potential to design bioinoculants based on PGPR will be discussed in the next part. [SOURCE: Page_44]

Plant-growth-promoting rhizobacteria: mechanisms improving rice health [SOURCE: Page_44]

Plant-growth-promoting rhizobacteria (PGPR) are bacteria from the soil that surround the root system of a plant (surface or endophytic) and whose metabolic activities improve the development or the tolerance of their host to certain stresses (Lugtenberg & Kamilova, 2009). The study of PGPR-plant interactions has revealed many specific mechanisms that are beneficial for plant growth, nutrition, defence and yield (Figure 6). [SOURCE: Page_44]

These beneficial rhizobacteria play a role in soil fertility and plant nutrient acquisition. Through their metabolic activities, they increase the bioavailability of certain nutrients essential for plant development. Studies have focused on several nitrogen-fixing bacteria, such as Rhizobium and Bradyrhizobium, Herbaspirillum, Azospirillum or Burkholderia sensu lato (s.l.), either nodulating or simply endophytic (Baldani et al., 2000). Bradyrhizobium ORS2011 and ORS278 inoculated on ancestral rice roots (O. brevigulata) increased its shoot growth and yield by 20% compared to the control (Chaintreuil et al., 2000). Inoculation with Azospirillum brasilense improves the growth of maize, rice and wheat while increasing their nitrogen use efficiency (NUE), accompanied in rice by an increase in photosynthetic efficiency and mitigation of drought stress (Zeffa et al., 2019, Thomas et al., 2019, Vannini et al., 2021). Burkholderia vietnamiensis, a PGPR selected for its high in vitro nitrogen fixation capacity, also increases rice shoot and root biomass (up to 40% and 72% respectively) and yield parameters under field conditions in a variety of cultivars (up to 20%) (Trân Van et al., 2000, Govindarajan et al., 2008). Paraburkholderia kururiensis has also been reported to have this atmospheric nitrogen fixation capacity and to induce rice growth and total nitrogen content to a greater extent than B. vietnamiensis (Baldani et al., 2000, Estrada-de Los Santos et al., 2001, Mattos et al., 2008). P. tropica, a sugarcane stem endophyte, also increases shoot and root biomass of sorghum and tomato and wheat yields, probably through its nitrogen fixation [SOURCE: Page_44]

25 [SOURCE: Page_44]

Figure 7: Schematic representation of differences in molecular signalling and mechanisms involved in pathogen-induced systemic acquired resistance (SAR), herbivore-induced resistance (HIR), and induced systemic resistance (ISR) triggered by beneficial soilborne microbes (from Pieterse et al., 2014) Solid lines: established interactions; dashed lines: hypothetical. Coloured arrows: systemic translocation of long-distance molecular or electric signals (indicated in the same colour at the base of the arrows). Abbreviations: ABA, abscisic acid; Ac, acetylation; DAMP, damage-associated molecular pattern; ET, ethylene; ETI, effector-triggered immunity; Fe, iron; HAMP; herbivore-associated molecular pattern; JA, jasmonic acid; MAMP, microbe-associated molecular pattern; Me, methylation; NB-LRR, nucleotide-binding–leucine-rich repeat; PCD, programmed cell death; PRR, pattern-recognition receptor; PTI, PAMP-triggered immunity; SA, salicylic acid; TF, transcription factor. [SOURCE: Page_45]

26 [SOURCE: Page_45]

capacity coupled with a phosphate solubilising capacity (Bernabeu et al., 2018, Silva et al., 2018, Kuramae et al., 2020). Phosphorus is another key nutrient for plant development and physiology. Unfortunately, due to binding and complexation reactions of this element, it is estimated that only 0.1% of the phosphorus naturally present in soil is bioassimilable and only 30% of phosphate inputs are directly available for crops (Sharma et al., 2013). The organic fraction of inaccessible soil phosphate is metabolised by specialised enzymes such as acid phosphatases, phytases or phosphonotases and C-P lyases, while the inorganic fraction is solubilised by organic or inorganic acid production that lowers pH, chelation and competition for P-binding sites, and direct assimilation from liquid or through the use of siderophores (Sharma et al., 2013, Tian et al., 2021). In addition to P. tropica, bacteria of the genera Pseudomonas, Bacillus, Serratia, Azotobacter, Azospirillum (brasilense) and Burkholderia (cepacia) solubilise phosphate (Lugtenberg & Kamilova, 2009, Sharma et al., 2013, Peng et al., 2020, Wahid et al., 2020, Tian et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_46]

PGPR have also been reported to induce root and shoot growth directly by producing metabolites in the rhizosphere, ressembling phytohormones, or by degrading them. The root growth promoting hormone auxin, derived from tryptophan, is the most widely studied phytohormones in terms of PGP effects and can be produced by members of the genera Klebsiella, Azospirillum, Herbaspirillum, Pseudomonas, Burkholderia s.l. (El-Khawas & Adachi, 1999, Zamioudis et al., 2013, Drogue et al., 2014, Pankievics et al., 2016, Backer et al., 2018). Its effect on root growth depends on the level of tryptophan produced by the plant: the induction of root weight induced by the auxin-producing Pseudomonas fluorescens WCS365 was only visible on the high tryptophan producing radish compared to the nine times lower producing crops (cucumber, pepper, tomato) (Lugtenberg & Kamilova, 2009). Mattos et al, 2008 showed that the endophytic Paraburkholderia kururiensis is capable of indole-3-acetic acid (IAA) production and secretion inside rice root tissues, whereas IAA-producing P. phytofirmans also uses it as a carbon, nitrogen and energy source, highlighting the potential role of PGPR in auxin coordination and balance in the rhizosphere of their host (Donoso et al., 2017, Esmaeel et al., 2018). Bacterial production of cytokinins and gibberellins affect root system architecture through their interaction with auxin levels. Bacterial cytokinin supply can also influence host exudation (Lugtenberg & Kamilova, 2009, Vacheron et al., 2013, Backer et al., 2018). Azospirillum brasilense has been reported to produce ethylene from the precursor methionine, promoting root hair development in tomato plants (Vacheron et al., 2013). The genome of Herbaspirillum frisingense and representatives of the genera Azospirillum (lipoferum, sp. B510), Enterobacter (spp. P23) and Paraburkholderia (phytofirmans, tropica) contain sequences encoding ACC deamination, enzyme responsible for the degradation of ethylene precursor, phytohormone involved in the management of [SOURCE: Page_46]

27 [SOURCE: Page_46]

Figure 8: Working model of induced systemic resistance (ISR) in Arabidopsis (from Yu et al., 2022) The crosstalk between plants and beneficial microorganisms influences each other growth and defence responses. Root exudates produced recruit them via chemotaxis, while beneficial microbes metabolites impacts their host growth and limit pathogen development. Upon recognition of beneficial microbes, several signalling pathways are simultaneously modulated as well. The salicylic acid (SA) pathway is rapidly induced, leading to a local basal defence response. Some mitogen-activated phosphorylation kinases (MAPK), specifically induced by PGPR recognition, repress this SA-mediated defence response and induces JA/ET signalling pathways. Simultanouesly, these are enhanced by the PGPR-triggered upregulation of MYB72 (linked with WRKY$_8$, 33, MYC$_2$ and ERF$_1$) and LOX$_2$ (which inhibition by WRKY11 was repressed by the PGPR signals). Both signalling pathways triggers the activation of NPR$_1$ and PR proteins (PR$_1$ to PR$_5$ as well as PDF$_1$.2), leading to the Induced Systemic Resistance (ISR). This is reinforced by the activiation of abscisic acid (ABA) pathway upon PGPR recognition, leading to the induction of stomatal regulation and ISR. This immunity response is typically restricted by microRNA (miR), which are being repressed upon PGPR signalling. When activated, ISR leads to calcium influx in leaves cells, ABA-induced stomatal regulation, reactive oxygen species scavenging and callose deposition mechanisms. [SOURCE: Page_47]

28 [SOURCE: Page_47]

stress (Sessitsch et al, 2005, Prigent-Combaret et al. 2008, Dias et al. 2018, Kuramae et al. 2020, Ngalimat et al., 2021). With this balance of ethylene levels, PGPR are able to maintain sufficiently high levels to promote abiotic stress tolerance, while reducing energy expenditure and maintaining root growth (Backer et al., 2018, Ngalimat et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_48]

In terms of abiotic stress tolerance, some PGPR alleviates the induced damages or increase their host plant tolerance to a variety of them. Under salt, drought and even cold stress conditions, the accumulation of osmolytes in plant cells helps the host to tolerate or get acclimated to the stress. Proline, glycine betaine and aldehydes accumulation has been reported   after   inoculation   of    Pseudomonas      pseudoalcaligenes,      Bacillus     subtilis, Paraburkholderia phytofirmans on Bacopa, rice, Arabidopsis and grapevine (Vacheron et al., 2013). Through mechanisms of membrane stabilisation, ROS scavenging or by hormonal balance modulation, certain PGPR species such as Pseudomonas putida on chickpea, Bacillus thuringensis on soybean, Pseudomonas fluorescens and Enterobacter sp P23 on rice can increase the tolerance of their plant host to heat, drought or flood, as well as salt stress (Esmaeel et al., 2018, Backer et al., 2018, Ngalimat et al., 2021, Sagar et al., 2021). Certain strains of Burkholderia, Paraburkholderia or Pseudomonas can be considered as potential bioremediators by regulating the assimilation of heavy metals such as cadmium or toxic aromatic compounds (phenols or benzene derivatives), thereby reducing the detrimental effects of pollution in a cultivated/agronomic soil (Dias et al., 2018, Wei et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_48]

The plant growth-promoting effect induced by rhizobacteria also includes their assistance in improving the host's tolerance to biotic stresses, at two complementary levels. PGPR reduce the harmful effects of pathogens in the root environment via antagonism or competition with rhizospheric phytopathogens (Lugtenberg & Kamilova, 2009, Backer et al., 2018). PGPR from Streptomyces, Bacillus, Pseudomonas and Burkholderia s.l. strains produce various specific antimicrobial compounds in the environment, such as hydrogen cyanide, putrescine, 4-hydroxybenzoate (Lugtenberg & Kamilova, 2009, Dias et al., 2018, Ngalimat et al., 2021). Other excreted compounds such as rhamnolipids and lipopeptides from PGPR strains have been recently investigated for their antimicrobial activities (Crouzet et al., 2020). Pseudomonas and Burkholderia rhamnolipids have been found to have antifungal properties (zoospore lysis, spore germination abortion and mycelial growth inhibition), thanks to their plasma membrane disorganisation potential. Paired with lipopeptides (also produced by a larger variety of PGPR and beneficial fungi, known to have antimicrobial properties), they are currently studied for their biosurfactant activities and potential for biocontrol (Choudhary et al., 2007, Crouzet et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_48]

29 [SOURCE: Page_48]

Direct antimicrobial effects of PGPR strains were reported against all types of phytopathogens, e.g. P. phytofirmans PsJN inhibited Botrytis cinerea spore germination and mycelial growth on grapevine, Peanibacillus lentimorbus reduced cucumber mosaic virus RNA accumulation in tobacco leaves, Bacillus sp. D13, B. velezensis or Pseudomonas fluorescens altering colony diameter, surface morphology and cell motility of Xoo (Backer et al., 2018, Esmaeel et al., 2018, Xie et al., 2018a, Ngalimat et al., 2021). Competition between PGPR and root phytopathogens may be related to colonisation (or infection) sites, niches and/or competition for nutrients (Lugtenberg & Kamilova, 2009). For the latter, the production of siderophores by Streptomyces, Pseudomonas aeruginosa and especially P. WCS strains (pyoverdine and pseudomonine) inhibits pathogen (e.g. Xoo) biomass by competing for iron uptake and inducing plant defences (Berendsen et al., 2015, Ngalimat et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_49]

These mechanisms are effective against the risk of infection at the same site, but do not prevent colonisation at a more distant site, such as another plant organ. However, the recognition of MAMPs derived from the presence, metabolism and activity of PGPR (flagellins, hormones, siderophores, antimicrobial compounds) triggers a local immune response of the host, reducing the ability of phytopathogens to infect (Backer et al., 2018, Basu et al., 2021, Ngalimat et al., 2021). In addition to this local defence response, these MAMPs of many beneficial rhizobacteria have been reported to increase the defence capacity of all plant parts and especially aboveground parts via induced systemic resistance (ISR) (Berendsen et al., 2012, Pieterse et al., 2014, De Kesel et al., 2021). This beneficial soil-borne microbe-induced defence priming is characterised by a faster and stronger activation of defence mechanisms expressed during a second infection by a wide variety of pathogens or herbivorous insects, and should be distinguished from resistance induced by insect or pathogen primary infection (Pieterse et al., 2014, Figure 7). While this type of systemic acquired resistance (SAR) transduction pathways are well known and are based on salicylic acid increasing levels that coordinate the activation of multiple pathogenesis-related genes, ISR transduction, pathways and specific effects are less clear (Yu et al., 2022, Figure 8). It has been reported that ISR requires functional ET and JA pathways, involving some marker genes such as the plant defensin 1.2 (PDF$_1$.2) and the JA-responsive VSP gene in Arabidopsis, but can be associated with SA accumulation and PAL activity in some specific cases (de Vleesschauwer et al., 2008, Tonelli et al., 2020, Yu et al., 2022). As SAR and its SA-related defence induction are generally thought to induce plant resistance to biotrophic and hemibiotrophic pathogens, whereas JA and ET signalling function for necrotrophic pathogens, beneficial bacteria could then induce one or the other depending on the type of phytopathogenic attack (Pieterse et al., 2014, Yu et al., 2022). This relies on the same mechanisms of MTI (increased callose and ROS accumulation in leaves, modulation of MAPK kinases, transcription factors and [SOURCE: Page_49]

30 [SOURCE: Page_49]

phytohormones) but in a faster and stronger manner, leading to a quicker and better control of pathogen infection (Pieterse et al., 2014, De Kesel et al., 2021, Yu et al., 2022). [SOURCE: Page_50]

The plant mobile signal translocated from beneficially colonised roots to shoots that is responsible for the rapid and strong ISR induction of defence responses remains unknown. Recently, the root-specific transcription factor MYB72 was reported to be an early ISR signalling factor responsible for the expression of the beta-glucosidase BGLU42; the expression of both genes is required for ISR in Arabidopsis in interaction with Pseudomonas simiae WCS417 (Stringlis et al., 2018). BGLU42 converts scopolin to scopoletin, which leads to its mobility in the soil and perhaps in the vascular system to the shoots in need (Stringlis et al., 2018, Stassen et al., 2021). Small RNAs, as well as microRNA (sRNA and miRNA) are also potential candidates, since multiple miRNA have been recently shown to have important roles in PTI and ETI triggering (repressing auxin signalling for the first, targeting conserved domains of NLR encoding genes for the latter) (Yu et al., 2022). Furthermore, two Bacillus strains have been reported to supress miRNA’s repression of ISR in Arabidopsis (B. cereus acting on miR472 when the plant is infected by Pseudomonas syringae pv. tomato DC3000, B. amyloliquefaciens FZB42 on miR846) (Yu et al., 2022). Much work remains to be done on ISR-specific signals induced by beneficial rhizobacteria, to better understand the pathways that induce plant defence without suppressing growth. [SOURCE: Page_50]

Thanks to all the described effects on plant biotic stress tolerance, several genera such as   Bacillus,   Pseudomonas      (putida   or   defensor   WCS374r)      and   Paraburkholderia (phytofirmans) are candidates for biocontrol of pathogens such as Pseudomonas syringae, Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae, Pyricularia oryzae and Botrytis cinerea on Arabidopsis, grapevine and especially rice (de Vleesschauwer et al., 2008, Timmermann et al., 2017, Esmaeel et al., 2018, Ashajyothi et al., 2020, Ngalimat et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_50]

Rice-associated PGPR focus [SOURCE: Page_50]

There were a lot of studies on the isolation, characterisation and application of PGPR species on the root system of rice (see Table 1). Early studies highlight the presence of Burkholderia, Bradyrhizobium, Enterobacter, Azospirillum and Herbaspirillum genera naturally occurring in rice fields, all improving its growth (Baldani et al., 1986, Chaintreuil et al., 2000, Trân Van et al., 2000, Elbeltagy et al., 2001, Ngalimat et al., 2021). Multiple strains of rice fields-isolated Bradyrhizobium show rice growth induction under controlled conditions with multiple rice cultivars (Piromyou et al., 2015, Cai et al., 2020). Azospirillum sp. B510 was reported to enhance tiller numbers, numbers of panicles and shoot length when inoculated [SOURCE: Page_50]

under greenhouse conditions or in paddy fields (Isawa et al., 2010, Bao et al., 2013). The same case was seen for A. lipoferum, as well as other PGPR such as Gluconacetobacter diazotrophicus, two strains of Burkholderia vietnamiensis and Herbaspirillum seropedicae in paddy fields for four commercial rice cultivars in India (Govindarajan et al., 2008). The increase of two rice cultivars growth and yield induced by Pseudomonas putida and P. fluorescens inoculation under field conditions is even greater in hyper saline soils, thanks to the alleviation of the effects of salinity (decrease of hydrogen peroxide, increase of catalase activity and proline content) (Norouzinia et al., 2020). Similar simultaneous growth boost and salt- alleviating effects via antioxidative defence systems were recorded with rice rhizosphere- isolated Burkholderia sp. AQ12 under growth chamber conditions (Maqsood et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_52]

A recent study on the potential PGPR identified from rhizospheric soil of paddy fields in Himalaya showed the presence of genera such as Bacillus, Pseudomonas, Streptomyces, Exiguobacterium, Aeromonas, Chryseobacterium, Enterobacter and Stenotrophomonas (Jasrotia et al., 2021). The isolation from different rice fields and reapplication under controlled conditions of potentially beneficial rhizobacteria, such as phosphate solubilising Bacillus oryzicola or B. sp. MR42 as well as Pseudomonas spp. MR11 and MR34 showed better seed germination, shoot and root biomass as well as yield increase and reduction of pathogens (Xoo and Burlholderia glumae) symptoms (Chung et al., 2015, Rasul et al., 2019). There are several reports of beneficial rhizobacteria inducing ISR in rice leaves against phytopathogens, but it depends on both microorganism’s species. In hydroponic cultures, Lysobacter gummosus OH17 association with Nipponbare induces the overexpression of several JA and ET related genes (OsLOX, OsACC, OsACO), triggering the up-regulation of PR proteins (OsPR1a, OsPR1b, OsPR$_5$, OsPR10) and rice resistance to a broad range of phytopathogens (P. oryzae, Rhizoctonia solani and Xoo) (Laborda et al., 2020). A wide diversity of Bacillus is reported to induce ISR in rice infected with fungal pathogens (Rhizoctonia solani, Alternaria alternata, Bipolaris sorokiana) or Xoo, while maintaining or increasing their growth (Chithrashree et al., 2011, Sahu et al., 2020). The rice-growth-promoting effect of Azospirillum sp. B510 comprises disease resistance against Pyricularia oryzae and Xoo under greenhouse conditions, via ET-related pathway (Yasuda et al., 2009, Kusajima et al., 2018). Both Pseudomonas defensor WCS374r (formerly known as P. fluorescens) and P. putida induces ISR in rice leaves infected by P. oryzae, with mechanisms based on a pseudobactin type of siderophores priming of cellular defence response and the in vitro evidenced mycelial growth inhibition respectively (de Vleesschauwer et al., 2008, Ashajyothi et al., 2020). On another hand, Serratia plymuthica induces ISR in rice leaves infected with the hemi-biotrophic P. oryzae, but increased the susceptibility to necrotrophic pathogens (Rhizoctonia solani and Cochliobolus miyabeanus) (de Vleeschauwer et al., 2009). [SOURCE: Page_52]
