Knowledge on rice response to beneficial bacteria [SOURCE: Page_54]

The study of molecular responses of plants to diverse phytopathogens enabled the assessment of the mechanisms of pathogenicity used to induce disease as well as the natural mechanisms of plant resistance, at play or to be elaborated in breeding or bioinoculants strategies. Ryu et al., 2006 states that the WRKY transcription factors family is involved in the transcriptional activation of defence-related genes upon Magnaporthe grisea and Xoo infection of rice. Resistant rice cultivars to P. oryzae exhibited elevated levels of expression of secondary metabolism and PR proteins, as well as an hypersensitive response phenotype due to interplay with ethylene (ET), jasmonic (JA) and salicylic (SA) acids (Wang et al., 2014, Li et al., 2016). Rice susceptible or resistant to the Burkholderia glumae infection describes different pattern of expression of genes related with receptor activity, defence response, programmed cell death and lipid metabolic process, with NLR being more expressed in resistant than susceptible interactions (Magbanua et al., 2014). Plants have signature responses to biotrophic, hemi-biotrophic or necrotrophic pathogens as well as mutualistic microorganisms, but does share some pathways between the four of them: PR proteins and NLR, ROS production or scavenging, as well as phytohormonal balances (ET, JA, SA and gibberelines) are modulated. It is the combination and integration of signals that determines the outcome of the interaction (Plett & Martin, 2017, Thoms et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_54]

A recent mini-review from Mukherjee et al., 2022, browse the pathways modulated by beneficial bacteria inoculation. They state that rice common response to beneficial rhizobacteria relates to the flavonoid synthesis and protein kinases being differentially expressed, auxin and ethylene as well as defence related genes being repressed while nutrient transporters related genes are being up-regulated. In more details, there are a few studies recording rice molecular responses to beneficial rhizobacteria, only considering single association. Rice seedlings in interaction with Sinorhizobium meliloti showed induction of the expression of RLK and NLR proteins (perception), auxin, brassinosteroids, cytokinines and gibberellins homeostasis, photosynthesis and carbohydrate metabolism, with a particular effect on root cell development: cell division rate being increased at early time points, switching to cell wall expansion and complexification afterwards (Wu et al., 2018). The interaction between rice roots and Herbaspirillum seropedicae triggers the local down-regulation of auxin signalling pathway and defence related genes (thionins, PBZ$_1$), the modulation of ethylene responsive factors, as well as the up-regulation of a metallothionein, responsible for abiotic and biotic stress tolerance (Brusamarello-Santos et al., 2012). A recent RNAseq study on the same interaction states that in rice roots, H. seropedicae triggers the down-regulation of PR [SOURCE: Page_54]

35 [SOURCE: Page_54]

proteins, auxin, salicylic acid, ethylene signalling pathways, flavonoid synthesis and isoprenoid, as well as wall-associated kinases, while inducing peroxidases to evade plant immune response and alleviate its defence response due to the bacterial colonisation. The repression of local defence response is paired with a modulation of metal ion metabolism: phytosiderophores (nicotianamine biosynthesis and enzymatic pathways) as well as iron transporters are induced (Brusamarello-Santos et al., 2019). This suggests that Herbaspirillum seropedicae inhibits defence responses of rice roots upon colonisation and helps its growth by increasing its iron and other essential nutrients uptake. Azospirillum colonisation effects on rice molecular pathways depends on the strain investigated, its behaviour and degree of adaptation (Drogue et al., 2014). A. sp. B510 induces the highest effects on secondary metabolism, especially on hydroxycinnamic acid derivatives and flavonoids in rice shoots, as well as repressing a wider set of genes than A. brasilense, suggesting a priming effect (Chamam et al., 2013, Drogue et al., 2014). There is a common rice response in roots to A. lipoferum 4B or A. sp B510, comprising the up-regulation of a PR10, cysteine-rich containing domains proteins, gibberellin and phytoalexin synthesis (Drogue et al., 2014). With specifically A. brasilense, auxin, flavonoid (chalcone synthases), defence-related genes (chitinases, PR, thionins) are down-regulated, while nitrate, ammonium, sugar transporters are up-regulated, with a modulation of ethylene pathway, transcription factors, as well as two marker genes of plant-beneficial endophytic bacteria interaction (SHR$_5$ and AGC kinases), suggesting a beneficial reprogramming of rice physiological state (Thomas et al., 2019). Wiggins et al., 2022 highlighted that H. seropedicae and A. brasilense share the same pattern of inhibition of auxin, ethylene biosynthesis and defence related genes, while increasing the expression of flavonoid biosynthesis, nitrate and sugar transporters. [SOURCE: Page_55]

Recent studies in our lab focused on rice transcriptomic responses to three endophytic beneficial Burkholderia s.l.: B. vietnamiensis, P. kururiensis and P. phytofirmans (King et al., 2019 & 2023). They state that there is a weak common signature response of rice roots but a broader common leaf response to endophytic Burkholderia s.l. colonisation. It comprises the up-regulation of genes related to development, transcription factors and antimicrobial compounds, paired with the repression of resistance genes and nitrogen metabolism (King et al., 2023). Both rice- native beneficials (B. vietnamiensis and P. kururiensis) share a pattern of elicitation of photosynthesis and development with a modulation of phytohormones and defence responses, but in a way that is specific to each bacterium: there is a temporal delay in induction of JA-related genes in leaves following the interaction with P. kururiensis compared to B. vietnamensis (King et al., 2019). These results, linked with the precedent ones with H. seropedicae and Azospirillum sp. suggests that there is a common response of rice to a variety of beneficial bacteria, with a down-regulation of pathogenic infection-related genes, [SOURCE: Page_55]

36 [SOURCE: Page_55]

a modulation of recognition receptors and early defence response as well as phytohormones and an up-regulation of development-related genes expression. This common set of genes is however generally weak compared with the whole dataset. When rice roots are inoculated with two strains of Azospirillum, only 17% of the total differentially expressed genes (DEGs) in rice roots were shared between the two conditions (Drogue et al., 2014). This pool of shared DEGs represents 25% of total DEGs for the comparison of three Burkholderia s. l. (Drogue et al., 2014, King et al., 2019). This is what Valette et al., 2020 found also, studying the transcriptomic and metabolomic response of rice to a diversity of Azospirillum and other PGPR originating from rice, wheat, maize or other crops: of 15 genes selected for their specific modulation by PGPR strains, only four were commonly regulated and could serve as marker of rice-PGPR interaction. Rice response to beneficial bacteria seems then more specific, depending on the PGPR strain, behaviour on roots, metabolism and benefits for the plant. This should be investigated on a deeper level to assess precisely the pathways modulated by a beneficial interaction between rice and any PGPR. [SOURCE: Page_56]

Arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi: commonalities and rice health [SOURCE: Page_56]

With the first evidence dating back to 450 million years ago, one of the oldest symbioses in the world is the mutualistic relationship between plants and arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF) (Smith et al. 2011). It is established between fungi of the Glomeromycota clade and more than 80% of vascular plants, ranging from trees to flowering plants, including agronomically important crops such as wheat, maize and rice (Schussler et al., 2001, Gutjahr et al., 2008, Jung et al., 2012). This symbiosis between crops and AMF species, their effect on local microbial communities and benefits have been reported in recent literature, but seems to be dependent on the symbiotic compatibility between crop cultivar and AMF species (Fiorilli et al., 2018, Campos et al., 2018, Zhang et al., 2018, Davidson et al., 2019, Emmett et al., 2021). They will be discussed in the following section of the chapter. [SOURCE: Page_56]

AMF symbiosis establishment and functioning: always beneficial? This interaction is mediated by a molecular dialogue between the two organisms (Figure 9). Under conditions of nutrient and especially phosphate starvation, plants secrete strigolactones from their roots into the rhizosphere, which induce spore germination and hyphal branching of the surrounding AMF (Ho-Plagaro & Garcia-Garrido, 2022). In turn, Myc factors secreted by the fungi are recognised by LysM domain-containing receptor-like kinases (LysM RLKs) and trigger common symbiosis signalling pathway (CSSP) genes (SYMRK, CASTOR and POLLUX) and calcium spiking (Nakagawa & Imaizumi-Anraku, 2015, Mbodj et al., 2018, Miyata et al., 2023). These Myc factors have recently been described as mycorrhizal [SOURCE: Page_56]

37 [SOURCE: Page_56]

Figure 9: Life cycle of arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (from Choi et al., 2018) Under optimal abiotic conditions, fungal spores germinate. (a) Both microorganisms detect diffusible signals in the exudates released by the potential partner. The perception of fungal signals (oligosaccharides and mycorrhizal lipooligosaccharides (Myc-LCOs)) triggers a nuclear Ca$_2$+ spike in the host rhizodermis, which activates the common symbiosis pathway (CSP) and regulates gene expression required for rhizodermal penetration (steps 1, 2, 3). (b) To adhere to the surface of the root cell, the AMF form attachment structures called hyphopodia, while the host cell produces a prepenetration apparatus (PPA) to accommodate the fungus intracellularly (45). (c) Fungal hyphae grow within the intercellular spaces of root cells and towards the inner cortex to initiate arbuscule formation (46). (d) Arbuscule development is divided into five stages, from development to degradation (stages i- v). (e) Successful arbuscule formation within the host cell allows direct exchange between the two, with host photosynthates being exchanged for mineral nutrients provided by the fungus, leading to the formation of nutrient storage vesicles and daughter spores. Abbreviation: KAR, karrikin. [SOURCE: Page_57]

38 [SOURCE: Page_57]

lipochitooligosaccharides (Myc-LCOs) and short-chain chitooligosaccharides (COs), which share the same structure but differ in their complexity (Choi et al., 2018). AMF symbiosis also depends on receptors such as CERK$_1$ and D14L, independently of the CSSP, to induce calcium spiking, large lateral root growth and AMF attachment (Chiu et al., 2017, Ho-Plagaro & Garcia-Garrido, 2022). To adhere the root surface, the fungal hyphae then transform into an adhesive structure called hyphopodia, which allows the fungi to access the internal root cortex. They spread through intercellular spaces and colonise cortical cells with highly branched intracellular structures called arbuscules, which are preferential sites of exchange with their host (Gutjahr et al., 2008 & 2009, Jung et al., 2012, Liu et al., 2021). The presence, quantity and functioning of these specific structures serve as markers of symbiotic compatibility between plants and AMF (Montero et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_58]

AMF species were initially thought to have low host specificity due to their ability to colonise and induce the growth of a wide range of plant species (Van Geel et al., 2016). Recent reports on crop mycorrhization have highlighted differences in symbiotic compatibility between AMF and crop species, as different one-to-one associations and combinations result in different plant colonisation intensities and phenotypic responses (Pérez-de-Luque et al., 2017, Suzuki et al., 2015, Campos et al., 2018, Silva et al., 2018). Meta-analyses have highlighted that crop mycorrhizal responsiveness is highly dependent on plant and AMF species, and that certain AMF-crop associations are more efficient for high crop yields (Lehman et al., 2012, Van Geel et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_58]

This symbiosis is based on a mutually beneficial trade between the organisms: plants return up to 20% of their photosynthates to the hosted fungi, allowing them to grow and explore the soil in search of essential nutrients that they will share with their vegetal host (Frey-Klett et al., 2007, Jung et al., 2012). This symbiosis is reported to be beneficial for plant growth and health, thanks to all the benefits discussed in the following part of the chapter. However, there are reports that AMF symbiosis is sometimes detrimental to plant host development and defence against phytopathogens (Jin et al., 2017, Bernaola et al., 2018). Non-mycotrophic plants that are not naturally mycorrhized may show negative effects of AMF interaction, as is the case for Arabidopsis thaliana, where the recognition of surrounding Rhizophagus irregularis induces strong local defence against it (Jin et al., 2017). Differences in carbon (C) metabolism and allocation to the AMF are reported to be a plausible explanation. Indeed, when in interaction with too-demanding AMF, C$_4$ grasses such as Andropogon gerardii and Schizachyrium scoparium failed to inhibit its strong root colonisation and hyphal abundance, resulting in growth deficits, compared to C$_3$ grasses that were able to prevent this (Grman, 2012). [SOURCE: Page_58]

39 [SOURCE: Page_58]

Figure 10: Arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi mutualism with rice and its benefits for growth and stress response The AMF symbiosis has been reported to benefit crops such as rice at three important levels: improved growth and nutrition, abiotic stress tolerance and enhanced defence against pathogens. The presence and establishment of interaction between rice and AMF enhances lateral root development and soil anchoring, as well as shoot growth at a later stage. The metabolic activity of AMF enables the solubilisation of inorganic phosphate (Pi) and improved uptake of essential nutrients such as nitrogen (N) or water by rice roots. AMF symbiosis also helps rice to mitigate salt stress and cadmium contamination by reducing their accumulation in roots and shoots and by accumulating specific osmoprotectants. Finally, AMF symbiosis helps rice to defend itself against both local and systemic pathogen infection via the mycorrhiza-induced resistance (MIR) pathways. At the earliest stages of the interaction, salicylic acid (SA)-dependent responses prevent local infection by pathogens, while systemic suppression of SA and induction of jasmonic acid (JA) primes rice aerial parts for pathogen attack. This figure was created with BioRender.com. [SOURCE: Page_59]

40 [SOURCE: Page_59]

Some plants allocate more carbon to more cooperative AMF species, while reducing the abundance of less efficient ones in favour of the best (Kiers et al., 2011). The same study showed that these preferences are also found in fungi, with the most cooperative AMF returning more P to the most C-productive plant host, while the less cooperative fungal species hoard phosphate from the less C-productive host, potentially depriving it of access to a vital nutrient and inhibiting its growth in the long term. The efficiency of AMF symbiotic cooperation is based on plant growth response, carbon cost per unit of P transferred and resource hoarding strategies (into vesicles or spores, depending on the AMF species) (Kiers et al., 2011). Other factors should be considered, such as plant genotype, AMF genetics and geographical origin, which can drive the plant-AMF association from highly parasitic to highly mutualistic (Jin et al., 2017, Davidson et al., 2019). This symbiotic equilibrium should be kept in mind when studying the interaction between crop species and AMF, as the 'always beneficial' view can quickly be disregarded when looking at it in detail. [SOURCE: Page_60]

Beneficial roles of the AMF symbiosis in crops growth, nutritional status, and stress tolerance When both interacting partners are cooperative, AMF symbiosis can have several important beneficial effects on host growth, defence response and overall health (Figure 10). The plant root response to mycorrhization has been extensively studied, both phenotypically and molecularly. This symbiosis triggers a significant increase in both root and shoot biomass production in agronomically interesting crops, especially maize, wheat and rice (Paszkowski & Boller, 2002, Gutjahr et al., 2009, Suzuki et al., 2015, Fiorilli et al., 2018, Campo & San Segundo, 2020). This improvement in plant growth is explained by several mechanisms due to the soil activity of the fungus. Its mere presence close to its host induces the development of up to 50% more lateral roots, which is the main site of preferential mycorrhization, in maize, Medicago truncatula or rice (Paszkowski & Boller, 2002, Oláh et al., 2005, Gutjahr et al., 2009, Chiu et al., 2022). These changes in root architecture allow better anchoring of the root system and the whole plant in its environment, which is sometimes crucial under adverse conditions (Chiu et al., 2022). The hyphal network of the symbiotic AMF allows it to cover a larger area of the soil than the area available to the roots alone. This greater prospection potentials recover water and essential but limited, poorly bioavailable nutrients for the development of its host, maintaining or even enhancing its growth under limiting conditions (Begum et al., 2019, Kadam et al., 2020). There are numerous reports stating that phosphate is one of the most essential nutrients for plant growth and metabolism, and a key driver of AMF-plant interactions (Smith et al., 2011). Inorganic phosphorus (Pi) is often crystallised or complexed in soil clay minerals [SOURCE: Page_60]

41 [SOURCE: Page_60]

or particulate matter, rendering it unavailable for uptake by plant roots (Artursson et al., 2006). This lack of bioavailability triggers the recruitment and long-term maintenance of AMF associations with needy host plants (Breuillin et al., 2010). This better uptake or direct transfer of inorganic phosphate affects the expression of Pi transporters and related genes, in particular the inhibition of the Pi starvation response (typical of low Pi soils) in the leaves of wheat and rice, associated with a higher Pi content in the shoots of these crop species (Yang et al. 2012; Suzuki et al., 2015, Berruti et al., 2016, Fiorilli et al. 2018, Campo & San Segundo 2020). AMF communities are also reported to enrich bacterial rhizospheric communities with phosphate solubilising bacteria, synergistically increasing plants uptake of this essential nutrient (Zhang et al., 2018). Reports on the induction of other plant transporters such as nitrogen (N), potassium (K), sulphur and microelements associated with their shoot content under mycorrhizal conditions shows that the AMF symbiosis has an important beneficial effect on the overall mineral nutrition of its host (Berruti et al., 2016, Mbodj et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_61]

This mutualistic interaction also helps plants to tolerate other abiotic stresses such as drought, heat (both low and high temperatures) and high salinity through several mechanisms, namely improved nutrient uptake and energy metabolism, plant water status, secondary metabolite accumulation and osmotic adjustment (Begum et al., 2019, Sagar et al., 2021). These mechanisms have been reported with a variety of AMF species, mainly Rhizophagus irregularis or R. intraradices, but also Funneliformis mosseae and other Glomus species, on a range of important crops (rice, tomato, lettuce, wheat, maize) (Mbodj et al., 2018, Begum et al., 2019 and references therein). Under salinity stress, AMF symbiosis maintains water uptake and osmotic and ROS balance by modulating aquaporins, triggering antioxidant enzyme activities and inducing the accumulation of osmotic adjustment metabolites (i.e. proline, glycine, betaine) while sequestering sodium in plant vacuoles (Mbodj et al., 2018, Sagar et al., 2021). AMF can compensate for the loss of photosynthetic capacity and competition for uptake of soil solution ions that typically occur under these drought conditions by maintaining the uptake of N, K, P or magnesium, which are typically important for photosynthesis (Sagar et al., 2021). Through all these effects, AMF mitigates the typical damage of salinity stress and maintains plant growth and yield. [SOURCE: Page_61]

In terms of abiotic toxicity mitigation, this symbiosis also alleviates heavy metal exposure in several plant species. The main mechanisms to avoid the loss of shoot and root growth and leaf chlorosis typical of metal toxicity are the immobilisation of metal in root cell vacuoles, the maintenance of photosynthesis in plants in contaminated areas, and the uptake of immobilised non-toxic nutrients (i.e. Cu, Zn and P) to dilute the metal present in plant tissues (de Andrade et al., 2015, Begum et al., 2019). Several studies on cadmium accumulation in [SOURCE: Page_61]

42 [SOURCE: Page_61]

rice have reported these mechanisms in F. mosseae and R. irregularis: in mycorrhized plants, cadmium influx is reduced in both roots and shoots, with cadmium (Cd) being sequestered in root cell vacuoles to prevent translocation to shoots and grains, and chelated to inactive, less toxic forms (Li et al., 2016, Chen et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_62]

It has been observed that mycorrhizal plants experience a reduction in symptoms due to pathogen pressure at two complementary levels. Fungal root colonisation by AMF acts as a protective barrier and competes with local phytopathogens for colonisation site and soil nutrients, as is the case against the root knot nematode Meloidogyne graminicola or root rot caused by Rhizoctonia solanii or Phytophthora infestans on tomato, potato or rice (Pozo & Azcòn-Aguilar, 2007, Jung et al., 2012, Shouteden et al., 2015, Dowarah et al., 2021). By altering the root exudation of secondary metabolites, AMF symbiosis also modifies the fitness and metabolism of the rhizospheric microbiota, as reported in the case of potato, which is less penetrated and attacked by the parasitic nematode M. incognita when mycorrhized with F. mosseae (Jung et al., 2012, Vos et al., 2012, Shouteden et al., 2015). On the other hand, the establishment of the interaction induces a slight induction of a local defence response (callose deposition, ROS, R proteins, phytoalexins and phenolic compounds production), led by SA- related genes and rapidly inhibited by the recognition of symbiosis signals (Kadam et al., 2020). In the case of mycorrhization, this immune response to a soil-borne phytopathogen is even faster and allows the root to more efficiently control the spread of the invader, at the site of mycorrhization or even in non-mycorrhized parts of the root system, as is the case for mycorrhized tomato plants against infection by the oomycete Phytophtora parasitica (Shouteden et al., 2015, Gupta et al., 2017, Kadam et al., 2020, Dowarah et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_62]

This beneficial effect on biotic stress isn't limited to the underground parts of mycorrhized plants. By modulating the phytohormonal pathways of JA, SA and abscisic acid (ABA), AMF symbiosis induces a priming of host defence responses in shoots via a mechanism called mycorrhiza-induced resistance (MIR) (Jung et al., 2012, Gupta et al., 2017, Fiorilli et al., 2018, Nishad et al., 2020). Cameron et al., 2013 and Kadam et al., 2020 describe it in two different stages depending on the degree of interaction. The root-localised, SA- dependent induction of the defence response during the initial establishment stages is de facto mycorrhiza-induced resistance that prepares the rest of the root system for further infection by a phytopathogen in a systemic acquired resistance-like response (Cameron et al., 2013). SA levels are rapidly inhibited by cross-talk with JA and ABA levels, reducing the fungal- induced MTI and pass through to the shoots, inducing a systemic initial priming of cell wall defences (Pozo & Azcòn-Aguilar, 2007, Kadam et al., 2020). There are still gaps and uncertainties about the triggers of this MIR priming, which is transferred from the root to the [SOURCE: Page_62]

43 [SOURCE: Page_62]

Figure 11: New biofertilizer development strategies (from Mitter et al., 2021) Four novel bioprospecting approaches are considered: single-species inoculants and synthetic microbial communities derived from culturomics, prebiotic formulations derived from root exudates, and whole microbiomes derived from natural or engineered ecosystems. After bioprospecting and in vitro testing, a suitable formulation must be developed to ensure shelf life and durability. The final steps before commercialisation include in planta trials under controlled (growth chamber and greenhouse) and uncontrolled (field) conditions, production scale-up to commercial scale, biosafety testing and compliance with applicable regulations. [SOURCE: Page_63]

44 [SOURCE: Page_63]

shoots of mycorrhized plants. However, this MIR seems to induce broad-spectrum resistance, as reduction of shoot symptoms and control of pathogen development have been reported in a variety of plant species, against a diversity of phytopathogenic infections, whether viral, bacterial or fungal (Kadam et al., 2020). Symbiosis between Medicago truncatula and R. intraradices systematically reduces leaf damage caused by Xanthomonas campestris pv. alfalfae by more than 70% (Liu et al., 2007). Mycorrhization between F. mosseae and wheat dramatically reduces symptoms caused by X. translucens infection (Fiorilli et al., 2018). Mycorrhization of rice (wild or cultivated) by R. intraradices enables it to resist attack by Pyricularia oryzae by modifying the secondary metabolism of its host, especially the hormonal balance (Campos-Soriano et al., 2012, Tian et al., 2019, Campo et al., 2020b). [SOURCE: Page_64]

Bioinoculants: replace chemical inputs, but with what strategies? [SOURCE: Page_64]

To address the fundamental needs of agriculture without the use of harmful chemicals or pesticides, the application of bioinoculants, such as biofertilizers or biological control agents, has been proposed and studied over the past decades. Prepared with beneficial microorganisms, they contribute to soil fertility and/or crop growth (Sivasakthivelan & Saranraj, 2013) by increasing nutrient uptake efficiency and tolerance to biotic and abiotic stresses (Busby et al., 2017). Two major groups of beneficial microorganisms are of interest for these bioinoculants: plant-growth-promoting rhizobacteria (PGPR) and arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF). Their beneficial effects on soil fertility or host health depend mainly on soil physical and chemical properties as well as plant species and cultivars (Schlaeppi & Bulgarelli, 2015, Haskett et al., 2021). Roles in facilitating nutrient uptake, induction of growth and grain yield, enhancement of abiotic stress tolerance as well as mitigation or reduction of biotic stress have been reported for both organisms (Vessey, 2003, Sivasakthivelan & Saranraj, 2013, Nehra & Choudhary, 2015, Hart et al., 2018, Basu et al., 2021, Basiru et al., 2021). Recently formulated PGPR genera include Azotobacter, Azospirillum, Bacillus, Paraburkholderia, Rhizobium and Bradyrhizobium thanks to their free nitrogen-fixing or phosphate-solubilising abilities and production of phytohormone-like metabolites in the vicinity of crop roots (Sivasakthivelan & Saranraj, 2013; Nehra & Choudhary, 2015, Bernabeu et al., 2018). The biofertilizer market is estimated to reach USD 3.5 billion by 2025, consisting for 80% of nitrogen-fixing microorganisms, followed by phosphate-solubilising microorganisms at 14% (Basu et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_64]

45 [SOURCE: Page_64]

Figure 12: Synthetic communities’ experiments: concepts and strategies (from Vorholt et al., 2017) Depending on the research questions, synthetic communities are selected and designed from the strains collected from microbiomes originating from natural habitats and interesting host. They are then inoculated on their associated host of selected genotypes, under selected (or not) conditions. The synthetic community assembly and selection depends on the question of interest: it can be based on phylogeny, or OTU clustering and interaction networks, as well as based on functions that they can bring to their host. [SOURCE: Page_65]

46 [SOURCE: Page_65]

Looking at biological control agents, some famous bacterial formulations such as Serenade or Proradix, based on Bacillus subtilis QST 713 and Pseudomonas sp. DSMX 13134, mediate Botrytis cinerea infections and a variety of phytopathogenic fungi on a wide range of vegetable growing cultures, respectively (Velivelli et al., 2014). Mycorrhizal products are generally used in horticulture but are gaining interest in crop applications due to their biofertilization and biological control properties (Islam et al., 2021, Díaz-Urbano et al., 2023). They are dominated by AMF species, with Rhizophagus irregularis, Funneliformis mosseae and Claroideoglomus etunicatum being the three main formulated species (39%, 21% and 16% respectively) (Basiru et al., 2021). Bioinoculants formulated with endophytic filamentous fungi also have clear advantages in both plant growth induction and phytopathogen control, with some synergistic effects when mycorrhizal and filamentous inoculants are co-inoculated (Díaz-Urbano et al., 2023). [SOURCE: Page_66]

There are some setbacks in the use of current bioinoculants, mainly because their performance under field conditions isn't as promising as it was under controlled conditions (Igiehon & Babalola, 2017, Hart et al., 2018). Conventional bioinoculants generally consist of one or two co-inoculated strains selected for their ability to promote plant growth, elucidated under in vitro or controlled conditions, which haven't been tested in the field (Hart et al., 2018, de Souza et al., 2020, Basiru et al., 2021). Their performance under field conditions is limited by competition with the pre-existing population or lack of specificity with the host plant (Schlaeppi & Bulgarelli, 2015; Haskett et al., 2021). Technical and practical constraints such as the formulation itself, its ease of use, its shelf life or product registration and patent filing are further limitations to consider when trying to develop a new and effective bioinoculant (Nehra & Choudhary, 2015; Basu et al., 2021, Figure 11). The main idea to increase the specificity and resilience of the bioinoculant in the soil is to ensure that the species used are already naturally present in the plant's root environment, don't harm the pre-existing root microbiota and ensure some beneficial roles for the host (de Souza et al., 2020). By studying the microbiome of the plant of interest using omics approaches, it is possible to identify consortia of microbial species that naturally interact with their host and extract the individuals that favour their growth before constructing a synthetic microbiota or community (Mazzola et al., 2016). In this way, multi-species inoculation in a synthetic community, co-occurring and specific to the crop of interest, would be a more effective and sustainable method than any of the previous ones for developing the agroecology of tomorrow (Busby et al., 2017, Figure 12). There are reports of co-inoculations being widely profitable for the host's health and growth, but it doesn’t always meet these expectations. A bacterial combination of Methylobacterium oryzae, Azospirillum brasilense and Burkholderia pyrrocinia does increase biomass, nutrient content and soil metabolic activity in different crops, including rice (Madhaiyan et al., 2009). [SOURCE: Page_66]

47 [SOURCE: Page_66]

A commercial bio-inoculant developed by Laboratorios CKC Argentina, formulated using Pseudomonas fluorescens and Azospirillum brasilense, did slightly improve the growth, content and number of grains in field rice, without affecting soil microbial communities, in a rice genotype-dependent manner (García de Salamone et al., 2012). Studies on the tripartite interactions (detailed in a following section) highlighted the need to choose associations between different types of microorganisms, already established in the microbial diversity of the soil or the rhizosphere of the host which are already reported to establish beneficial interactions with the crop host cultivar of interest, in a synthetic community formulation (Mar Vázquez, 2000, Hoseinzade et al., 2016, Sagar et al., 2021, Poveda & Eugui, 2022). [SOURCE: Page_68]

Bioinoculants with AMF species: (too) high hopes and expectations? [SOURCE: Page_68]

Although commercial AMF inoculants have been widely used in horticultural production, they have recently regained interest in the formulation of crop bioinoculants to replace polluting pesticides and chemical inputs (Hart et al., 2018, Islam et al., 2021). AMF- induced crop growth, improved nutritional status and healthier overall crop condition under controlled conditions and in greenhouses lead to questions about their efficacy under field conditions. 65% of the trials were carried out under greenhouse conditions, where the controls consisted of plants in substrates without fungal spores (Berruti et al., 2016). The observed differences in root colonisation and reduction of bioinoculant growth enhancement claims in field versus greenhouse trials are probably due to the fact that controls are colonised by naturally occurring AMF communities of field soils, which could naturally improve their health while interacting with the inoculated AMF bioinoculant and reducing its efficiency (Igiehon & Babalola, 2017). A study in the Canadian prairies found that the same bioinoculant had different persistence in soils, being detectable for three consecutive growing seasons in one, but disappearing after three months in the other (Islam et al., 2021). This bioinoculant also has a strong effect on natural AMF communities, driving the evolution of indigenous fungal communities and, more broadly, probably the whole root-associated microbiota, as previously described (Basiru et al., 2021, Dowarah et al., 2021). The meta-analysis by Basiru et al., 2021 reported that R. irregularis and F. mosseae are part of the most widely formulated AMF species, thanks to their efficiency to rapidly colonise roots of a wide variety of hosts, nutrient feeding capacities and other microbial associations and dialogues. A large part of the available bioinoculant formulations are still based on one or two single AMF species, while recommendations for an efficient formulation state that they should include a mixture of species, consider the origin and soil physicochemical characteristics, with a high number of propagules, include beneficial bacteria and have a long shelf life (Berruti et al., 2016, Igiehon & Babalola, 2017, Basiru et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_68]

There are still large inconsistencies in AMF formulations depending on the AMF species mixed, the formulation and propagule type used, the plant species and the environment in which it is applied, and testing is often biased (greenhouse trials only, inappropriate controls, extreme conditions), which must be considered when trying to formulate a specific AMF bioinoculant for specific environmental conditions (Hart et al., 2018). Next generation AMF bioinoculants should consider all these specific aspects to ensure good establishment and persistence of the applied beneficial microorganisms in the field (Igiehon & Babalola, 2017, Hart et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_70]

AMF symbiosis in paddy fields: potential for proper biofertilisation and current knowledge on natural interactions [SOURCE: Page_70]

As mentioned above, rice farming typically floods rice fields as an irrigation method, resulting in waterlogged anaerobic conditions in the soil, which is detrimental to the establishment and function of the AMF symbiosis. However, it has been reported that fungal propagules naturally present in anaerobic soils can still develop to form new functional AMF individuals when dry conditions finally occur (Lumini et al., 2011, Vallino et al., 2014). By switching to water-saving irrigation methods such as alternate drying and wetting, rain-fed irrigation, and others (Richards & Sanders, 2015, Saito et al., 2018), mutualism between cultivated rice and naturally occurring AMF communities would be possible, which would be beneficial for rice growth and yield in the long term. These AMF communities in water- damaged soils could be supplemented with specific AMF species, formulated according to the needs (biofertilisation, bioremediation needs, environmental factors, rice genotype, etc.) to ensure a proper reconstruction of the beneficial fungal root-associated community (Berruti et al., 2016). Furthermore, a recent analysis highlighted that the four major rice producing areas in China, representing one third of worldwide rice production areas, have worrying concentrations of arsenic, cadmium and lead, which could be mitigated by efficient AMF communities (de Andrade et al., 2015, Li et al., 2016, Chen et al., 2019, Mu et al., 2019, Zhuang et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_70]

At the beginning of this millennium, rice was one of the model plants used to understand the physiological and molecular basis as well as the phenotypic effects of arbuscular mycorrhization in non-legume plants (Güimil et al., 2005, Gutjahr et al., 2008 & 2009, Campos-Soriano et al., 2010). These studies under controlled conditions first highlighted the potential of AMF to improve rice health under more complex soil conditions (Table 2). Under greenhouse conditions, there is now a large body of evidence that rice mycorrhization produces all of the above beneficial effects on a broader scale. [SOURCE: Page_70]

51 [SOURCE: Page_70]

Figure 13: Simplified representation of interactions between soil, crop host roots, arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF) and mycorrhiza-helper bacteria (MHB), in two AMF life stages: symbiosis establishment (A) and functioning (B) (adapted from Frey-Klett et al., 2007) Blue numbers: direct effects of MHB. Purple numbers: direct effects of AMF. Green numbers: direct effects of host roots. Brown numbers: direct effects of soil. A: (1) MHB improves soil conduciveness to AMF; (2) MHB improves root receptiveness to AMF; (3) MHB interacts with plant–AMF recognition and symbiosis establishment; MHB promotes germination of AMF propagules and survival and growth of the mycelium; (5) AMF selects bacterial populations in the rhizosphere; (6) the soil supports presymbiotic fungal growth; (7) the soil determines root receptiveness to AMF; (8) roots select bacterial populations in the rhizosphere. B: (1) MHB contributes to nutrient mobilization from soil minerals and organic matter, and to detoxification of the mycorrhizospheric soil; (2) MHB has an impact on root architecture through the production of growth factors and protection of plants against phytopathogens; (3) MHB improves fungal nutrition by, for example, the provision of nitrogen in the case of diazotrophs and enhances mycelial extension by the production of growth factors; (4) fungal exudates serve as nutrients for MHB; (5) AMF mobilizes nutrients from soil minerals and organic matter; (6) the soil provides the plant with water and solutes; (7) the root contributes to mobilization of nutrients from soil minerals and organic matter; (8) AMF transfers water and mineral nutrients to the roots and protects the plant against pathogens; (9) the root provides the fungus with photosynthates. (Mechanisms 8 and 9 are specific to the symbiotic mycorrhizal interaction.) This figure was created with BioRender.com. [SOURCE: Page_71]

52 [SOURCE: Page_71]

However, these beneficial effects are dependent on the rice cultivar, developmental stage and AMF species (Suzuki et al., 2015). Surprisingly, there are relatively few studies that simultaneously address rice growth, abiotic and biotic stress tolerance and molecular response in a variety of cultivars and AMF models. Two complementary studies by Campos- Soriano et al. showed that mycorrhization of japonica cultivar Senia by Rhizophagus intraradices induced a growth boost at the tillering stage, coupled with basal and systemic defence responses against Pyricularia oryzae (Campos-Soriano et al., 2010 & 2012). All 12 japonica rice cultivars mycorrhized by either F. mossseae or R. irregularis showed an increase in shoot Pi content, but showed a contrasting pattern of AMF colonisation and effects on their rice growth, ranging from a decrease, to insignificant, to an increase in height (Campo et al., 2020a). These discrepancies were also observed in the mycorrhizal growth response of 64 rice cultivars in association with a single AMF species, F. mosseae (Suzuki et al., 2015). When mycorrhized with F. mosseae or R. intraradices, another japonica cultivar is more tolerant to cadmium toxicity due to its effect on cadmium influx, sequestration in vacuoles and chelation to inactive forms (Li et al., 2016, Chen et al., 2019). Reports on the biological control potential of rice mycorrhization also depend on the combination studied, as reported by Campo et al., 2020a among their 12 japonica cultivars. On the other hand, a multi-AMF species inoculant on two tropical japonica cultivars increased both their growth and susceptibility to insect attack and Rhizoctonia solani infection (Bernaola et al., 2018). To develop AMF bioinoculants for rice production under water-saving conditions, it is essential to deepen our understanding of the symbiotic compatibility between a greater diversity of rice and AMF species. This will shed light on combinations that are both beneficial for rice growth and responses to environmental stresses, which could be directly applied in rice fields. [SOURCE: Page_72]

Initial studies on the AMF communities in paddy fields reported little or no colonisation of different rice cultivars under conventional farming conditions (high inputs flooded monocultures) (Lumini et al., 2011, Vallino et al., 2014). There are scarce studies on the composition and diversity of AMF communities in paddy fields, but this topic gains interest since the discovery that partial to complete aerobic soil conditions enables AMF establishment and symbiosis function (Vallino et al., 2009). Experimental and farmers’ fields from all over the globe (Ghana, United States, India, Burkina Faso) shows clear colonisation by AMF species, especially in rainfed and alternate wetting-drying irrigation systems (Sarkodee-Addo et al., 2020, Chialva et al., 2020, Surendirakumar et al., 2021, Barro et al., 2022). Evenmore in fields, AMF communities and rice colonisation are highly dependent on plant cultivar and developmental stage, irrigation mode and geographical site (Wang et al., 2015, Suzuki et al., 2015, Sisaphaithong et al., 2017, Sarkodee-Addo et al., 2020, Masson et al., 2022). Members of the Glomerales, Archaeosporales and Diversisporales are generally the predominant orders [SOURCE: Page_72]

53 [SOURCE: Page_72]

across all studies (Lumini et al., 2011, Chiala et al., 2020, Barro et al., 2022). In China and Ghana, members of the Glomeraceae, Claroideoglomeraceae and Paraglomeraceae families have been found in paddy fields, including the well-studied R. irregularis and F. mosseae species (Wang et al., 2015, Sarkodee-Addo et al., 2020). In Indian fragrant black rice fields, Surendirakumar et al., 2021 identified both R. intraradices and F. mosseae in soil and root samples. These last three AMF species are reported to have beneficial effects on multiple rice cultivars but their symbiotic compatibility with rice has been poorly studied, i.e. the relationship between fungal colonisation, phenotypic responses of rice growth and defence against phytopathogen and molecular responses of a variety of rice to these AMF. [SOURCE: Page_73]

Tripartite interactions based on mycorrhiza helper bacteria: a new hope for bioinoculants? [SOURCE: Page_73]

In the soil, during mycorrhizal symbiosis, AMF are surrounded by other microorganisms that can affect the symbiosis they establish with their plant host (Figure 13). Bacteria that enhance the establishment or functioning of mycorrhization are termed mycorrhiza helper bacteria (MHB) and inhabit the mycorrhizosphere (consisting of the areas around the fungi, mycorrhized roots, spores and associated mycelium in the soil) (Frey-Klett et al., 2007, Sangwan & Prasanna, 2021, Figure 13). By endophytically colonising or forming biofilms on AMF hyphae, they have both direct (increase in germination rate, hyphal development and ramification) and indirect (root growth and branching promoting effects, increase in bioavailability of essential nutrients, pathogen inhibition) means to support the AMF symbiosis (Frey-Klett et al., 2007, Sangwan & Prasanna, 2021). Phosphate solubilising bacteria have been extensively studied in this tripartite interaction because of their key role in the establishment of the AMF symbiosis. In the mycorrhizosphere, this type of bacteria has a synergistic effect on the plant host's assimilation of phosphate, specifically by mineralising phytate into the AMF hyphosphere (Cameron et al., 2013, Zhang et al., 2014). In turn, AMF have been reported to enrich rhizosphere bacterial communities with phosphate-solubilising bacteria (PSB) (Zhang et al., 2018). Among the MHB, closely associated bacteria have been found from already established PGPR such as Lysobacter, Bacillus, Burkholderia, Rhizobium, Herbaspirillum, Azospirillum, Bradyrhizobium and the originally studied Pseudomonas (fluorescens BBc$_6$) (Frey-Klett et al., 2007, Sangwan & Prasanna, 2021). Studies on the specificity of the interaction between MHB, AMF and their plant host, as well as the specific effects of these MHB on the AMF symbiosis, need to be conducted to properly understand how this interplay benefits plant health (Basiru et al., 2023). [SOURCE: Page_73]

54 [SOURCE: Page_73]

This concept of mycorrhizal helper bacteria and their effect on both AMF and plant host raises an important question for agronomy. Does the addition of multiple beneficial microorganisms, interacting with each other and with the crop, sharing the same microenvironment with specific beneficial roles for their host, have additional, synergistic or antagonistic effects on plant health and yield? This is the postulate of the new bioinoculant formulation hypothesis that needs to be further elaborated (Begum et al., 2021). However, the results are very contrasting depending on the plant host variety, symbiont genotype and parameter studied. Co-inoculation of Glomus fasciculatum with the Azotobacter chrococcum and Pseudomonas fluorescens showed an increase in shoot and root biomass of two tomato cultivars, with one being more efficiently mycorrhized than the other (Kavatagi & Lakshman, 2014). The synergy of co-inoculation with a mixture of AMFs and two Pseudomonas was reported to increase wheat yield by 41%, as well as the nutritional value of the grain through improved assimilation, use and allocation of nitrogen and phosphorus (Mäder et al., 2010). In the same study, these synergistic effects were not observed in rice or mung bean crops. However, when rice is inoculated with both Gigaspora albida and Azotobacter or Pseudomonas sp., soil and plant levels of N, P, K, as well as grain yield, are increased (Chen et al., 2023). The combined use of the PSB Bacillus sp. PSI$_7$ and a synthetic AMF community (comprising Funneliformis mosseae, F. geosporum or Rhizophagus intraradices, among others) simultaneously increased PSB population densities, AMF colonisation intensity and post-harvest soil fertility, resulting in higher maize and wheat grain yield (Wahid et al., 2020). These beneficial effects of co-inoculation also include increased abiotic stress tolerance in plant hosts. In tobacco, the co-occurrence of Glomus versiforme and Bacillus methylotrophicus improved drought tolerance through the production of phenolics and flavonoids (by at least 58%) and offset the deleterious effects of stress (reduced growth and electrolyte leakage) (Begum et al., 2021). Salt stress tolerance is improved in two different rice varieties in the field by the combination of Pseudomonas putida and P. fluorescens with Rhizophagus irregularis, thanks to better catalase activity, proline accumulation and an increase in grain quality (nitrogen content, yield, grain biomass) (Norouzinia et al., 2020). Cadmium tolerance induced by inoculation of rice roots with F. mosseae and R. irregularis depends on the soil bacterial communities with which they interact (Chen et al., 2019). Nevertheless, certain associations may have deleterious effects on the plant host. The association between the AMF F. mosseae and P. graminis improves growth and reduces symptoms due to an attack by X. translucens on wheat, whereas the opposite is observed when A. brasilense is inoculated (Vannini et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_74]

55 [SOURCE: Page_74]

It is interesting to note that inoculation with PGPRs can lead to a change in the mycorrhization frequency of AMFs. Indeed, we can observe a decrease in the frequency of an AMF naturally isolated from wheat on its preferred host, as well as a decrease in the number of arbuscules when wheat 'Mercato' interacts with R. irregularis and P. putida (Mäder et al., 2010, Pérez-de-Luque et al., 2017). On the other hand, an increase in colonisation and arbuscular rate was observed in the interaction between R. irregularis and Thymus serpyllum or Antennaria dioica in the presence of Bacillus amyloliquefaciens (Xie et al., 2018b). Although there is sometimes some evidence of functional competition, in these specific cases the interaction between hosts - AMFs - PGPRs results in better host growth and health. The influence of fungal colonisation on the density and function of the bacterial community is difficult to quantify (Pérez-de-Luque et al., 2017, Xie et al., 2018b). Fungal metabolites such as trehalose enhance MHB recruitment, function and biofilm formation on hyphae (Frey-Klett et al., 2007). To understand how this beneficial recruitment occurs, several other attraction mechanisms need to be investigated. Zhang et al. (2018) showed that AMF recruits alkaline phosphatase producing bacteria to provide Pi mineralising activity in the rhizosphere of maize and soybean. To maintain rice tolerance to cadmium, R. irregularis actively recruits the cadmium-absorbing actinobacteria Arthrobacter (Chen et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_75]

It can be assumed that co-inoculation of AMF and PGPR would allow better stability and establishment of the inoculum due to their interactions with each other and their modifications of the root metabolism of their host. The presence and functioning of the AMF symbiosis creates a specific microenvironment in which certain strains of MHB reside and thrive, establishing them in the plant microbiota (Basiru et al., 2023). In a similar way than plants controlling their microbiota by phenols exudation, AMF and rhizobacteria can actively alter and control the plant microbiota by acting on the plant's exuded metabolites (Artursson et al., 2006, Cameron et al., 2013). This structure and nurture the establishment of beneficial microbiota and contributes to plant resilience (Stringlis et al., 2018). Evidence of these virtuous circles suggests that co-inoculation or multi-strain inoculation may be a way to improve bio- inoculants. Initial reports of the use of microbial consortia containing AMF in rice fields are effective, with improved soil structure, rice growth and grain yield, while allowing for a reduction in N and P fertiliser use (Bao et al., 2022). The greater the intraspecific richness of the inoculum, the greater the beneficial effects on the plant, the multiple roles in improving host health, and in the establishment and enrichment of root microbiota (Zhang et al., 2020a, Hu et al., 2021, with Pseudomonas consortia). [SOURCE: Page_75]

56 [SOURCE: Page_75]

All these prospects of consortia having beneficial and perennial effects on crops give hope for future bioinoculant formulations. The study of the phenotypic and molecular effects of single to dual inoculation with different beneficial microorganisms on their host response will lead to the characterisation of crop responses to a diversity of beneficials. This comparison of common and specific physiological changes induced by different microorganisms (bacteria versus AMF) in crops of agronomic interest will help to decipher highly compatible interactors that can be used in the field, as well as genetic targets for improvement in crop breeding. In the light of this literature study, my thesis will be organised around the study of the phenotypic and molecular responses of rice to a variety of microorganisms naturally associated with it at the root level, with the aim of application to the biocontrol of pathogens. [SOURCE: Page_76]

The general objective of my thesis is to gain a better understanding of the general and specific responses (physiological, phenotypic and molecular) of a cereal of interest, rice, to phytobeneficial microorganisms (PGPR, AMF, single or combined). The general idea is to highlight these common and specific molecular pathways, first in the face of a diversity of bacteria, then during symbiotic interactions with mycorrhizal fungi, and finally during combinations of both types of microorganisms. [SOURCE: Page_76]

78 [SOURCE: Page_97]

Chapter 2: Phenotypic and molecular responses of rice during interaction with the plant-growth- promoting endophyte Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 [SOURCE: Page_98]

Ludivine GUIGARD$_1$, Nicolas BUSSET$_1$, Isabelle RIMBAULT$_1$, Lionel MOULIN$_1$, Pierre CZERNIC$_1$ [SOURCE: Page_98]

1 PHIM Plant Health Institute, Univ Montpellier, IRD, CIRAD, INRAE, Institut Agro, Montpellier, France [SOURCE: Page_98]

Ce chapitre est rédigé en anglais, sous forme d’article. Il se focalise sur l’étude des réponses phénotypiques et moléculaires du riz à une inoculation racinaire de la bactérie endophyte du riz Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 (Pk). Le but de cette étude était de démontrer les bénéfices apportés à la croissance du riz par cette bactérie et de décrire précisément la résistance systémique induite (ISR) provoquée après une infection secondaire par une bactérie phytopathogène, Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99 (Xoo). Une analyse complémentaire des effets phénotypiques de son inoculation dans un substrat différent est discutée à la fin de ce chapitre, pour mettre en lumière l’impact de l’environnement sur les interactions biotiques. [SOURCE: Page_98]

79 [SOURCE: Page_98]

Résumé du chapitre 2 Des études préliminaires de la réponse du riz à 7 jours après son inoculation racinaire montraient de forts changements moléculaires, à la fois à l’échelle racinaire et foliaire, révélés par des analyses transcriptomiques (RNAseq, King et al., 2019 & 2023). L’étude du génome de cette bactérie stimulant la croissance de la plante a révélé des capacités de fixation libre de l’azote N$_2$, de production de phytohormones ainsi que de composés antimicrobiens (Dias et al., 2019). Deux études sur la réponse transcriptomique de Pk M130 à une stimulation par un broyat de plants de riz (Coutinho et al., 2015) ou des exsudats racinaires de riz (Wallner et al., 2023) ont également révélé des réponses complexes potentiellement liées à la mise en place de l’interaction racinaire avec le riz. Dans notre étude, nous avons cherché à démontrer les effets d’amélioration de croissance de la plante hôte par une inoculation racinaire, cette souche représentant une piste potentielle pour la formulation de nouveaux bioinoculants spécifiques du riz. Deux méthodes d’inoculation ont été testées : l’inoculation à partir d’une suspension bactérienne provenant directement d’une croissance sur boîte de pétri en milieu gélosé Luria Broth à faible concentration en sel (LBm), ou après croissance en milieu liquide. Deux types de témoins ont complété cette analyse : les inoculums autoclavés, afin de vérifier que les potentiels effets de l’inoculation sont dû à l’interaction bactérie – riz, et un contrôle traité avec seulement de l’eau stérile. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Dans un premier temps, la colonisation racinaire de Pk sur les racines de riz a été vérifiée à plusieurs points de cinétique (3, 7, 14, 21, 28 et 35 jours après addition des inoculums provenant de culture liquide ou solide). Quel que soit le type d’inoculation choisi, la bactérie est capable de coloniser les racines efficacement (en colonisant les espaces intercellulaires, certaines cellules ainsi que les poils absorbants, à des taux jusqu’à 1.41.106 et 2.40.106 bactéries par mg de racine, via une culture liquide ou sur boîte respectivement). Dans un second temps, les effets sur la croissance et le rendement du riz ont été vérifiés après quatre mois de croissance. L’inoculation bactérienne, quelle que soit sa méthode de croissance, n’induit pas de différence sur la biomasse foliaire sèche ou sur le poids des panicules en grains. Cependant, lorsque l’on étudie le potentiel de réduction de symptômes dus à une infection par Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae (Xoo), l’inoculation à partir d’une culture sur boîte réduit significativement l’étendue de chlorose sur les feuilles infectées (57%), alors que l’inoculum provenant de culture liquide n’induit pas cet effet. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Pour approfondir notre étude, une étude transcriptomique (par RT-qPCR de quelques gènes candidats dans un premier temps, une analyse RNAseq est en cours de séquençage) a été initiée. Nous avons choisi d’analyser les réponses systémiques du riz à l’inoculation [SOURCE: Page_99]

80 [SOURCE: Page_99]

racinaire de Pk, provenant d’une culture sur boîte, en condition infiltrée par Xoo, ou non. Les résultats montrent que Pk influe sur l’expression de gènes de réponse au manque de nutriments et d’autres liés à la voie du jasmonate (en condition témoin). En cas d’attaque par la bactérie phytopathogène (24h après inoculation), les mécanismes spécifiquement liés à l’ISR (modulation des défenses, voies de signalisation du jasmonate et de l’éthylène) ne semblent pas affectés par l’interaction avec Pk. Ainsi, la réduction de chlorose induite par la présence de Pk a peut-être lieu à un autre temps d’infection par Xoo, ou n’affecte pas les gènes marqueurs choisis. Les résultats de l’analyse RNAseq devraient élargir notre vision des mécanismes mis en jeu. [SOURCE: Page_100]

81 [SOURCE: Page_100]

82 [SOURCE: Page_101]

Phenotypic and molecular responses of rice during interaction                 with           the           plant-growth-promoting endophyte Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 Ludivine GUIGARD$_1$, Nicolas BUSSET$_1$, Isabelle RIMBAULT$_1$, Lionel MOULIN$_1$, Pierre CZERNIC$_1$ [SOURCE: Page_102]

1 PHIM Plant Health Institute, Univ Montpellier, IRD, CIRAD, INRAE, Institut Agro, Montpellier, France [SOURCE: Page_102]

Introduction In recent years, due to their potential to improve crop productivity and sustainability, plant microbiome studies have gained interest. The microbiome represents a cohort of naturally occurring microorganisms that inhabits the plant rhizosphere or specific organs and helps sustain the growth of their plant host under adverse conditions (Berendsen et al., 2012). It has beneficial effects on soil fertility (mobilisation of essential nutrients, decomposition of organic matter) or plant growth (production of phytohormones near roots and direct induction of biomass production) (Babalola et al., 2021). Microbiomes can also influence host health through their disease-suppressing capacity at the soil level, either through direct competition with phytopathogens in the rhizosphere or induction of the host defence response (Vannier et al., 2019, Bakker et al., 2020, Trivedi et al., 2020). All these beneficial effects on host health make the study and engineering of plant microbiomes a powerful potential bioinoculant to optimise the reliability, resource use and environmental impact of food production (Busby et al., 2017). Among these beneficial microorganisms, plant-growth-promoting rhizobacteria (PGPR) have been well studied in a wide variety of genera and hosts. This type of interacting rhizobacteria can provide a variety of benefits to their hosts, via their geochemical effects in the soil, their assistance in the nutritional status of the plant, its growth or its tolerance to abiotic and biotic stresses (Lugtenberg & Kamilova, 2009, Backer et al., 2018). The metabolism of certain types of beneficial strains increases the bioavailability of nutrients essential for plant growth, such as nitrogen-fixing or phosphate-solubilising bacteria. These types can be found in several bacterial genera: rice inoculated with Bradyrhizobium sp. OSR278 shows a 20% increase in yield over rice in N-fertilised soil (Chaintreuil et al, 2000). The rice endophytic Burkholderia vietnamiensis, which fixes high amounts of nitrogen in vitro is responsible for a 20% increase of rice yield (Trân Van et al., 2000). Wheat biomass and nitrogen use efficiency [SOURCE: Page_102]

83 [SOURCE: Page_102]

(NUE) are also increased when inoculated with Azospirillum brasilense (Zeffa et al., 2019). Bacteria of the genera Bacillus, Serratia, Azotobacter or Azospirillum (brasilense) and Paraburkholderia (phytofirmans) are able to solubilise inorganic or organic phosphate (P) thanks to specialised enzymes (phosphatases, phytases), thereby increasing the proportion of bioavailable P (otherwise estimated at 0.1% of total soil P) (Sessitsch et al., 2005, Lugtenberg & Kamilova, 2009, Sharma et al., 2013, Peng et al., 2020, Wahid et al., 2020). With mechanisms of cell wall stabilisation, osmolytes accumulation and reactive oxygen species (ROS) detoxification, some strains can increase host tolerance to a variety of abiotic stresses (heat, drought or flooding, salt stress) (Esmaeel et al., 2018, Backer et al., 2018, Sagar et al., 2021). Strains of Burkholderia, Paraburkholderia or Pseudomonas can be considered as bioremediators due to their effect on the regulation of cadmium or toxic aromatic compounds (phenols or benzenes), reducing the harmful effects of pollution in fields (Dias et al., 2018, Wei et al., 2021). Biological control of pathogens is also a reported beneficial effect of certain strains of PGPR. Some Bacillus, Pseudomonas or Paraburkholderia compete or control local root infection by phytopathogens through the rapid colonisation of their host roots and the synthesis of antimicrobial compounds (hydrogen cyanide, putrescine, 4-hydroxybenzoate) that control the colonisation of other microorganisms (Lugtenberg & Kamilova, 2009, Dias et al., 2018, Ngalimat et al., 2021). These very local mechanisms are coupled with the induction of root cell defence responses, with microbe-triggered immunity (MTI) mechanisms (reviewed in chapter 1). This MTI is rapidly inhibited by the production of metabolites such as succinoglycan or calcium chelators, or by modulation of the jasmonate and salicylate balance (Berendsen et al., 2012, Pieterse et al., 2014, Nishad et al., 2020). Once the interaction is established, PGPR can help induce systemic resistance to a wide range of foliar phytopathogens, which is different from systemic acquired resistance (SAR) because it doesn't rely on SA signalling or PR-proteins accumulation. This phenomenon is therefore referred to as Induced Systemic Resistance (ISR), which relies more on jasmonate- and ethylene-dependent signalling and induction of defense genes expression only after leaf phytopathogen attack (Pieterse et al., 2014, Tonelli et al., 2020). ISR induces a stronger and faster defence response only upon pathogen recognition, thanks to a low-cost adaptive defence mechanism called priming (Tonelli et al., 2020). Bacteria of different genera can be mentioned as candidates for biological control of phytopathogens thanks to their induction of ISR in different plant species. Pseudomonas defensor WCS374r (formerly P. fluorescens), P. putida or P. aeruginosa have been reported to induce ISR in rice infected with Pyricularia oryzae (formerly Magnaporthe oryzae) (De Vleesschauwer et al., 2008, Ashajyothi et al., 2019, Ngalimat et al., 2021). Paraburkholderia phytofirmans triggers ISR in a variety of plant species under infection by [SOURCE: Page_103]

84 [SOURCE: Page_103]

Pseudomonas syringae, Botrytis cinerea, Verticillum dahliae or even Fusarium oxysporum, (Timmermann et al., 2017, Esmaeel et al., 2018). Earlier studies of microbial-induced plant transcriptome changes focused on phytopathogens to elucidate molecular pathways of recognition and disease-inducing signalling (Sana et al., 2010). This is also the case for rice, where bacterial phytopathogen attacks and rice diseases are the major cause of yield loss, with millions of hectares of rice infected annually (Ngalimat et al., 2021). Bacterial phytopathogens can infect all the different organs of rice, with the notorious Xanthomonas oryzae (pv. oryzicola or oryzae) and Burkholderia glumae, (Sana et al., 2010, Grewal et al., 2012, Magbanua et al., 2014, Wang et al., 2019). The different pathways induced by their recognition by rice was well-studied until recently with a global epidemiology of Xoo (Diallo et al., 2023). Compared to the increasing data on the impact of phytopathogens on rice physiology, information on how rhizobacteria modulate their rice host transcriptome is scarce. Genera such as Herbaspirillum, Azospirillum, Pseudomonas and Paraburkholderia are considered in the current literature, and rice responses to these PGPR are associated with phytohormone modulation and suppression of defence responses (Brusamarello-Santos et al., 2019, Valetteet al., 2020a & b, Wang et al., 2020). In the Burkholderia sensu lato (s.l., including Burkholderia, Paraburkholderia, Trinickia, Caballeronia, Mycetohabitans, Robbsia, and Pararobbsia, Mullins & Mahenthiralingam, 2021), most plant-beneficial species are included in the Paraburkholderia genus, but some as B. vietnamiensis remain in the Burkholderia sensu stricto genus together with human opportunists as well as plant phytopathogenic species (Estrada-de los Santos et al., 2016, King et al., 2019). Recent studies on the phenotypic or molecular responses of rice to beneficial Paraburkholderia have focused on three main members, P. tropica, P. phytofirmans and P. kururiensis. The first species is reported to have plant-growth promoting activities on sorghum, wheat and barley, suggested to be linked with its biofilm formation on roots and in vitro activities of nitrogen fixation, phosphate solubilisation and antifungal production (Kuramae et al., 2016, Bernabeu et al., 2018, García et al., 2019). The second one is a well-studied PGPR with reported effects on Arabidopsis thaliana, rice, tomato, grapevine, maize, potato and other agronomically-interesting crops, via its 1- aminocyclopropane-1-carboxylate deaminase (ACC) enzymatic activity (preventing ethylene signalling, important abiotic and biotic stress response phytohormone), auxin and siderophores production, as well as its induction of defence response, both local or systemic (ISR) (Ledger et al., 2016, Timmermann et al., 2017, Esmaeel et al., 2018, King et al., 2023). Paraburkholderia kururiensis is a PGPR reported to induce rice’s growth and yield, being an endophytic diazotrophic strain isolated from this crop species (Mattos et al., 2008, King et al., 2019, King et al., 2023). The early response of rice to this particular bacterium has been investigated and ethylene response, development-related genes, glutathione metabolism and [SOURCE: Page_104]

85 [SOURCE: Page_104]

resistance proteins expression were found to be modulated in roots, together with increases in expression of genes related to jasmonate response and oxidative phosphorylation in shoots (Vargas et al., 2012, King et al., 2019 & 2023). Our aim was to verify if Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 (Pk) was able to induce ISR against a Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99 (Xoo) infection. We characterised the phenotypic and molecular responses of rice to Pk during a long-term interaction in a defined set-up. We also monitored Pk colonisation of rice roots both at quantitative (colony forming units, CFU) and qualitative (epifluorescence microscopy) levels at 3, 7, 14, 21, 28- and 35- days post-inoculation (dpi) in a new sterile environment described in Materials and methods. We tested two types of inoculation preparation (from bacterial cells grown on a solid medium or in liquid pre-culture) to understand if they affected the colonisation of rice. This was also tested in a long-term experiment, where rice growth and defence against Xoo were analysed after several weeks of growth in association with Pk. We then analysed Pk-induced molecular changes in rice leaves using RNAseq (recently sent to sequencing) and qRT-PCR on a panel of marker genes for growth, nutrient transport, hormonal pathways and defence responses in leaves with or without Xoo infiltration to characterise the molecular pathways and plant genes affected by Pk interaction and related to ISR. [SOURCE: Page_105]

Material and Methods [SOURCE: Page_105]

Plant and bacterial material and growth Oryza sativa ssp. japonica cv. Nipponbare was chosen for this study and prepared as follows. Rice seeds were dehusked and surface-disinfected by immersion in 70% ethanol for 3 min, then in 3.8% sodium hypochlorite supplemented with 1% Tween 20 under agitation (180 rpm) for 30 min. Seeds were rinsed three times with sterile water, three times with 2% filtered sodium thiosulfate and three more times with sterile water. They were incubated overnight at 28°C in sterile water in the dark and 100 µL of the last rinse and imbibition water were plated on tryptic soy agar (Sigma-Aldrich) Petri dishes to confirm surface disinfection. After the imbibition step, four rice seedlings were placed onto a sterile system composed of 1,3 L pots containing a 50:50 sterile mix of sand and sieved perlite, placed onto a water recovery cup and closed into a Sunbag transparent filtered bag. Plants were left to grow in a growth chamber (12 h day/night, 28 °C day, 26 °C night, 75% humidity). Silica- supplemented Hoagland’s solution (recipe in Supplementary Table 1) was provided sterilely as needed throughout rice growth (without flooding the substrate). This system enabled rice to grow in sterile conditions until four weeks after sowing, where plants were too high to be contained in bags, which had to be opened. [SOURCE: Page_105]

86 [SOURCE: Page_105]

Wild-type Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 (ABIP1108, Pk) bacterial cells were transformed by electroporation with the pIN29 plasmid, comprising a chloramphenicol (Cm) resistance gene as well as the DsRed gene under the control of a constitutive TAC promoter (Vergunst et al., 2010), as explained in King et al., 2019. Both the wild-type and DsRed Pk strains were cultured as follows: they were plated onto modified Luria broth with less salt (LBm), supplemented for the tagged strain with chloramphenicol (200 µg. mL-1, LBm Cm^200) from glycerol stocks (20%, -80 °C) and incubated at 28 °C for 72 h. Liquid 20 mL cultures in LBm (supplemented if needed with Cm^100) were inoculated in 50 mL falcon tubes, incubated at 28 °C under agitation (180 rpm) overnight the day before the inoculation. 1 mL of this overnight culture was used to inoculate a fresh liquid medium and left to grow until DO = 1 (“liquid culture”). In the meantime, colonies from the petri dish used for the liquid culture were scraped off with 2 mL of sterile water, then diluted to 20 mL in a 50 mL falcon tube (“solid media”). Bacterial cells from both types of cultures were then centrifuged for 10 min at 2,500 g and resuspended in sterile water. [SOURCE: Page_106]

Rice root colonisation patterns and intensities To assess the long-term colonisation rate and pattern of Pk, each plantlet was inoculated with 108 bacterial cells of DsRed-tagged P. kururiensis M130 (ABIP2202) 4 days after sowing, either with the petri-dish culture (“solid culture”) or the liquid preculture (“liquid culture”). They were grown in a growth chamber for several weeks. Rice roots were harvested at 3, 7, 14, 21, 28- and 35-days post-inoculation (dpi). Each root system was transferred in a 2 ml screwed-tube containing 1 mL of sterile water and one sterile ceramic bead. Each tube was weighted and grinded using a FastPrep 24TM 5G (MP Biomedicals) at 6 m/s for 40 s. The suspension was then serial-diluted and inoculated in LBm Cm^200 then incubated at 28 °C for 48 h. Colony-forming units (CFU) were enumerated. The root-associated total bacterial population was measured on five rice root systems. Microscope observations of Pk colonisation on rice roots were performed on the primary root to compare colonisation patterns on the same root tissue. Primary roots were harvested at the same time points as CFU counting and mounted between slide and slips cover and directly observed using a Nikon Eclipse Ni-E epifluorescence microscope (Montpellier Ressource Imagerie (MRI) platform). [SOURCE: Page_106]

. [SOURCE: Page_106]

Rice biomass, yield and Xoo tolerance phenotypes assays Rice seedlings were prepared and planted as described in the sterile system. Wild-type strain of Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 was prepared as described above, with both petri-dish and liquid cultures. Plantlets were inoculated at 4 days post sowing, under 5 conditions. Control plants were inoculated with sterile water. Two other control conditions were added, with autoclaved solid or liquid culture, to check if the sole presence of bacterial cells affects rice growth and defence responses. Solid and liquid cultures were prepared to inoculate 108 bacterial cells per plantlet. After nine weeks of growing, infection of Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99 (Xoo) was carried out as explained in Niño-Liu et al., 2005 (n ≥ 9). Chlorosis and necrosis symptoms were recorded 14 days later. Mock inoculations were made with sterile water to assess that the sole clipping of the leaf doesn’t induce any disease symptoms. After 14 weeks of growth, the plants began to flower. At 16 weeks of growth, when the grains were ripe, watering was stopped to allow the plants to dry. At 18 weeks after sowing, the plants were harvested and the number of in-grains panicles, their dry weight and the total dry weight of the plants were recorded after a week of drying at 40 °C. [SOURCE: Page_108]

Transcriptomic responses of rice leaves to root inoculation with Pk and Xoo-infiltration on leaves Rice seeds were sown after surface-sterilisation and germination into anti-coiling pots containing a sterile inert substrate composed of 7 volumes of sand, 2 volumes of sieved perlite and 1 volume of vermiculite. They were inoculated as described earlier, at a rate of 108 bacterial cells per plantlet. After eight weeks of growth (watered as necessary with a modified Hoagland’s solution whose composition is available in Supplementary Table 1), samples of the last fully developed leaf, in a healthy state or 24 h after an infiltration with a suspension of Xoo (with a syringe pressed against the leaf) from control and Pk plants were taken and snap- frozen into liquid nitrogen before storage at -80 °C. RNA was extracted from leaf samples collected and prepared for RNAseq and qPCR analyses. They were first snap-frozen into liquid nitrogen and then cold-ground to a fine powder using a TissueLyser II (Retsch) at 30 Hz, for 15 s twice. Each biological replicate consisted of three leaf samples, with three biological replicates for each condition. Total RNA was extracted using TriReagent (Sigma) and a DNAse (QIAgen) treatment was added in the protocol before purification with the RNA Clean & Concentrator kit (Zymo), according to the manufacturer’s instructions. Quantity and quality of total RNA was assessed using a Nanodrop^1000 spectrophotometer (ThermoFischer). [SOURCE: Page_108]

Approximately 120 ng of total RNA from each biological replicate was used for retrotranscription into cDNA using SuperScript III Reverse Transcriptase (Thermo Fisher Scientific). cDNAs were diluted 6-fold and RT-qPCRs were performed using the Takyon™ Low ROX SYBR 2X MasterMix blue dTTP (Eurogentec) on a LightCycler 480 qPCR system (Roche). Plate preparation was automated using the epMotion 5070 pipetting robot (Eppendorf). Four independent biological replicates were analysed for each condition, each one analysed in triplicate. Relative gene expression is calculated by comparing each sample to the standard’s (number of cycles of EF1a, ΔCt), and then to the control group (Pk vs Ct, ΔΔCt, with or without Xoo inoculation). Fold change is calculated with 2-meanΔΔCt and the logFC represents the relative expression of each marker gene as indicated in Pfaffl, 2001. The list of marker genes, their function and the primers used in this study are listed in Table 1. [SOURCE: Page_110]

Statistical analyses Statistical analyses were performed using Rstudio (version 2022.2.0.443) and R (version 4.1.3) software with the packages "readxl", "tidyverse", "ggplot^2", "rstatix", "ggpubr", "multicompView", "car", "pcr", "pheatmap". As the phenotypic data of rice long-term growth met the hypotheses of normality (Shapiro test, p > 0.05) and homoscedasticity (Levene test, p > 0.05), one-way ANOVA with Tukey test (α = 0.05) was used for pairwise group comparisons. For biocontrol traits, these data did not fulfil the normality and homoscedasticity hypotheses, so non-parametric ANOVA with Kruskal-Wallis test (α = 0.05) followed by pairwise comparisons with Wilcoxon tests (α = 0.05) were used for mean group comparisons of measured phenotypic traits. The "pcr" package was used to analyse the expression of rice marker genes. Student T-tests were used to assess statistical differences between bacterial inoculation and marker gene expression. To visualise the relative expression of each marker gene as a function of rice genotype and AMF inoculation, a heat map of LogFC was generated using the "pheatmap" package. [SOURCE: Page_110]
