Results [SOURCE: Page_112]

Solid versus liquid inoculum effect on Pk rice root colonisation Colonisation rates and patterns of DsRed-tagged Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 are visible in Figure 1 and Table 2. To assess how and to what extent rice is colonised by P. kururiensis M130, the quantification and qualification of the colonisation pattern of a DsRed-fluorescent tagged Pk was conducted, for more than a month after inoculation. The two inoculation types were investigated, to see if the culture type before inoculation influences the viability and colonisation capacity, hence its interaction with its host, of the strain. Pk was able to colonise rice roots efficiently at a level always higher to 105 bacterial cells per mg of roots (Figure 1A). Mean CFU per mass of roots grows gradually for both conditions, without being statistically different at any time point between both inoculation methods (t.test p-value < 0.05, Table 2). In terms of rice roots colonisation, events of intercellular colonisation were seen as early as at 3 days post-inoculation, colonisation of rice roots hair at 7 days post-inoculation, whereas events where plant cells were colonised appeared at 14 days post-inoculation, in both liquid or solid-media inoculated rice roots (Supplementary Figure 1 & 2). Highest CFU values are reached at 21 days post-inoculation for the liquid pre-culture method and at 28 days post-inoculation for the inoculum made from the solid media (1.41.106 and 2.40.106 bacterial cells per mg of roots, respectively, Table 2). A high intensity of colonisation is maintained 21 days after inoculation, and a decrease at the smallest level of CFU is attained at 35 days post-inoculation (1.22.105 and 1.45.105 bacterial cells per mg of roots for the petri dish inoculation type and the liquid inoculum respectively). [SOURCE: Page_112]

Phenotypic rice response to Pk root interaction on growth, yield and Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae infection traits Results about the effects of root inoculation by Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 on four months-old rice growth, yield and symptoms (chlorosis or necrosis induced by the infection) spread of Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99 are visible in Figure 2 and Table 3 and 4. At 14 days post-clipping, chlorosis spread induced by the leaf-clipping of Xoo is significantly reduced by 56,8% when rice is inoculated via bacteria recovered directly from the petri dish, whereas every other inoculation method (dead cells and liquid preculture) have no effect on this symptom (Figure 2A, Table 4). Necrosis spread isn’t affected by any of the Pk inoculation methods (Figure 2B, Table 4). There is no significant effect of Pk inoculation on rice’s panicles or total shoot dry weight, independently of the inoculation method used (Figure 2 C & D, Table 3). [SOURCE: Page_112]

Molecular systemic rice responses to Pk in a healthy state or after Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae infiltration The phenotypic observations that Pk doesn't significantly induce the growth of Nipponbare, while reducing the symptoms induced by Xoo infection (Figure 2), lead to the hypothesis that Pk inoculation has more of an impact on rice physiology. The systemic molecular responses induced by its interaction with Pk were then investigated. We selected 23 marker genes related to rice development, phytohormone balance, mineral nutrition, defence and interaction with microorganisms. Their expression in leaves was normalised to that of the EF1a reference gene. The list of selected leaf marker genes, their function and the primers used in this study are presented in Table 1. The heatmap of their relative expression in leaves of plants in interaction with Paraburkholderia kururiensis, in a healthy state or after 24 h of infiltration with Xoo is visible in Figure 3. Absolute levels of expression compared to the respective control (non-Pk inoculated plants) are shown in Supplementary Figures 3, 4 & 5 (Pk in a healthy state, upon Xoo infection, Xoo induced). The transcription factor OsWRKY$_7$ was recently reported to be involved in Pyricularia oryzae resistance in rice (Tun et al., 2023), while OsSHR$_5$, a homolog of the sugarcane LRR-RLK gene involved in the endophytic association of plant N$_2$ fixation, is differentially expressed in plants inoculated with beneficial Burkholderia (King et al., 2019). Both were selected to assess their expression in healthy or Xoo-infiltrated leaves of control or Pk-inoculated leaves. OsWRKY$_7$ expression is significantly repressed in healthy rice leaves associated with Pk and isn't significantly modulated by Xoo infection (Figure 3). An opposite behaviour is observed for OsSHR$_5$, although it is not significantly repressed in Xoo-inoculated Pk leaves. In Xoo infected controls, this gene expression is significantly induced (Figure 3, Supplementary Figures 3, 4 & 5). The expressions of two development-related genes, OsYABBY$_6$, responsible for abaxial-adaxial polarity and leaf cell growth (Jiang et al., 2016), and OsXTH17, a xyloglucan endotransglucosylase hydrolase involved in primary cell wall formation (Lin et al., 2019), were recorded. In healthy Pk inoculated rice, their expression doesn't differ from that of control plants (Figure 3, Supplementary Figure 3). When plants are inoculated with a Xoo, the expression of both cell growth-related genes is reduced in Pk inoculated plants, but only significantly for OsYABBY$_6$. In Xoo diseased leaves of control rice, an opposite modulation of these development-related genes is to be noted (up-regulation of OsYABBY$_6$, down- regulation of OsXTH17 expressions) (Figure 3, Supplementary Figure 5). To understand the effect of Pk inoculation on mineral homeostasis in healthy or diseased rice plants, a nitrate reductase (OsNIA$_1$), an iron transporter (OsIRO$_2$) and three Pi transporters, marker of the Pi starvation response (OsMGD$_2$, OsPAP23 and OsSPX$_3$), were selected (Tang et al., 2012, Ogo et al., 2006, Secco et al., 2013). In healthy Pk rice leaves, [SOURCE: Page_114]

the expression of all the above-mentioned genes was repressed (significant for OsMGD$_2$, OsPAP23), except for an induction of OsIRO$_2$ expression (not statistically significant, Figure 3). These trends are repeated in Xoo-infiltrated leaves, although to a lesser extent (Supplementary Figure 4). In Xoo-infected leaves of control plants, all of these marker genes expression is significantly induced (not significant for OsIRO$_2$) (Figure 3, Supplementary Figure 5). Since phytohormones such as jasmonate (JA), salicylate (SA) and ethylene (ET) are widely reported to be modulated by microorganism’s mere presence or interaction with their host, we selected marker genes whose products are involved in these balances: OsAOS$_1$, responsible for JA biosynthesis, OsJAZ$_6$, OsMYC$_2$ and OsJamyb, involved in JA signalling (and with Xoo resistance, Yokotani et al., 2012, Lu et al., 2016, King et al, 2019, Yang et al., 2019), OsACS$_1$, the 1-aminocyclopropane-1-carboxylate synthase responsible for ET biosynthesis (Petitot et al., 2017), and OsNPR$_1$ and OsWRKY45, mediating SA biosynthesis and signalling respectively (Kumari et al., 2016). Under our conditions, the expression of SA- and ET-related genes wasn't significantly modulated by either Pk inoculation or Xoo infiltration (Figure 3, Supplementary Figures 3 & 4). On the other hand, the expression of OsJAZ$_6$ and OsMYC$_2$ was significantly repressed in the leaves of healthy Pk-inoculated plants (OsJamyb and OsAOS$_1$ were not statistically significantly repressed, Figure 3). All phytohormones related genes expressions were significantly induced by the infection of Xoo in control leaves (Figure 3, Supplementary Figure 5) with the exception of OsJamyb which was down-regulated. To assess how Pk root inoculation affects the defence response in healthy or Xoo- infected leaves, the expression of several defence-related genes was examined. OsPR1a, OsPBZ$_1$ and OsPR$_5$ are pathogenesis-related proteins, while OsPAL$_4$ and OsDXS$_3$ are responsible for a broad-spectrum disease resistance response and phytoalexin production in rice, respectively (Deiteil et al., 2012, Petitot et al., 2017, Okada et al., 2009, Valette et al., 2020). OsWRKY30 and OsMPK10 are responsible for early disease-mediated signalling (Ryu et al., 2006, Nguyen et al., 2014). In both healthy and Xoo-inoculated leaves, Pk inoculation didn't induce any significant modulation of these defence marker genes, except for a repression of OsMPK10 expression in Xoo-inoculated leaves (Figure 3). In diseased control leaves, the expression of early disease-mediated signalling genes was modulated (up- regulation of OsWRKY30 and down-regulation of OsMPK10), the expression of broad- spectrum disease resistance response and phytoalexin production was induced, as well as the expression of OsPBZ$_1$ (Figure 3, Supplementary Figures 3, 4 & 5). [SOURCE: Page_116]

97 [SOURCE: Page_116]

Discussion In this chapter, the effect of long-term interaction of a model japonica rice cultivar, Nipponbare, with a native endophytic Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 (Pk) on both phenotypic and molecular traits was investigated. Two inoculum preparation methods were tested on the roots of rice seedlings, with Pk grown on solid media only or after liquid culture. As controls, both types of inoculant were autoclaved before inoculation and one control condition was treated with sterile water only. Looking at the colonisation rate of Pk at multiple time points, no significant difference in viable cell counts was observed between the two types of inoculation, except for a slight increase with solid-inoculated Pk cells compared to liquid- inoculated at 28 days post-inoculation (Figure 1, Table 2). In terms of the phenotypic response of rice to the two types of inoculation, Nipponbare growth (total shoot dry weight per plant) and yield (panicle dry weight per plant) were not significantly affected by Pk inoculation, regardless of the type of inoculation (Figure 2 C & D, Table 3). However, only Pk inoculation from a solid medium has a significant effect on the reduction of chlorosis symptoms induced by Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae (Xoo) (Figure 2A, Table 4). [SOURCE: Page_117]

Effect of the type of inoculation on rice responses to a PGPR The main hypothesis for these results is that the type of culture used has an effect on bacterial metabolism and eventually on their plant-growth promoting activities. The composition and nutrient acquisition modes are different between agitated liquid media and immobile solid media: nutrient dissolution and accessibility is much easier in an agitated culture broth, while bacteria are forced to move around the immobile solid medium to access them, but at a higher rate of development than in broth cells (Bonnet et al., 2019). There are several earlier reports that bacterial growth on surfaces has an effect on their metabolism, depending on the bacteria studied (induced or decreased growth rate, activity and respiration, decrease in mortality) (van Loosdrecht et al., 1990). A study of Bradyrhizobium japonica cell wall rearrangement in two types of media showed that bacteria grown on solid media for 24 hours showed cell wall rearrangements, while cells cultured in broth for a further four hours showed higher metabolic activities (higher amounts of cytosolic ribosomes) with cell wall rearrangements, suggesting that the change in media induced both morphological and physiological changes in Bradyrhizobium cells (Zeroual et al., 1994). These modifications are reported to have an effect on the plant growth promoting activities of the bacteria, both in terms of biological nitrogen fixation (BNF) and phosphate solubilising activities. In the search for legume-nodulating bacterial strains also capable of solubilising phosphate from different sources, in vitro experiments on GELP media showed that some strains were able to solubilise P more readily in liquid media than in its solid form, while others could only retrieve P from [SOURCE: Page_117]

98 [SOURCE: Page_117]

aluminium-bound complexes (Marra et al., 2011 & 2012). These differences in PGP capabilities may explain why Pk grown in solid media induces systemic resistance in rice leaves, but not Pk grown in liquid media. Its metabolism and in particular the metabolites produced could be studied in both media types. Furthermore, solid bioinoculants have been reported to have a longer shelf life than liquid- formulated ones under the same conditions, while their bacterial population has been shown to be more persistent in the soil and to induce a more reliable increase in crop yield from one year to another compared to the liquid ones (Bashan et al., 2014, Berger et al., 2018, Basiru et al., 2021, Mitter et al., 2021). In our specific case, a solid bioinoculant based on solid-grown Paraburkholderia kururiensis could be an interesting new formulation for biological control purposes. [SOURCE: Page_118]

Paraburkholderia kururiensis plant-growth-promoting abilities depends on the environmental context Under our conditions, inoculation of Nipponbare roots with the endophyte Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 didn't have a significant direct positive effect on rice shoot biomass or total panicle weight (Figure 2 C & D). King et al, 2023, while investigating the inoculation effect of three Burkholderia s.l., found that this strain was responsible for a slight but significant induction of Nipponbare grain number under greenhouse conditions. Previous reports have highlighted the potential for biostimulation of this bacteria, on multiple hosts. Endophytic colonisation of Brazilian rice Guarani by Pk strongly increased total dry weight and nitrogen content, fresh root and shoot biomass, as well as the number and production of inflorescences (Baldani et al., 2000, Mattos et al., 2008). A chickpea-derived nodulating bacterial strain, phylogenetically close to Pk, showed clear increases in shoot and grain biomass in five different chickpea cultivars (Gopalakrishnan et al., 2018). Mattos et al., 2008 proposed that Pk produces indole-3-acetic acid, as the activity of its plant reporter gene (DR$_5$- GUS) was induced upon inoculation and the number of lateral roots and root hairs increased in rice roots soaked in Pk supernatant. Analysis of the Paraburkholderia kururiensis KP23 and M130 strains genomes revealed several potential additional PGP activities, such as nitrogen fixation, ACC deaminase (ethylene modulation), degradation of toxic aromatic compounds (abiotic    stress   tolerance),   production   of   antimicrobial   substances   (putrescine,   4- hydroxybenzoate) and protein secretion systems (type III, IV, V and VI, thought to be involved in the establishment of endophytism) (Dias et al., 2019). In the presence of rice exudates, the expression of membrane transporters, secretion systems, motility, chemotaxis and adhesion are induced in P. kururiensis (Coutinho et al., 2015). Furthermore, transposon sequencing analysis highlighted the need for functional motility, chemotaxis, quorum sensing as well as [SOURCE: Page_118]

99 [SOURCE: Page_118]

glycolysis, antimicrobial production and osmoprotection pathways in Pk, suggesting their role in rice endophytic colonisation (Wallner et al., 2022). The same study highlighted the differences in Pk colonisation of japonica and indica rice, both in terms of bacterial quantification in roots and genes essential for their colonisation. Despite the potential benefits of this endophytic interaction, our results are concordant with Vargas et al., 2012, who didn't find a clear growth improvement in two indica rice cultivars (IR42 and IAC4440) at early time points. On two different African rice cultivars, a Pk strain from Burkina Faso increased (Orylux 6) or decreased (TS$_2$) rice growth after two weeks of interaction, whereas M130 inoculation decreased growth in both cultivars (Sondo et al., 2023). Overall, as there isn't a clear understanding of Paraburkholderia kururiensis rice growth induction, its benefits must depend on the rice cultivar, its degree of adaptation to its hosts as well as the environmental conditions. [SOURCE: Page_119]

Biological control induced by Paraburkholderia kururiensis In our study, inoculation of rice roots with Paraburkholderia kururiensis reduced chlorosis symptoms caused by Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae infection (Figure 2A). To our knowledge, this is the first report of Pk having a clear systemic biological control effect in rice, triggering an ISR phenotype. Another bacteria of this genus, P. phytofirmans PsJN, was reported to induce resistance to Pseudomonas syringae DC3000 in Arabidopsis thaliana, with the plant showing reduced symptoms, reduced reactive oxygen species (ROS) accumulation and transcriptional changes in SA-, JA/ET-responsive defence genes (Timmermann et al., 2017 & 2019). With its ability to colonise grapevine stem by migration through the xylem, PsJN is also responsible for antibiosis against Allorhizobium vitis, triggering crown gall in Vitis vinifera stems (Trong et al., 2022). In the same plant species, PsJN can completely control the development of Xyllela fastidiosa, even after disease establishment, through both antibiosis and the priming of grapevine defence responses, highlighting a strong potential in biological control of a large spectrum of diseases (Baccari et al., 2018). Another species, P. sabiae, has been reported to outcompete a wide range of phytopathogens in vitro (including Burkholderia plantarii, Ralstonia solanacearum, Pseudomonas syringae and Pectobacterium carotovorum) thanks to its type VI-1 secretion system (Hug et al., 2023). Such effects have now to be tested in planta. In the light of all these results, the induced mechanisms of the ability of Paraburkholderia kururiensis to reduce Xoo symptoms in rice should be investigated. Since its genome harbours a variety of potential biological control mechanisms, ranging from antibiosis systems to the induction of plant defence responses, the in planta mode of action [SOURCE: Page_119]

100 [SOURCE: Page_119]

should be investigated (Dias et al., 2019). In our study, we tried to evaluate the second type of mechanisms: does Pk induce ISR in rice leaves against Xoo? [SOURCE: Page_120]

Transcriptomic changes due to Parabukholderia kururiensis interaction and Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae infiltration To assess the transcriptomic changes induced by both bacterial inoculations, two comparisons were designed. Eight-week-old rice leaves were sampled with and without prior root inoculation with Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 (healthy state), with and without prior 24-hour infiltration with Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99 (diseased state). The results are shown in Figure 3, which highlights the overall effect of Pk in a healthy state, a Xoo- diseased state, compared to Pk-untreated plants. Overall, infection with Xoo strongly modulated the expression of all marker genes in leaves of Pk-untreated rice. The most striking are the up-regulation of all nutrient transporters and the strong modulation of the expression of jasmonate-, ethylene-, salicylic acid- and defence-related genes. Our results are consistent with previous work on rice response to Xoo infection at the same time point: KEGG pathways related to photosynthesis, plant hormones biosyntheses and ribosomes were also modulated (Wang et al., 2019). Grewal et al, 2012, noted that susceptible rice tended to arrest growth to upregulate the expression of its defence- related genes, whereas resistant rice triggered the induction of recognition pathways (MAPK signalling and surface receptors), and abscisic acid-, jasmonate- and ethylene-related responses. Under our conditions, a modulation of the expression of OsYABBY$_6$ and OsXTH17 was clearly visible, while an up-regulation of the expression of an early disease-mediated signalling (OsWRKY30), the expression of the broad-spectrum disease resistance response (OsPAL$_4$) and phytoalexin biosynthesis (OsDXS$_3$), as well as the expression of OsPBZ$_1$ was associated with a strong induction of the defence response (Figure 3, Supplementary Figure 5). However, OsAOS$_1$, OsACS$_1$ and OsNPR$_1$, all closely related to JA, ET and SA biosynthesis, were significantly up-regulated in 24h diseased leaves, as well as OsMYC$_2$ and OsJAZ$_6$, which were reported to be induced in resistance to Xoo (Yang et al., 2019). All these results point to an induced defence response against Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae in a susceptible rice cultivar (as we observe clear Xoo symptoms 14 days after inoculation). Looking at the effect of Paraburkholderia kururiensis on rice marker genes of physiological pathways in a healthy state, fewer significant differentially expressed genes were visible. OsWRKY$_7$, OsMGD$_2$ and OsPAP23 as well as OsJAZ$_6$ and OsMYC$_2$ were all significantly repressed. The expression of all other marker genes was either not significantly different or too weakly affected (Supplementary Figure 3). The OsWRKY$_7$ transcription factor was found to be induced in rice infected with Pyricularia oryzae (Tun et al., 2023), whereas [SOURCE: Page_120]

101 [SOURCE: Page_120]

the exact opposite was seen in rice leaves inoculated with a beneficial bacterium. OsMGD$_2$ and OsPAP23 were reported to be induced in rice under inorganic phosphate starvation, recruiting transporters and translocators more actively. However, the opposite was observed in leaves inoculated with Pk. Some Burkholderia s. l. have phosphate solubilising abilities, such as Paraburkholderia tropica, which enhances wheat, sorghum and tomato, and Burkholderia sp. AQ12, which enhances rice growth (Estrada-de-los-Santos et al., 2016, Kuramae et al., 2020, Maqsood et al., 2021). Rasul et al., 2019 founded three strains of phosphate-solubilising bacteria and IAA producers capable of inhibiting Xoo disease under in vitro (antibiosis) and net house conditions. It may be interesting to evaluate the phosphate solubilising ability of Paraburkholderia kururiensis, even though it wasn't the target of the comparative Paraburkholderia genomics done by Dias et al., 2019. Furthermore, the only phytohormone significantly affected by Pk inoculation in the long term was jasmonate signalling, with suppression of OsJAZ$_6$ and OsMYC$_2$ responsible for inducing Xoo resistance (Yang et al., 2019). However, these two genes weren't significantly induced upon infiltration with Xoo (Figure 3, Supplementary Figure 4). OsJAZ$_6$ expression was still repressed, as was leaf cell growth related OsYABBY$_6$ and early disease signalling OsMPK10 expression. This was quite surprising as our main hypothesis for the reduction of Xoo symptoms was an ISR signalling, i.e. a priming of JA/ET and defence related responses. Our study only focuses on one time point. It cannot be excluded that ISR priming occured, but at a different time point that we have chosen. 24h post inoculation was chosen because previous studies found some interesting effects of Xoo inoculation on rice responses at this timepoint (Wang et al., 2019). However, some others reported significant modulation of rice leaf transcriptomics as early as one-hour post-inoculation (Grewal et al., 2012, Timmermann et al., 2017). A time course experiment with rhizobacteria such as P. kururiensis root inoculation after one hour, six hours, twelve hours, etc. of Xoo infiltration could be interesting to assess the kinetics of ISR (if the reduction of Xoo symptoms is mediated by modulation of the leaf transcriptome). On the other hand, since nutrient-starved transporters weren't significantly induced under Pk conditions, the better tolerance to biotic stress may be due to an overall better health status of the rice plants. [SOURCE: Page_121]

102 [SOURCE: Page_121]

Conclusion In this first experimental chapter, the phenotypic and molecular responses of Nipponbare to the interaction with the beneficial endophyte Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 were investigated. The aim of this study was to demonstrate the benefits provided to rice growth by this bacterium and to describe precisely the Induced Systemic Resistance (ISR) caused after secondary infection by a phytopathogenic bacterium, Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99 (Xoo). We tested if the mode of bacterial culture impacted rice colonisation, growth and tolerance to a phytopathogen. Rice root colonisation was efficient (colonising inter- and intracellular spaces, at more than 105 bacteria per mg of root at least) at all selected timepoints, whether Pk was cultured on a solid media or in a liquid culture. This indicate that the bacteria establish itself in the rice root system even after 35 days of growth, independently of the inoculation type. After four months of growth, rice growth and yield parameters weren’t influenced by Pk inoculation. However, solid media-cultured Pk root inoculation reduced the chlorosis extent of rice leaves infected with Xoo, highlighting the potential of this strain to trigger ISR in rice leaves. The typical molecular pathways induced by this immunity responses (defence, jasmonate- and ethylene- related pathways) weren’t significantly affected by Pk inoculation on Xoo-challenged plants. Thus, the reduction in chlorosis induced by the presence of Pk may have occurred at a different time of infection by Xoo, or may not have affected the selected marker genes. An RNAseq analysis, currently being sequenced, should broaden our understanding of the mechanisms involved. [SOURCE: Page_122]

Complementary                  results:        Pk-induced             rice       phenotype modifications depends on the growth substrate In this chapter, the study of rice response to a root inoculation with the native endophyte Paraburkholderia kururiensis on phenotypic and molecular traits was investigated. The substrate used was either a 50:50 mix of sand and sieved perlite, either a 70:20:10 mix of sand, perlite and vermiculite in rather small pots. Either way, the substrate was inert to ensure that potential visible effects were only due to Pk inoculation. I conducted a study on rice response to the same bacterium under greenhouse conditions, in potting soil in 2 L pots (composition of the potting soil in Complementary Table 1.1). I wanted to check if the visible phenotypic traits in poor nutrient bioavailability conditions were still visible when Pk was inoculated in the soil microbiota. After six weeks of growth, Xanthomonas oryzae pv oryzae was infected on the last fully-grown leaf and chlorosis symptoms were recorded after 14 days. The harvest occurred at eleven weeks of growth, height of shoot and roots were recorded, and their dry weight after one week of drying at 40 °C. Results of rice growth and Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99 symptoms spread on their leaves are visible in the following Complementary Figure 1.1. There is no significant effect of Pk inoculation on rice growth (total dry weight per plant) or tolerance to Xoo infection (chlorosis spread) when rice is cultured under greenhouse and potting soil conditions. These results put in light the fact that bacterial inoculation in more complex soil and microbiota conditions doesn’t always result in bioinoculant establishment and functioning, as can be seen for multiple single strain inoculations (Mazzola & Freilich, 2017, King et al., 2023). The biological control potential of Paraburkholderia kururiensis on rice infected by the phytopathogenic bacteria Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99 is probably also influenced by environmental conditions. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Chapter 3: Rice transcriptomic responses to a variety of rhizobacteria leads to identification of new marker genes of PGPR interaction [SOURCE: Page_138]

Ludivine GUIGARD$_1$, Aurore COMTE$_1$, Nicolas BUSSET$_1$, Marine CAZZANIGA$_1$, Hans RIOS$_1$, Lionel MOULIN$_1$, Pierre CZERNIC$_1$ [SOURCE: Page_138]

1 PHIM Plant Health Institute, Univ Montpellier, CIRAD, INRAE, Institut Agro, IRD, Montpellier, France [SOURCE: Page_138]

Ce troisième chapitre est rédigé sous la forme d’article scientifique, en anglais. Il porte sur l’étude des réponses moléculaires du riz, communes ou spécifiques, à une grande diversité de bactéries. Celles-ci ont été sélectionnées suivant un gradient d’adaptation aux plantes et au riz, pour essayer de déceler des signatures spécifiques du riz, locales ou systémiques, à la présence de bactéries non-adaptées (Escherichia coli et Stenotrophomonas maltophilia), de bactéries adaptées à la colonisation racinaire de plantes (Pseudomonas defensor et Bradyrhizobium sp.), ou de bactéries isolées de racines de riz (Azospirillum sp et Burkholderia plantarii). En résultats complémentaires de ce chapitre se trouve l’étude des réponses phénotypiques du riz à l’inoculation de chacune des bactéries testées précédemment, sur des paramètres de croissance, de rendement et de tolérance à un stress biotique. [SOURCE: Page_138]

119 [SOURCE: Page_138]

Résumé du chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_139]

Dans ce troisième chapitre de thèse, l’étude des réponses phénotypiques et moléculaires du riz à l’inoculation racinaire par une diversité de bactéries (PGPR et non PGPR) a été réalisée avec plusieurs angles d’approche. L’étude des interactions du riz avec son environnement et les différentes bactéries qui composent son microbiote racinaire a permis de mettre en lumière des taxons bactériens bénéfiques pour sa croissance (biostimulants) ou sa tolérance à des agents phytopathogènes divers (contrôle biologique) (Timmermann et al., 2017, Backer et al., 2018). Au sens large, ils sont nommés « rhizobactéries améliorant la croissance des plantes » (PGPR). Cependant, PGPR comme phytopathogènes induisent       des réactions moléculaires de défense lors              de leur reconnaissance (PTI) (Jones & Dangl, 2006, Stringlis et al., 2018, Teixeira et al., 2021). Il existe des lacunes dans les connaissances sur la manière dont les plantes différencient les PGPR des phytopathogènes et sur la manière dont elles choisissent de tolérer ce type d’interaction mutualiste ou de s’immuniser contre une infection. En cela, il y a peu d’études disponibles sur la caractérisation de la réponse moléculaire du riz à une interaction avec des bactéries provenant de différentes niches écologiques et présentant des comportements de colonisation différents. [SOURCE: Page_139]

Nous avons voulu caractériser la réponse du riz à une diversité de bactéries suivant un gradient d’adaptation connu aux racines du riz Nipponbare. Nous avons sélectionné deux bactéries « non-adaptées aux plantes » que sont Escherichia coli DH5α (souche de laboratoire) et Stenotrophomonas maltophilia ABIP44 (Nautiyal et al., 2010, Berg et al., 1999). Nous avons choisi deux bactéries « adaptées aux plantes », dont Pseudomonas defensor WCS374r isolé de la pomme de terre induisant une réponse de défense systémique chez le riz contre un pathogène fongique (de Vleesschauwer et al., 2008) et Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278, isolé de la légumineuse tropicale Aeschynomenae en bordure de rizière et induisant la croissance du riz (Chaintreuil et al., 2000). Enfin, nous avons sélectionné deux bactéries « isolées de riz », Azospirillum sp. B510, modèle PGPR étudié chez Nipponbare, et le pathogène des jeunes pousses de riz Burkholderia plantarii ATCC43733 (Isawa et al., 2010, Solis et al., 2006). Nous avons tout d’abord observé leur comportement de colonisation sur des racines de riz à 7 et 14 jours après inoculation. Les ratios DO-CFU ont été calculés pour chacune des bactéries inoculées. Toutes les bactéries sont capables de coloniser les racines de la variété Nipponbare (plus de 105 bactéries par mg de racine), avec des différences sur les plans qualitatifs comme quantitatifs. En résultats complémentaires de cet article, l’effet de leur inoculation simple a été exploré sur des aspects de croissance (hauteur des plantes et production de biomasse) et de tolérance à un stress biotique (infection par Xanthomonas [SOURCE: Page_139]

120 [SOURCE: Page_139]

oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99). Toutes les mesures ont été faites huit semaines après inoculation racinaire à 108 bactéries / plant. Les aspects de croissance et de rendement (biomasse totale produite, poids des grains et poids moyen d’un grain de riz) ont été effectuées à quatre mois après inoculation. Cette étude nous a permis de mesurer l’impact sur la croissance et la tolérance du riz à un stress biotique, induit par les PGPR choisis mais également par certaines bactéries non-adaptées a priori à la colonisation des plantes. [SOURCE: Page_140]

Une fois ces observations phénotypiques faites, nous avons voulu caractériser les réponses moléculaires communes et spécifiques du riz à une interaction bactérienne, quelle que soit sa niche écologique d’origine : adaptée aux plantes ou non, PGPR ou pathogène, isolée du riz ou non. Nous avons procédé à une analyse transcriptomique des racines et des feuilles de riz 7 jours après inoculation avec chaque type de bactérie. Le riz Nipponbare a pour cela été cultivé en conditions contrôlées, en culture hydroponique. Les plantes contrôles ont été inoculés avec de l’eau stérile. Cette analyse a révélé que, dans nos conditions de culture (hydroponie), le riz présente une réponse commune lorsqu'il est exposé à une inoculation bactérienne quel que soit son degré d’adaptation à la colonisation, ou à des bactéries non adaptées aux plantes. Cependant, cette réponse ne concerne qu'un petit nombre de gènes, rarement annotés. De plus, ces réponses sont beaucoup moins prononcées que celles observées lorsque le riz est en contact avec des bactéries adaptées aux plantes ou à des bactéries bénéfiques. Nos résultats soulignent la capacité du riz à répondre de manière spécifique aux bactéries qui l'entourent grâce à la complexité des interactions moléculaires impliquées. [SOURCE: Page_140]

La réponse commune aux PGPR choisies s’appuie sur une induction de l’expression des gènes liés à la croissance cellulaire et à la gestion de stress qu'il soit d'origine abiotique ou biotique, ainsi qu’à la diminution de l'expression des gènes liés au système immunitaire, tels que les récepteurs de reconnaissance biotique, les facteurs de transcription, les protéines de résistance et de pathogénicité, à la fois dans les racines et les feuilles. Nos résultats, bien que préliminaires, suggèrent que les PGPR modulent spécifiquement ces voies en influençant des mécanismes de signalisation particuliers, ce qui crée la réponse spécifique du riz que nous avons observé. Une étude cinétique des gènes impliqués par RNAseq sur plusieurs temps post-inoculation pourrait permettre de mieux décortiquer la complexité des réseaux de régulation sous-jacents. Cela pourrait être couplé avec une approche de mutagénèse ciblée des gènes marqueurs retrouvés dans nos analyses, afin de vérifier leur intérêt dans la réponse phénotypique (taux de colonisation et effet sur la croissance) et moléculaire (transcriptomique locale ou systémique) du riz muté. [SOURCE: Page_140]

121 [SOURCE: Page_140]

122 [SOURCE: Page_141]

Chapter 3: Rice transcriptomic responses to a variety of rhizobacteria leads to identification of new marker genes of PGPR interaction Ludivine GUIGARD$_1$, Aurore COMTE$_1$, Nicolas BUSSET$_1$, Marine CAZZANIGA$_1$, Hans RIOS$_1$, Lionel MOULIN$_1$, Pierre CZERNIC$_1$ [SOURCE: Page_142]

1 PHIM Plant Health Institute, Univ Montpellier, CIRAD, INRAE, Institut Agro, IRD, Montpellier, France [SOURCE: Page_142]

Introduction Nowadays, there is growing interest in the study of interactions between plants and their environment and more specifically, microorganisms living naturally in their vicinity. The current goals in agronomy, led by the upcoming effects of climate change, is to ensure worldwide food safety and constantly-growing production needs, as well as replacing pesticides and chemical inputs without any yield losses. A current hypothesis to reach these objectives relies on the benefits given by plants microbiomes and its engineering (Busby et al., 2017, Li et al., 2023). This cohort of naturally occurring microorganisms help sustain their plant’s growth in unfavourable conditions via their improvement of soil fertility by mobilising essential nutrients and organic matter decomposition and their direct enhancement of plant’s growth with their production of phytohormonal substances (Babalola et al., 2021, Berendsen et al., 2012). It can also impact the host’s health via its disease-suppressive capacity at the soil level, either by antibiosis, or direct competition with phytopathogens within the rhizosphere (Bakker et al., 2020). In this cohort of microorganisms interacting with the plants, plant growth promoting rhizobacteria (PGPR) were quickly considered. These beneficial bacteria enhance plants' health and growth through multiple well-studied mechanisms. Some bacteria such as Pseudomonas sp. induce a direct stimulation of growth with the production of phytohormones- like substances or volatile organic compounds in the rhizosphere (Vacheron et al., 2013, Backer et al., 2018). Nitrogen fixing bacteria such as Azospirillum sp. or Bradyrhizobium sp., or phosphate solubilizing bacteria such as Paraburkholderia tropica or Bacillus sp. can improve their host mineral nutrition with the increase of biodisponible nutrients in the roots’ vicinity (Baldani et al., 1986, Tian et al., 2021). PGPR can also increase their host tolerance to abiotic and biotic stresses, such as P. phytofirmans both inducing growth and enhancing salt stress tolerance in Arabidopsis thaliana and protecting it against a virulent strain of Pseudomonas syringae (Ledger et al., 2016, Timmermann et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_142]

123 [SOURCE: Page_142]

Although the effect of beneficial bacteria on plant growth and overall health has been extensively studied, there are still knowledge gaps on how plants differentiates beneficial from pathogenic microorganisms, triggering mutualism or immunity signals. The specific recognition of specific molecular patterns derived from microorganisms or created by harming the root cells during colonisation gives hints to the plant about which type of microorganisms tries to get through. Pathogen or more broadly, Microbe-Associated Molecular Patterns (PAMPs or MAMPs) derive from microorganisms in the proximity of root cells that could interact directly with the roots: flagellins, chitin, peptidoglycans and lipopolysaccharides (Henry et al., 2012, Zipfel et al., 2017). Damage-Associated Molecular Patterns (DAMPs), are produced when microorganisms harm cells during colonisation, such as oligogalacturonides produced by microbial enzymes or the extracellular release of ATP (Henry et al., 2012, Thoms et al., 2021). They are perceived at the neighbouring root cell surface with Pattern Recognition Receptors (PRRs), with high-affinity receptor-like kinases or proteines (RLK and RLP) (Buendia et al., 2018, Chen et al., 2021). When recognised, MAMPs trigger a localised defence response, with calcium influxes, reactive oxygen species accumulation in the cells, callose deposition and Mitogen-Activated Protein Kinases (MAPK) phosphorylation (Chuberre et al., 2018). Within minutes, this phenomenon will induce the phosphorylation of transcription factors of ethylene, salicylate and jasmonate, leading to the activation of the host defence responses (Nishad et al., 2020). Overall, this will lead to a Pathogen or Microorganisms - triggered immunity (PTI or MTI), controlling the spread of the infection by the considered microorganism (Chen et al., 2021). Some microorganisms produce specific effectors interfering with MTI and causing disease known as Effector Triggered Susceptibility (ETS). In resistant plants, these effectors are perceived thanks to resistance R proteins, with the most widely studied: nucleotide-binding leucine-rich repeats (NLR). There is growing evidence explaining that NLR’s complexity due to their numerous ramification types is a part of plants' natural arms-race against pathogens (Kourelis et al., 2021). This recognition will lead to the effector-triggered immunity (ETI) and enable the control of the microorganism’s growth and colonisation of root cells (Nishad et al., 2020). MAMPs originate from either beneficial, commensal or pathogenic types of microorganisms, meaning that all types of microorganisms can trigger MTI (Jones & Dangl, 2006, Stringlis et al., 2018, Teixeira et al., 2021). Beneficial microorganisms have developed mechanisms to avoid this costly immune response of their host. Some PGPR have evolved specific MAMPs, divergent from the pathogenic ones; that’s the case for rhizobia having evolved non-immunogenic flagellins (Zipfel et al., 2017, Yu et al., 2019a). Microorganisms can also interfere with their host immune signalling; the production of fungal sRNA targets and induces degradation of host defence genes mRNA (Chen et al., 2021). This is also found in [SOURCE: Page_143]

124 [SOURCE: Page_143]

interaction with commensals such as Dyella japonica and the beneficial bacteria Pseudomonas simiae, suppressing root growth inhibition and flagellin 22-induced MTI in Arabidopsis with the use of Type 2 Secretion system (T2SS) and the production of gluconic acid (Yu et al., 2019a & b, Teixeira et al., 2021). The down-regulation of the host defence responses is necessary for rhizobial and arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi symbiosis, presumably following the recognition of immunity signals such as Myc and Nod lipochitooligosaccharides (LCO) (Zipfel et al., 2017). In rice, a recent study showed how receptor competition between OsCEBiP and OsMYR$_1$ for the binding of OsCERK$_1$ is at the origin of the determination between symbiosis or immunity (Zhang et al., 2021). Upon recognition, the combination of multiple signalling pathways linked with either symbiosis or immunity responses seems to be needed, since there is a large degree of overlap in the pathways regulated by pathogens, commensals or beneficials (ROS, ethylene, jasmonate and salicylate, PR proteins) (Plett & Martin, 2018). Being the staple food of more than half of the world’s population, rice is one of the most important crops produced in the world, with a major role in Asia’s export earnings (Prasad et al., 2017). Rice fields are subjected to phytopathogens infection, throughout the growing season, including Pyricularia oryzae, Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae or Meloidogyne graminicola that have the potential to decimate whole fields if not treated (Liu & Wang, 2016). Since the molecular responses of rice to different types of phytopathogens is well- defined nowadays (Güimil et al., 2005, Liu & Wang, 2016), there is growing interest in its molecular responses to beneficial microorganisms and more particularly, PGPR. Multiple studies have found out that rice responds to a variety of selected rhizobacteria, sometimes with signalling pathways differing from the Common Symbiosis Signalling Pathway (CSSP) (Chen et al., 2015). Studies have focused on rice’s interaction with a single bacterial strain, such as members of the genera Herbaspirillum, Azospirillum, Pseudomonas, Burkholderia sensu lato (s.l.) or Azoarcus. These types of responses are generally linked with a modulation of phytohormones and repression of defence responses, with specific responses depending on the genus: Herbaspirillum modulating iron homeostasis (Brusamarello-Santos et al., 2019), Azospirillum and Paraburkholderia phytofirmans (PsJN) inducing hydroxycinnamic acid derivatives in root exudates and modulating jasmonate (Valette et al., 2020 a&b, King et al., 2023) and P. putida inducing the release of 1-amino-1-carboxylic acid (ACC) (Wang et al., 2020). Relatively few studies have extended their experimental questions and set-up to multiple strains, to understand if there are general and specific responses of rice to multiple bacterial inoculations, and which pathways are affected. Drogue et al. (2014) studying rice root transcriptomic responses between two rice genotypes and two Azospirillum strains already showed that common and specific responses of rice exist between two [SOURCE: Page_144]

125 [SOURCE: Page_144]

phylogenetically close strains. Valette et al. (2020 a&b) extended even more their set up to include more Azospirillum strains, Herbaspirillum seropedicae and Paraburkholderia phytofirmans PsJN, known PGPRs and one pathogenic strain, Burkholderia glumae. Transcriptomic and metabolomic common and specific signatures could be compared between beneficials and pathogenic bacteria, explaining that most of the specific genes found to be specific to Azospirillum - rice interaction were also induced by the other PGPR strains and some by the pathogenic strain (Valette et al., 2020b). Recent studies in our lab on rice’s transcriptomic responses to the inoculation of three relatively-close Burkholderia s.l. endophytes put in light these common and specific responses, with for example a common modulation of the jasmonate phytohormone, but with different timing between the strains (King et al., 2019, 2023). In its latest article, King et al. (2023) also demonstrate that PsJN, originating from a different host than rice, induces different rice phenotypic and molecular responses than the rice isolated Burkholderia vietnamiensis or P. kururiensis. These results showed that rice can respond quite differently to microbes from different hosts of origin. Searching for rice’s transcriptomic responses to bacteria ranging from different ecological niches will enable the definition of rice marker genes of beneficial, commensal, or detrimental interactions. To our knowledge, our work is the first study using RNAseq methods to dive deeper in rice’s common and specific molecular responses towards a wide array of strains, in a defined set up. Six bacterial strains were selected depending on three different ecological niches, with two “non-plant-adapted”: the laboratory strain           Escherichia coli    and the ubiquist Stenotrophomonas maltophilia (Nautiyal et al., 2010, Berg et al., 1999), two “plant-isolated”: Bradyrhizobium sp. (isolated from Aeschynomene roots in a paddy field, Chaintreuil et al., 2000) and Pseudomonas defensor (isolated from potato, de Vleesschauwer et al., 2008) respectively known to induce rice growth and ISR against Pyricularia oryzae (Cai et al., 2020, de Vleesschauwer et al., 2008), two “rice-isolated”: Azospirillum sp. with PGP activity (Isawa et al., 2010) and Burkholderia plantarii, a pathogen responsible for seedling root rot (Solis et al., 2006). The rate and pattern of colonisation by each of our selected bacteria of Nipponbare rice roots in a hydroponic system was assessed. Our results demonstrate that all bacteria where able to colonise rice roots at early time points. A RNAseq analysis on rice’s leaf and root in response to bacterial root inoculation was conducted. Our results show that there is no clear common response to the inoculation by all of our selected bacteria, or to the two non- plant adapted ones. We assessed rice’s common transcriptomic response to our selected PGPR in both shoots and roots. We also focused on rice’s specific responses to each of them and in which way they differ from the pathogen-induced response. This enabled us to understand which particular transcriptomic pathways in rice are affected by each selected bacterium. [SOURCE: Page_145]

126 [SOURCE: Page_145]

Material and Methods [SOURCE: Page_146]

Plant growth and bacterial inoculations Oryza sativa ssp. japonica cv. Nipponbare was chosen for this study and prepared for all hydroponic experiments as follows. Rice seeds were dehusked and surface-disinfected as explained in Guigard et al. (in Front. Plant Sci., in press), by immersion in 70% ethanol for 3 min, then in 3.8% sodium hypochlorite supplemented with 1% Tween 20 under agitation (180 rpm) for 30 min. Seeds were rinsed three times with sterile water, three times with 2% filtered sodium thiosulfate and three more times with sterile water. They were incubated overnight at 28 °C in sterile water in the dark (imbibition step) then germinated on 8% H2O agar plate for 30 h at 28 °C. To confirm surface disinfection, 100 µL of the last rinse and imbibition waters were plated on tryptic soy agar (Sigma-Aldrich) Petri dishes. As explained in King et al., 2019, five homogeneous rice seedlings were then transferred to sterile magenta boxes (SPL Lifesciences Co. Ltd) filled with 150 mL of autoclaved perlite and 200 mL of sterile hydroponic medium (recipe in Supplementary Table 1). Plants were grown in a growth chamber (16 h light; 8 h dark; 28 °C; 70% humidity). Bacterial strains used in this study are listed in Table 1. All bacterial strains were cultured as follows: glycerol stocks (20%) of bacterial cells, conserved at -80 °C were plated on the appropriate media depending on the strain and incubated for 72 h at 28 °C or 96 h at 37 °C for the Bradyrhizobium strain. Liquid 20 mL cultures in the appropriate liquid media (more precision below) were inoculated in 50 mL falcon tubes and incubated overnight under agitation (180 rpm) at 28°C. Except for the slowly growing Bradyrhizobium, 1 mL of all overnight cultures were inoculated in a fresh liquid medium for 4h. Bacterial cells were then centrifuged for 10 min at 2,500 g and resuspended in sterile water. After the determination of the OD/CFU ratio for each strain, plantlets were inoculated with 107 bacterial cells 4 days after sowing. [SOURCE: Page_146]

Bacterial transformation Fluorescent-labelled strains were used for root colonization experiments (Table 1). The GFP-tagged Bradyrhizobium ORS278 was provided by Dr Eric Giraud. It was obtained by inserting the non-replicative plasmid pVO155-npt^2-GFP (Okazaki et al., 2016) into the BRADO3982 kaiC gene, previously shown to not play a symbiotic role during the interaction with Aeschynomene spp (E. Giraud, personal communication). The GFP-tagged Azospirillum sp. B510 was kindly provided by Dr Florence Wisniewski-Dyé, tagged as described in Chamam et al., 2013. [SOURCE: Page_146]

Stenotrophomonas maltophilia ABIP44 bacterial cells were transformed by electroporation with the pIN29 plasmid, comprising a chloramphenicol (Cm) resistance gene as well as the DsRed gene under the control of a constitutive TAC promoter (Vergunst et al., 2010). After 24 h of incubation on low salt Luria broth (LBm, Sigma-Aldrich) Cm (100 μg·ml−1) at 28 °C, the most fluorescent colonies were selected. The DsRed-tagged Pseudomonas defensor WCS374r, Burkholderia plantarii ATCC 43733, Escherichia coli DH5α were already available in the lab, transformed as explained above. Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278 was grown on Yeast-Mannitol Agar (YMA), its GFP- tagged derivative was grown on YMA supplemented with Kanamycin (100 µg. mL-1). Both Azospirillum sp. B510 grow on LBm, the GFP-tagged needs to be supplemented with Gentamycin (10 µg. mL-1). Both Escherichia coli DH5α grow on Luria Broth (LB), the DsRed tagged needs to be supplemented with Cm (100 µg. mL-1). The other wild-type strains grow on LBm, their DsRed-tagged associated needs to be supplemented with Cm (100 µg. mL-1). [SOURCE: Page_148]

Characterisation of rice root colonisation Rice roots were harvested at 1, 7- and 14-days post-inoculation (dpi). Each root system was transferred in a 2 ml screwed-tube containing 1 mL of sterile water and a sterile ceramic bead. Each tube was weighted and grinded using a FastPrep 24TM 5G (MP Biomedicals) at 6 m/s for 40 s. The suspension was then serial-diluted and inoculated in appropriate media depending on the inoculated strain and incubated at 28 °C for 24 h (except for ORS278 which was incubated at 37 °C for 48 h). Colony-forming units (CFU) were enumerated. The root- associated total bacterial population was measured on five rice root systems. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Microscopy Microscopic observations of each strain’s colonisation on rice roots were performed on the primary root to compare colonisation patterns on the same root tissue. Primary roots were harvested at 7 and 14 dpi and mounted between slide and slips cover and directly observed using a Zeiss AxioImager Z$_2$ epifluorescence microscope. [SOURCE: Page_148]

RNA extraction For each inoculation condition, four biological replicates (four pools of five plants) were harvested at 7 dpi, according to King et al., 2019. Five whole root systems were harvested and pooled, representing one root biological replicate. Five last mature leaves were harvested, representing one leaf biological replicate. Directly after harvest, samples were snap-frozen in liquid nitrogen and stored at −80 °C. [SOURCE: Page_148]

129 [SOURCE: Page_148]

Figure 1: Quantification of rice roots colonisation for our selected bacterial strains. Nipponbare rice seedlings were grown in a hydroponic system and inoculated 5 days after germination with 1.107 bacterial cells. Colony-forming units were characterised at 1, 7 and 14 days after inoculation (n = 5). [SOURCE: Page_149]

Figure 2: Microscopic observations of each bacterial pattern of colonisation (7 days post-inoculation). Rice roots at 7 days post-inoculation under colonisation of fluorescent strains of Escherichia coli DH5α (A), Stenotrophomonas maltophilia ABIP44 (B), Pseudomonas defensor WCS374r (C), Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278 (D), Azospirillum sp. B510 (E) and Burkholderia plantarii ATCC 43733 (F). (A), (B), (C) & (F) strains were tagged by electroporation with the DsRed plasmid. (D) strain was transformed by the insertion of the non-replicative plasmid pVO155-npt^2-GFP in kaiC gene. (E) strain was tagged by conjugaison with the eGFP-containing pMP2444 plasmid. [SOURCE: Page_149]

130 [SOURCE: Page_149]

Root samples were grinded in liquid nitrogen with cooled mortar and pillar, until a powder was obtained. Leaf samples were snap-frozen in liquid nitrogen, then cold-grinded with a TissueLyser II (Retsch) set to 30 Hz for 30 s. Total RNA was extracted using the TriReagent protocol (Sigma) and a DNAse (QIAgen) treatment was added in the protocol before purification with the RNA Clean & Concentrator kit (Zymo), according to the manufacturer’s instructions. The quantity and quality of total RNA was assessed using a NanoDrop 1000 spectrophotometer (ThermoFisher) and a BioAnalyzer 2100 (Agilent). [SOURCE: Page_150]

RNA sequencing and processing For each condition, total RNA of three biological replicates for each rice organ (RNA Integrity Number > 6) were sequenced. 48 libraries were prepared with the Illumina’s Stranded mRNA Prep Ligation kit and NovaSeq 6000 2x^100 pb Illumina sequencing was performed (Sequence By Synthesis method, NovaSeq Reagent kit) by MGX-Montpellier GenomiX core facility (MGX) France (https://www.mgx.cnrs.fr/). The qualitative and quantitative validations of libraries were verified thanks to a Fragment Analyzer (kit High Sensitivity NGS) and qPCR (LightCycler 480, ROCHE). Raw Illumina reads in FASTQ format quality control was done thanks to the FastQC software (version 0.11.9) Read quality was controlled and then aligned on the rice genome (version IRGSP 1.0) and counted (HiSAT$_2$ v^2.2.1, StringTie v^2.1.7, Galaxy software). [SOURCE: Page_150]

Differential gene expression and enrichment analysis. Diane software (version 1.0.6.), linked with edgeR, was used to calculate differential gene expression between non-inoculated and inoculated conditions. All genes having an adjusted p-value inferior to 0.01 and a a |log^2FoldChange| superior to 1 were considered as significantly differentially expressed and chosen for our listings. All functional enrichment analysis was performed using g: Profiler (version e^109_eg^56_p^17_1d^3191d) with g:SCS multiple testing correction method applying significance threshold of 0.05. To avoid redundancy between GO terms, we used ReviGO (version 1.8.1.) to reduce the number of enriched GO terms by removing the redundant GO terms listed, replacing terms with the most accurate and understandable expression. [SOURCE: Page_150]

131 [SOURCE: Page_150]

Figure 3: Transcriptomic responses of Nipponbare to bacterial inoculation Differentially expressed genes in the leaf (A) and root (B) compartments when in interaction with each selected strain. Here are displayed the number of differentially expressed genes with a |log^2FoldChange| > 1 at an adjusted p-value < 0,01. Red= up-regulation. Blue= down-regulation. Plain colours= total DEGs per condition. Hatched colours= number of DEGs specific of the interaction [SOURCE: Page_151]

132 [SOURCE: Page_151]

Results [SOURCE: Page_152]

Quantification and qualification of the colonisation of each bacterium on rice roots We first wanted to evaluate the ability of each bacterial strain to colonise rice seedling roots at early time points. We either selected or built bacterial strains expressing a fluorescent marker (DsRed or GFP) and carrying an antibiotic resistance gene (see Materials and Methods and Table 1). Rice grown in a sterile hydroponic system were inoculated with each strain individually and the root-associated bacteria were enumerated by plate counting (CFU) after 1, 7 and 14 days. Microscopic observations were done at 7- and 14-days post- inoculation. All results can be seen at Figure 1 & 2, Supplementary Table 2 and Figure 1. All selected strains colonised Nipponbare’s roots, at all-time points (Figure 1). E. coli, S. maltophilia and A. sp bacterial densities increased with time, reaching their highest mean level at 14 dpi, at 5.77.107, 4.83.107 and 6.73.107 cells per mg of roots respectively (Figure 1, Supplementary Table 2). B. ORS278, P. defensor and B. plantarii reached their highest mean bacterial load levels at 7 dpi, with 8.37.105, 4.14.105 and 7.85.106 cells per mg of roots respectively before a slight decrease at 14 dpi. At the earliest point, 1 day after inoculation, non-plant-adapted bacteria (E. coli and S. maltophilia) revealed the lowest colonisation rates, after steadily increasing until reaching the second and third highest colonisation rates (Supplementary Table 2). B. ORS278 and P. defensor exhibited a higher mean CFU level at the beginning and didn’t increase as fast as non-plant adapted bacteria afterwards (Figure 1, Supplementary Table 2). The rice-isolated Azospirillum sp. strain showed the highest rate of rice root colonisation at the latest time point (Figure 1). [SOURCE: Page_152]

Rice transcriptomic general response to a diversity of bacteria To understand how rice was impacted and responds to bacteria coming from different backgrounds, a transcriptomic analysis via RNAseq was conducted on both rice’s shoot and roots. A total of 1.3 billion reads were retrieved among all sequenced samples, with an average of 34.5 million reads in root samples and 26.6 million reads in leaves (Supplementary Table 3). A principal component analysis discriminated the transcriptomic signatures of rice, depending on their inoculated bacteria, compared with the non-inoculated control (Supplementary Figure 2). The number of differentially expressed genes (DEGs, |log^2FoldChange| > 1 at an adjusted pvalue < 0.01) in both compartments, depending on the inoculated bacteria is shown in Figure 3. Considering all different conditions, 5671 DEGs were modulated in roots and 5193 in leaves. The proportion of DEGs induced by the inoculation of either of the two non-plant adapted bacteria, E. coli and S. maltophilia, was weak compared [SOURCE: Page_152]

to the plant-adapted one. It was even more true for the specific DEGs (i.e. DEGs modulated specifically by one bacteria, in hatched colours in Figure 3). In roots, 223 DEGs were modulated by the inoculation of E. coli, but only 4 were specifically up-regulated (Figure 3). Regarding S. maltophilia’s effect on rice roots, only 125 DEGs overall and 5 specific DEGs were retrieved. The leaves response of rice was even weaker when inoculated with either one of these bacterias, with overall 149 and 35 DEGs modulated by E. coli and S. maltophilia respectively. Rice transcriptomic modulations to plant-adapted bacteria had richer DEGs pools. Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278 was the bacterial strain modulating the highest number of roots DEGs: 2044, with 1264 down-regulated and 780 up-regulated (Figure 3). This strain also modulated the highest number of specific DEGs in roots: 954, with 617 down-regulated (around 49 %) and 337 up-regulated (around 43%). Rice roots inoculated with the other plant- adapted P. defensor had 555 DEGs (294 down-regulated, 261 up-regulated) with a small proportion being specific to this type of inoculation (around 3% of its total DEGs). There were larger numbers of DEGs modulated in leaves by plant-adapted bacteria than non-plant adapted ones. Bradyrhizobium sp triggers 1086 DEGs, with 17% being specific to its interaction. P. defensor modulates 451 total DEGs, with only 3 % of them being specific to its inoculation (Figure 3). The rice-associated model Azospirillum sp B510 had 1398 (733 down-regulated, 665 up-regulated) DEGs, whereas B. plantarii modulated 1326 DEGs (400 down-regulated, 926 up-regulated). The latter triggered a specific rice root response, with 67% of its total DEGs being specific to its inoculation. On the other hand, in leaves, Azospirillum sp triggered more total DEGs than the phytopathogen (1793 to 1679, respectively), but the latter triggered more specific DEGs than the first one (around 52% of the total DEGs, compared to 40% in leaves of rice inoculated with Azospirillum sp, Figure 3). [SOURCE: Page_154]

There is a weak common transcriptomic response of rice to all bacterial inoculation and to non-plant-adapted bacteria To describe the physiological processes and pathways modulated in response to bacterial interaction in rice, Gene Ontology (GO) terms and Kyoto Encyclopedia of Genes and Genomes (KEGG) pathway enrichment analysis was performed on each pool of DEGs shared in the rice response to all bacteria, all non-plant-adapted bacteria, all PGPR and all plant- adapted bacteria (including the phytopathogen) (Figures 6, 7 & 8, Table 2, Supplementary Tables 4 to 8). DEGs names were passed through three databases to ensure a good characterisation: Oryzabase, the Rice Annotation Project DataBase (RAP-DB) and Rice Genome Annotation Project (RGAP). Gathering all the DEGs common to all treatments, there were 21 and 10 common DEGs, in root and shoot samples respectively (Table 2). Five DEGs corresponded to transcription factors (OsbHLH133, regulating iron distribution and OsMADS59), a multi antimicrobial extrusion protein (OsSTA240), a kinase and a hypothetical protein are up- regulated in roots, independently of the strain inoculated. The relatively small proportion of common up-regulated genes in roots wasn’t enough to decipher significant GO terms or KEGG pathway enrichment. However, genes related to protein kinase activity (especially serine/threonine), catalytic activity and carbohydrate binding were enriched in the down- regulated pool of DEGs (Table 2). Sixteen DEGs corresponding to disease resistance proteins (OsPR$_5$, Os^09g^0356200), Wall-associated kinases (WAKs, OsWAK85, 82, 11) and other protein-kinases (OsSHR$_5$, Os^11g^0625900) were indeed down-regulated (Table 2). In leaves, 10 DEGs in total were commonly regulated by either of the 6 bacterial strains (Table 2). Five DEGs corresponding to nucleic acid, RNA or ATP-binding proteins or ribosomal proteins were up-regulated, whereas 5 DEGs corresponding to a heat-shock factor, a Zincfinger domain containing protein and some conserved hypothetical ones were down- regulated (Table 2). The GO terms and KEGG pathway analyses highlighted an enrichment for genes related to RNA binding and processing and ribosome biogenesis in the up-regulated DEGs, and genes linked with chaperone binding in the down-regulated DEGs (Supplementary Table 6). There were only 3 DEGs specifically modulated by non-plant-adapted bacteria, E. coli and S. maltophilia: a leucine-rich repeat encoding gene down-regulated in roots and 2 DEGs were up-regulated in leaves, corresponding to a NAD(P)-binding domain containing protein and a hypothetical conserved gene (Supplementary Table 4). [SOURCE: Page_156]

Signatures of common responses to PGPR and to plant-adapted bacteria exist in rice transcriptomic data The comparison between the common and specific DEGs linked with the inoculation of rice roots by PGPR (Bradyrhizobium sp, P. defensor & Azospirillum sp) is described in Figures 6 & 8 and Supplementary Table 7. In the root compartment, 180 DEGs (90 in either way) are commonly regulated by the three PGPR, representing 8% from all 2027 DEGs modulated by at least one of them (Figure 4). Among them, DEGs related to nitrogen sensing and nitrate transport (OsALN, OsNRT$_2$.4 respectively), cell-wall biogenesis-related genes (OsXTH15, OsGAUT$_4$), ROS metabolism (prx^26, OsDAO$_4$), or abiotic stress (OsCMO, OsDCA$_1$, OsrbohH, Os^02g^0150700, OsRFP) were up-regulated. The different pathways encountered in this little number of up-regulated DEGs was associated with no significant GO term or KEGG pathway. DEGs related to defence response (OsvWA36, OsWRKY62, [SOURCE: Page_156]

137 [SOURCE: Page_156]

Figure 4: Comparison of rice transcriptomic responses to PGPR Venn diagrams show the number and proportion of DEGs that are shared between or are specific to Bradyrhizobium sp ORS278 (pink), Azospirillum sp B510 (green) and / or Pseudomonas defensor WCS374r (purple) inoculation on leaves (A) and roots (B). Bold numbers show the total number of DEGs regulated, the ones next to a red arrow represent the up-regulated portion and ones near blue arrows the down-regulated portion. [SOURCE: Page_157]

138 [SOURCE: Page_157]

OsPR10a, OsALD$_1$, OsDHFR, cytochromes), abiotic tolerance (OsHAK16 & 17, OsWRKY47, OsHSP70 & 71, OsPGL13, OsAAO$_1$, OsAKR$_2$), receptor-like protein kinases (OsWAK43, OsRLK22, OsCEBiP) were down-regulated in roots inoculated by either of the PGPR. This is to be linked with the enrichment analysis showing that the common rice root response to PGPR was enriched in genes implicated in the stress response (defense response and ascorbate and aldarate metabolism) among the down-regulated genes (Supplementary Table 7). From the overall 1341 DEGs that were specifically regulated in leaves by at least one of the three PGPR, the common pool represented 9% (127, 35 up-regulated, 92 down- regulated, Figure 4). There was a few highly up-regulated DEGs (log^2FoldChange > 2), but the GO terms highlighted a significant up-regulation of genes related to nucleosomal and chromatin assembly, binding and organisation and DNA recombination (Supplementary Table 7). Among the 92 down-regulated genes, DEGs related to transcription factors (OsWRKY19 & 7 & 24 & 72), defence response (OsWRKY28, OsRLCK162, OsPAD$_4$, OsGRAS44), abiotic stress response (OsERF91, OsVQ$_5$, OsHMP43, OsCLT$_1$, Os^01g^0597800) can be noted, representing a general down-regulation of stress responses. GO terms enrichment also showed a down-regulation of heat-shock protein binding and chaperone-mediated protein folding (Supplementary Table 7). When looking at the common rice response to plant-adapted bacteria, namely to P. defensor WCS374r, Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278, Azospirillum sp. B510 and B. plantarii ATC43733 (described in Figure 5 and Supplementary Table 8), 33 and 115 DEGs were commonly regulated by each of the 4 bacteria considered, in roots and shoots respectively. Since there was a small proportion of commonly-regulated genes in root, no GO term or KEGG pathway was retrieved. However, DEGs related to development (OsPKS10, OsPME19, Os^11g^0673200), a thionin (OsTHI29) and a regulator of hypersensitive response cell death (OsNAC$_4$) were up-regulated in this common pool of genes. Genes implicated with biotic stress (Os^05g^0404400), carbohydrate transport (OsSWEET2b), chloroplast homeostasis (OsPGR$_5$, OsGSE) were commonly down-regulated. Interestingly, there were 10 DEGs that were down-regulated in rice roots inoculated with the PGPRs and up-regulated in those with the phytopathogen. These genes were related to defence response (OsPR1b, rNBS41, Os$_1$- Cys PrxB), reproductive organ development (OsAAP10C), a phosphatase-reductase (OsAPRL$_1$), a nodulin (Os^04g^0422300), a malonyl-transferase (Os^02g^0483500) and two expressed proteins (Os^06g^0200600 and Os^05g^0368100). In leaves, enrichment analysis highlighted that catalytic activities, as well as chloroplast and plastid related genes were enriched in the up-regulated list of DEGs. It also contained DEGs related to abiotic stress response (OsFRO$_2$, OsNCED$_1$, PPR, OsPAP1d, Supplementary Table 8). In terms of down-regulated genes, GO terms of defence response [SOURCE: Page_158]
