Bold numbers show the total number of DEGs regulated, the ones next to a red arrow represent the up-regulated portion and ones near blue arrows the down-regulated portion. [SOURCE: Page_159]

140 [SOURCE: Page_159]

to oomycetes and response to oomycetes were enriched in the common rice leaf response to plant-adapted bacteria. Among the 77 down-regulated DEGs, genes related to nutrient transporters (OsAMT$_3$;2, OsHAK$_5$, OsNPF$_7$.2), disease resistance (OsBBI$_1$, Os^04g^0378575, Os^12g^0174700, OsWRKY67, OsPAL$_7$, OsNPR$_4$, Os^04g^0514600), lignin biosynthesis (OsPAL$_7$, Os^4CL$_5$), two cupredoxins (OsUCL24 & 33), two heat stress transcription factors (OsHSF15 & 23), two cytochromes p^450 (CYP76L$_1$, OsCYP71E$_5$) and two Auxin-responsive genes (OsSAUR21 & 26) were found. Surprisingly, there was a pool of 10 genes commonly regulated by all four inoculations on rice roots, which were down-regulated in PGPR-inoculated roots and up-regulated in the pathogen-inoculated ones. This group contains genes encoding a pathogenesis-related protein (OsPR1b), an amino-acid permease responsible for tiller number and grain yield (OsAAP$_5$), stress-related transferase, phosphatase and peroxiredoxin (OsMAT-2, OsAPRL$_1$, Os$_1$-Cys Prx B), a disease-resistance related NBS-LRR protein (rNBS41), a putative nodulin (Os^04g^0422300), a salicylic-acid mediated ATPase (Os^03g^0802500) and two proteins of unknown function (Os^06g^0200600, Os^05g^0368100) (Supplementary Table 5). [SOURCE: Page_160]

Transcriptomic signatures specific of rice interaction with PGPR In this last part, the characterisation of physiological pathways that were specifically modulated by each plant-growth promoting rhizobacteria was investigated by reviewing the list of specific DEGs modulated by each of them. There were some wide differences between PGPR inoculation effects in terms of number of DEGs and the organ involved. Azospirillum sp. B510 specifically modulated 457 and 728 DEGs in roots and leaves respectively, while Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278 impacted 954 and 186 DEGs and P. defensor WCS374r 18 and 13 DEGs (Figure 4). These discrepancies between bacteria gave hints on the specificity of interaction with their host. The 50 most modulated DEGs (both organs, in both ways) were selected for each bacterial inoculation, while all the list was investigated for P. defensor. Upon interaction with P. defensor, since the respective lists of DEGs contained less than 20 genes, there was no particular GO term or KEGG pathways being enriched. However, looking at the raw DEGs lists, expression of genes related to Zn-finger containing proteins and other hypothetical proteins were up-regulated while a serine-threonine protein kinase (OsWAK105) as well as a gene related to sheath blight resistance (OsPGIP$_1$) were both repressed. In leaves, the expression of genes related to response of abiotic and biotic stress were up-regulated, while the expression of some genes related to signal transduction were repressed (OsWRKY$_8$, OsPPS$_1$, Os^11g^022880) (Supplementary Table 9). Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278 modulated more than ten times more DEGs than P. defensor in both compartments. In rice roots in interaction with this PGPR, among the 50 [SOURCE: Page_160]

141 [SOURCE: Page_160]

Figure 6: Summary of the common and specific transcriptomic rice responses to plant- growth-promoting rhizobacteria at seven days post-inoculation On the left are the general rice response to all PGPR in our study, namely a modulation of transcription factors and biotic and abiotic stress response in leaves, with an increase in the expression of genes related to nitrogen sensing and cell growth as well as a decrease in biotic and abiotic stress responses and overall signalling in rice roots. On the right are the specific rice response to each of the PGPRs (purple: P. defensor WSC374r, dark red: Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278, yellow: Azospirillum sp. B510). Genes indicated in spheres are proposed marker genes of common rice response to PGPR on the left and specific rice association with each PGPR on the right. [SOURCE: Page_161]

142 [SOURCE: Page_161]

DEGs more intensely up-regulated, we found genes related to cellular growth (multiple different esterases-related genes), phosphate homeostasis (OsPT$_6$), as well as laccases and detoxifying related proteins. In terms of down-regulated DEGs, multiple receptor-like cytoplasmic kinases (RLCKs), WAKs and transcription factors as well as leucine rich repeats proteins and OsLOX$_8$, related to biotic stress recognition were among the 50 most intensely repressed genes. In leaves of rice inoculated with this PGPR, genes related to RNA modifications, amino acid transporters and photosystems were up-regulated, while a gene involved in jasmonate signalling (OsJAZ12), defence response, shikimate biosynthesis and hypersensitive response processes-related genes were down-regulated (Supplementary Table 10). In rice inoculated with Azospirillum sp. B510, there were more genes differentially expressed in leaves than in roots, which was the opposite for every other inoculation effect. In the belowground compartment, the expression of genes related to auxin (OsJAR$_3$), ethylene (OsERF083), expansines, transcription factors and detoxification are up-regulated, while pathogenesis-related (OsPR10), heat-shock related proteins (hsp^20), heavy-metal associated domain and leucine rich repeat containing proteins expression was repressed. In leaves of rice in interaction with this bacteria, iron and nitrogen homeostasis (OsIMA$_1$ & 2, OsIRO$_2$ & 3, OsNRT$_1$.1B), as well as growth and defence-related genes (OsFH15, Os^12g^056950, OsCDSP32) expression was up-regulated, while pathogenesis-related proteins (OsPR1a), phytoalexin biosynthesis (OsCPS$_2$, OsDXS$_3$, OsbHLH25), transcription factors (OsWRKY62 & 76) and serine-threonine kinases expression was down-regulated (Supplementary Table 11). [SOURCE: Page_162]

Discussion In this study, rice molecular responses to a range of bacteria was investigated with fine precision with a RNAseq analysis. The selection of bacteria on a gradient of adaptation to rice roots was done based on literature and their reported effects on diverse plant hosts. Two non- plant-adapted bacteria were selected, the lab strain Escherichia coli DH5α and the ubiquist Stenotrophomonas maltophilia ABIP44. Two plant-adapted bacteria were added, both inducing beneficial effects on rice growth (Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278) and tolerance to pathogens (Pseudomonas defensor WCS374r). A Nipponbare-isolated PGPR model, Azospirillum sp. B510 as well as Burkholderia plantarii ATCC43733, chosen for his contrasting behaviour (rice roots seedling pathogen) represented the rice-isolated bacteria. [SOURCE: Page_162]

143 [SOURCE: Page_162]

Link between degree of adaptation of rice of bacteria, their intensity of colonisation and their modulation of rice transcriptomic response Nipponbare root colonisation by our chosen bacteria depended on the bacterial type inoculated. Non-plant adapted bacteria colonised roots without any stagnation or decrease, starting with the lowest colonisation rates at one day post-inoculation, whereas plant-adapted ones tended either to plateau at 7 days post-inoculation (B. sp. ORS278 and B. plantarii) or increase gradually (P. defensor and A. sp B510) (Figure 1). At the last timepoint, rice-isolated bacteria showed among the greatest level of colonisation of Nipponbare roots (Supplementary Table 2). This put in light their natural adaptation to their host. However, the result that non- plant adapted bacteria had two of the highest colonisation rates at the later timepoint is striking. The main hypothesis for this is that rice doesn’t control their colonisation rate as it can do for plant-adapted bacteria. This can be linked with the fact that less root cell wall autofluorescence, a trait generally linked with defence response (Veereshlingam et al., 2004), was reported in root inoculated with non-plant adapted bacteria compared with plant-adapted or the phytopathogen at both 7- and 14-days post-inoculation (Figure 2, Supplementary Figure 1) and the smallest list of modulated DEGs among the total list at 7 days post-inoculation (Figure 3). There did not seem to have a link between rice root colonisation rates and number of modulated DEGs among bacteria. These numbers were nevertheless dependent on the bacterial adaptation to rice roots: non-plant-adapted had the smallest numbers of DEG, while plant-adapted and even more rice-isolated strains modulated larger lists of DEGs (Figure 1 & 3). [SOURCE: Page_163]

A weak common transcriptomic response leads to a specialisation of rice response to rhizobacteria Among our results, the common rice response to bacterial inoculation only represents 21 DEGs in roots and 10 in leaves (Table 2). Although light, the common response of rice to a bacterial inoculation, regardless of the ecological niche of the bacteria, was richer in roots, with a clear modulation of biotic recognition signalling pathways, supposedly upon bacterial recognition. In leaves, remobilisation of some cellular processes took place, with the regulation of nucleic-acid binding proteins encoding genes. This response represented 0.4% and 0.2% of the total DEGs modulated either in roots or shoots respectively. This result was in concordance with literature. Valette et al., 2020b, upon inoculation of Nipponbare with ten various PGPR strains, found out that on 15 rice marker genes of various physiological processes, only 4 were commonly modulated (OsDXS$_3$, OsDTC$_2$ and Os^02g^0582900 being [SOURCE: Page_163]

144 [SOURCE: Page_163]

activated and OsPR1b being inhibited). Even upon Nipponbare’s inoculation with two different but phylogenetically close Azospirillum strains, only 108 genes were commonly regulated by both strains (1.5%). On another cultivar (Cigalon), this common pool modulated upon A. lipoferum 4B or A. sp. B510 represented 10% (Drogue et al., 2014). In King et al., 2023 analysis, three Burkholderia s. l. commonly regulated 25% and 28% of up- and down-regulated DEGs respectively. To decipher the common rice response to any bacterial inoculation, the non-plant adapted bacteria were included. However, they showed small amount of DEGs modulated in both rice roots and shoot, poorly inducing rice responses in terms of number of DEGs. This hints the fact that rice triggers a specific response to PGPR and plant-adapted bacteria only, strengthened by the larger amounts of DEGs in those pools. Upon recognition of our chosen plant-adapted bacteria, the expression of defence-related genes were up-regulated and those related to development were down-regulated in rice roots. In leaves, abiotic and biotic stress mitigation were modulated, while nutrient transport and biosynthesis were repressed. Those mechanisms put in light the slight induction of general defence mechanisms typically occurring upon bacterial recognition and the related trade-off between growth and defence. In this pool of DEGs, 10 were down-regulated by PGPR inoculation while up-regulated in the pathogen-inoculated condition (Supplementary Table 5). They were related to defence mechanisms, with OsPR1b, OsMAT-2, OsCys PrxB and rNBS41, as well as an ATPase mediated by salicylic acid. This phytohormone response is induced in the control of phytopathogens, in systemic acquired resistance (SAR) or induced systemic resistance (ISR) (de Vleesschauwer et al., 2008, Yu et al., 2022). Our result highlights that specific modulation of defence response of rice could be a marker of differentiation between PGPR and pathogens. In our study, only one bacterial root phytopathogen was selected, to differentiate rice responses to a pathogen from what was specifically modulated by plant-adapted PGPR. More transcriptomic analyses of rice responses to PGPR compared with phytopathogen should help fill that knowledge gap about the differentiation between both bacterial types. The common transcriptomic response of rice to PGPR is summarised in Figure 6. This common pool of DEGs represents 3% and 2% of the complete set of DEGs in roots and shoots of this analysis, and 8% and 9% of the DEGs modulated by at least one PGPR (Figure 4). In rice roots inoculated by either of the PGPR, a clear modulation of abiotic stress tolerance, with an increase of the expression of cell-wall biogenesis-related genes and a decrease of defence and PTI-inducer recognition receptors-related genes expression was recorded. In rice leaves, genes implicated in mechanisms related to nuclear processes were induced while those related to defence and abiotic stress response were once again repressed. Overall, rice inoculated with either one of these PGPR showed a molecular response indicative of growth induction and stress mitigation. [SOURCE: Page_164]

145 [SOURCE: Page_164]

Rice transcriptomic response to plant-growth promoting bacteria depends on bacterial lifestyle and degree of adaptation to rice Plant transcriptomic responses due to Pseudomonas defensor WCS374r was investigated for the first time in our study. At 7 days post-inoculation, its presence on Nipponbare roots specifically modulated 18 DEGs in roots and 13 in leaves (Figure 4, Supplementary Table 9). A serine-threonine kinase in particular, OsWAK105 and one C2H$_2$ Zn-finger containing protein expression were induced in roots. The latter was reported to have effects on plant development and abiotic stress tolerance (Liu et al., 2022). In rice leaves upon inoculation with this PGPR, abiotic and biotic stress mitigation as well as transcription factors (OsWRKY$_8$) were modulated. This strain was isolated from the rhizosphere of potato and is reported to induce ISR in rice against P. oryzae (de Vleesschauwer et al., 2008). Despite modulating the smallest pool of DEGs amongst the PGPR and showing the smallest rate of rice roots colonisation (Figure 1), complementary studies highlighted its help on rice growth and tolerance to Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99 (Xoo), suggesting that its PGPR abilities are visible on the long term (Complementary Figures 2.1. & 2.2.). Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278 was isolated from Aeschynomene rhizosphere in a rice field and reported to induce rice growth (Chaintreuil et al., 2000). It was also reported to trigger ISR in Arabidopsis thaliana against P. syringae, at one-month post-inoculation via transcriptomic priming of JA and ET-influenced genes (PDF$_1$.1, PAL$_1$) (Cartieaux et al., 2008). At 7 days post-inoculation on Nipponbare roots, this strain triggered cellular growth and processes and represses biotic stress recognition (RLCK, LRR) expression in roots, while increasing the expression of cellular activity-related genes and repressing shikimate biosynthesis and hypersensitive response processes in leaves (Supplementary Table 10). Its presence affected the growth-defence trade-off in favour of more cellular activities and growth while evading MTI- related recognition patterns at early timepoints, leading to rice growth and biotic stress tolerance enhancement (also reported in the complementary results). Azospirillum sp. B510, isolated from Nipponbare roots and shown to increase diverse rice cultivar growth, was the strain with the highest rate of colonisation at 14 days post inoculation (Figure 1, Supplementary Table 2). Its biological control potential was reported against P. oryzae and Xoo, with the ISR being dependent on ethylene signalling-related genes expression (Yasuda et al., 2009, Kusajima et al., 2018). This genus was extensively used in rice transcriptomic response to PGPR. Drogue et al., 2014 listed 34 genes induced in different Azospirillum-rice cultivar interactions, of which 8 genes were found differently expressed in our data: 2 genes related to iron homeostasis (Os^01g^0647200: OsIMA$_1$ and Os^01g^0952800: OsIRO$_2$), a cytochrome p^450 (Os^02g^0569400), a phytoalexin biosynthesis-related gene [SOURCE: Page_165]

146 [SOURCE: Page_165]

(Os^07g^0190000: OsDXS$_3$)) and 4 hypothetical gene (Os^07g^0537900, Os^10g^0195250, Os^12g^0236100, Os^01g^0495701). Thomas et al., 2019, overviewed the molecular signalling pathways in plant roots upon inoculation with A. brasilense, and several transcription factors auxin-related and transporters genes specifically modulated in their study were found in our data (AP$_2$/ERF, NAC, GRAS, SAUR, nitrate transporters). Under our study, root cellular growth, auxin and ethylene-related genes and detoxification processes were up-regulated while OsPR10, hsp^20 and MTI-triggering LRR expression were repressed in roots. In Nipponbare leaves, A. sp B510 root inoculation also induced nitrate and iron homeostasis as well as repressed defence related genes expression (OsPR1a, OsDXS$_3$, OsWRKY62 & 76) (Supplementary Table 11). Iron deficiency-inducible genes OsIMA$_1$ & OsIMA$_2$ are known to positively regulate iron uptake pathways in rice, and their overexpression leads to tolerance to iron deficiency and accumulation in rice leaves and seeds (Kobayashi et al., 2021). The bacteria could then have a role in its host iron uptake and accumulation. [SOURCE: Page_166]

Conclusion In this chapter, the molecular responses of rice to a wide variety of bacteria have been studied along a gradient of adaptation to its colonisation. Our results showed that all selected strains colonised Nipponbare’s roots efficiently (more than 105 bacterial cells per mg of root), with different rates depending on their degree of adaptation to rice colonisation. E. coli and S. maltophilia showed the highest rates at fourteen days post-inoculation compared to the PGPR and the phytopathogen, highlighting the colonisation control effect of rice roots on plant- adapted bacteria, either beneficial or pathogenic. The main objective of this study was to characterise the general and specific early molecular responses of rice to interaction with a variety of bacteria. In summary, the lack of control and interaction established by rice with non-plant adapted bacteria was highlighted by the low list of DEGs compared to the number of DEGs for the plant-adapted ones. This resulted in a weak common rice response to each bacterial type, representing less than 1% of the total DEGs pool. Common rice responses to plant-adapted and PGPR also represented small percentages of the total analysis, confirming the fact that plants modulated their diverse and complex molecular physiology specifically for each bacterium encountered. PGPR induced a general increase in the expression of growth and stress-related genes, while repressing the expression of defence-related pathways (receptor recognition activity as well as pathogenesis-related proteins), both in roots and shoots. All three PGPR used in this study modulated specific aspects of these general patterns highlighted in Figure 6, demonstrating once again that rice specifically modulated its physiology to interact with a specific bacterium. [SOURCE: Page_166]

Supplementary Figure 1: Microscopic observations of each bacterial pattern of colonisation (14 days post-inoculation) Rice roots at 14 days post-inoculation under colonisation of fluorescent strains of Escherichia coli DH5α (A), Stenotrophomonas maltophilia ABIP44 (B), Pseudomonas defensor WCS374r (C), Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278 (D), Azospirillum sp. B510 (E) and Burkholderia plantarii ATCC 43733 (F). (A), (B), (C) & (F) strains were tagged by electroporation with the DsRed plasmid. (D) strain was transformed by the insertion of the non-replicative plasmid pVO155-npt^2-GFP in kaiC gene. (E) strain was tagged by conjugaison with the eGFP-containing pMP2444 plasmid. [SOURCE: Page_174]

Complementary results: Phenotypic rice response to a diversity of bacteria: growth, tolerance to a leaf pathogen and yield traits To complement our work on rice molecular responses to a diversity of bacteria from different ecological niches, its phenotypic response to their long-term interaction was investigated. For the assessment of the impact of bacterial inoculation on rice growth and Xoo- tolerance response, twenty 800 mL-anti-coiling pots (Comptoir Vert, France) with potting soil were used per condition, and were left to grow for 8 weeks before harvest. The potential of each bacteria to enhance rice’s defence response against a shoot phytopathogen was also investigated. 50 days-old Nipponbare plants were infected by leaf-clipping with Xanthomonas oryzae pv oryzae PXO99 (Xoo) strain as described in Niño-Liu et al. 2005 and the extent of Xoo-induced chlorosis (yellowing of the leaf) and necrosis (leaf tissue being completely degraded) was recorded 14 days later, when the harvest for the phenotypic parameters was carried out (maximum height on the day of the harvest, dry shoot weight after seven days at 46 °C) (n= 18). For the assessment of the impact of bacterial inoculation on rice yield, twelve 2 L pots were used per condition and were left to grow for four months. Maximum height was recorded, while total dry weight, total grains dry weight and number of grains per panicles were recorded after a wekk of drying at 46 °C (n= 12). For these experiments, plants were grown in a greenhouse (28 °C, 70% humidity, from April to July 2023). Each plantlet was inoculated with 108 bacterial cells one week after sowing. Results are available in Complementary Figures 2.1. and 2.2. and Complementary Tables 2.1. and 2.2. Under our conditions bacterial inoculation had either a beneficial, a detrimental or no significant effect on rice’s growth. The inoculation of E. coli induced a decrease in both height and weight of the plant, whereas the interaction with the other non-plant adapted bacterium, S. maltophilia promoted growth on both parameters (Complementary Figure 2.1. A & B). The inoculation of Azospirillum sp. B510 didn’t induce a statistically different growth phenotype compared to the uninoculated plants, probably because of a lot of variability seen between replicates, on both height and weight of shoots (Complementary Figure 2.1. A & B, Complementary Table 2.1.) Height was statistically significantly improved by the inoculation with B. ORS278, P. defensor and B. plantarii. Dry weight of rice shoots inoculated with Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278 was also induced significantly (Complementary Figure 2.1. A & B, Complementary Table 2.1.). Under our conditions, bacterial inoculation had negative or no significant effects on the extent of symptoms (chlorosis and necrosis spread on the infected leaf) induced by Xoo. The root inoculation of E. coli, S. maltophilia and B. plantarii didn’t induce significant differences in chlorosis spread on rice leaves. [SOURCE: Page_194]

Xoo infection was undergone by leaf-clipping two weeks prior to the harvest. Chlorosis (C) and necrosis spread (D) in cm were recorded. [SOURCE: Page_195]

Rice plants inoculated with each PGPR (B. OSR278, P. defensor and A. sp. B510) showed a significant reduction in symptoms of chlorosis (Complementary Figure 2.1.C, Complementary Table 2.1). There was no bacterial inoculation that significantly affected necrosis spread on rice leaves under our conditions. The impact of the long-term association between Nipponbare and a range of bacteria from different ecological niches was investigated on yield parameters. Nipponbare was grown in soil for four months after inoculation and its maximum height, dry shoot weight as well as the total dry weight of grains and mean weight of one grain was measured (n= 12). Azospirillum sp. B510 and Burkholderia plantarii ATC43733 had significant beneficial effects on rice height (Complementary Figure 2.2., Complementary Table 2.2). Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278 had a negative effect on total shoot and grain dry weight (Complementary Figure 2.2., Complementary Table 2.2).). Escherichia coli inoculation was the only condition inducing mean grain weight (Complementary Figure 2.2., Complementary Table 2.2).). [SOURCE: Page_196]

Under our growth conditions, there doesn’t seem to have a link between potential of colonisation and phenotypic effects on rice growth or tolerance to phytopathogens. At eight weeks post-inoculation, non-plant adapted bacteria had contrasted effects on rice growth: Escherichia coli inoculation decreased both rice height and biomass production, while S. maltophilia induced them. Among the chosen PGPR, only Bradyrhizobium induced both parameters at eight weeks of interaction (Complementary Figure 2.1.). All of these beneficial effects became non-significant after four months of growth, except for a rice height induction by both rice-isolated bacteria and a slight increase in mean grain weight following E. coli inoculation (Complementary Figure 2.2.A & D). Bacterial inoculation seemed to modulate rice growth parameters but only PGPR inoculation diminished the Xoo-induced chlorosis spread on infected leaves, while non-plant adapted bacteria or the phytopathogen did not (Complementary Figure 2.1. C & D). This is in line with results from Timmermann et al., 2017, stating that ISR effects and extent depended on the degree of plant-adaptation and PGP abilities in protective bacteria on Arabidopsis leaves infected with P. syringae D3000. E. coli DH5α was already reported to have beneficial impacts on maize shoot and root biomass production, stating that this strain should also be considered as a possible plant-adapted soil bacterium (Nautiyal et al., 2010). Rice growth stimulation could be independent from their direct association with bacteria, but rather linked with bacterial enzymatic processes in soil, rendering nutrient more available for rice uptake (Backer et al., 2018). Our strain of Stenotrophomonas maltophilia did not affect rice in a greater way than E. coli or the other PGPR strains, but this species was recently reported to have some beneficial endophytic plant strains, enhancing for example chickpea yield (Berg et al., 1999, Gopalakrishnan et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_196]

177 [SOURCE: Page_196]

While the beneficial effects of our chosen PGPR were numerous, it would be interesting to sequence the genome of our S. maltophilia strain to decipher potential PGP traits within its genome. [SOURCE: Page_198]

Overall, all bacterial inoculations had an effect on rice growth parameters at some point (eight weeks or four months post inoculation). Overall, non-plant adapted bacteria had opposite effects (one being detrimental and the other beneficial at eight weeks), while beneficial bacteria tended to increase rice growth, tolerance to a phytopathogen and yield parameters. These effects depended on the time point chosen and the bacterial strain. The specific mechanisms involved in inducing these beneficial effects under our conditions should be of greater interest to understand how even non-plant adapted bacteria could have beneficial effects on rice yield (soil fertility, interaction with the resident microbiota, etc.). [SOURCE: Page_198]

Chapter 4: Symbiotic compatibility between rice cultivars              and         arbuscular                mycorrhizal                 fungi genotypes affects rice growth and mycorrhiza- induced resistance [SOURCE: Page_200]

Ludivine Guigard^1, Lea Jobert^1, Nicolas Busset^1, Lionel Moulin^1, Pierre Czernic^1* 1 PHIM Plant Health Institute, Univ Montpellier, IRD, CIRAD, INRAE, Institut Agro, Montpellier, France [SOURCE: Page_200]

Ce chapitre est rédigé sous la forme d’un article, il a été soumis à Frontiers in Plant Science le 17 Août 2023, accepté sous révisions mineures le 27 septembre 2023, publié le 24 octobre 2023. Il se focalise sur l’étude de la compatibilité symbiotique entre six cultivars de riz appartenant aux deux sous-espèces indica (IR64 et Phka Rumduol) ou japonica (Nipponbare, Kitaake, Azucena, Zhonghua 11) avec trois espèces modèles de champignons mycorhiziens à arbuscules (AMF) (Funneliformis mosseae FR140, Rhizophagus intraradices FR121 et R. irregularis DAOM197198). Cette compatibilité symbiotique a été testée sur des paramètres de colonisation racinaire, de croissance des plants, de leur tolérance à un agent phytopathogène ainsi que sur l’expression de gènes marqueurs de la santé de la plante. Des résultats complémentaires sont disponibles à la fin du chapitre, démontrant l’impact de l’environnement biotique et abiotique sur la symbiose mycorhizienne. [SOURCE: Page_200]

181 [SOURCE: Page_200]

Résumé du chapitre 4 Après les deux chapitres précédents centrés sur l’étude des réponses du riz à des rhizobactéries phytobénéfiques, nous nous sommes intéressés à la réponse de ce même hôte végétal en interaction avec des champignons mycorhiziens à arbuscules (AMF). Comme décrit en chapitre 1 dans ma synthèse bibliographique, ces champignons mycorhiziens sont bénéfiques à la santé de leur hôte soit par des mécanismes d’amélioration de croissance (assimilation de nutriments ou stimulation directe) ou des mécanismes de contrôle biologique d’agents phytopathogènes, notamment par une résistance induite par la mycorhization (MIR). Cependant, ces effets bénéfiques dépendent de l’hôte végétal ainsi que de l’espèce fongique considérée. De plus, peu d’études se sont focalisées sur la réponse d’une diversité de riz face à plusieurs AMF afin de déceler et d’expliquer leurs différences de compatibilité symbiotique. Notre étude cherchait à approfondir nos connaissances sur le sujet, en essayant de mettre en évidence des combinaisons qui seraient bénéfiques, neutres voire négatives sur les points de vue de la croissance et de la santé du riz, ou de sa réponse face à des stress environnementaux. Le chapitre 4 traite de l’étude de la compatibilité symbiotique entre six cultivars de riz appartenant aux deux sous-espèces indica (IR64 et Phka Rumduol) ou japonica (Nipponbare, Kitaake, Azucena, Zhonghua 11) avec trois espèces modèles de champignons mycorhiziens à arbuscules (AMF) (Funneliformis mosseae FR140, Rhizophagus intraradices FR121 et R. irregularis DAOM197198). Pour cela, nous avons suivi : i) des paramètres de croissance du riz (hauteur des plantes, poids frais des racines ainsi que poids frais et secs des parties aériennes), ii) l’intensité de la colonisation fongique sur les racines (colonisation et contenu en arbuscules), iii) les réponses moléculaires systémiques à la mycorrhization (RT-qPCR dans les feuilles de riz mycorhizés), et iv) des symptômes dus à une infection par la bactérie phytopathogène (Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99 (Xoo)). L’ensemble de ces paramètres ont été utilisés comme des proxy de la compatibilité symbiotique entre riz et AMF, dans un contexte de croissance appauvri en nutriments, en particulier en carence phosphatée. La mycorhization dépendant fortement de l’environnement autour des racines de l’hôte végétal et afin d’optimiser les conditions permettant la mycorhization du riz, un substrat pauvre et inerte composé de sable, de perlite et de vermiculite, supplémenté uniquement à l’aide d’une solution nutritive de Hoagland modifiée et diluée a été utilisé. Les trois espèces d’AMF choisies sont capables de coloniser les racines de tous les cultivars de riz choisis, avec des différences d’efficacité (à la fois sur l’intensité de colonisation et la présence d’arbuscules) suivant la combinaison étudiée. Dans nos conditions de croissance, les riz indica ont une intensité de mycorhization plus faible que les japonica. La [SOURCE: Page_201]

182 [SOURCE: Page_201]

mycorhization du riz induit à la fois des effets neutres, bénéfiques ou délétères sur la croissance du riz et l’étendue de symptômes foliaires dus à Xoo suivant la combinaison étudiée. Rhizophagus irregularis DAOM197198 s’est avéré être l’AMF colonisant le plus intensément le riz, quel que soit le cultivar étudié. Il induit également à la fois la croissance du riz et réduit les symptômes causés par Xoo sur les feuilles, parmi toutes les combinaisons étudiées et particulièrement sur Nipponbare. Les analyses transcriptomiques par RT-qPCR sur les feuilles d’IR64 et Nipponbare mycorhizés mettent en lumière deux réponses différentes à la mycorhization sur une diversité de gènes marqueurs physiologiques suivant le cultivar étudié. Dans l’ensemble, lors d’une situation de compatibilité symbiotique entre riz et AMF dans nos conditions expérimentales, la colonisation et le fonctionnement de la symbiose permet une augmentation de la croissance du riz, une répression des réponses moléculaires de privation de nutriments comme le phosphate et atténue les symptômes d’infection phytopathogène. [SOURCE: Page_202]

183 [SOURCE: Page_202]

184 [SOURCE: Page_203]

Symbiotic compatibility between rice cultivars and arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi genotypes affects rice growth and mycorrhiza-induced resistance [SOURCE: Page_204]

Ludivine Guigard^1, Lea Jobert^1, Nicolas Busset^1, Lionel Moulin^1, Pierre Czernic^1* 1 PHIM Plant Health Institute, Univ Montpellier, IRD, CIRAD, INRAE, Institut Agro, Montpellier, France [SOURCE: Page_204]

Introduction In recent years, there has been a growing interest in the naturally occurring interactions between plants and the inhabitants of their root microbiome. It has been widely reported that this cohort of microorganisms plays a role in the growth of their host and in its tolerance to biotic and abiotic stresses (Berendsen et al. 2012; Schlaeppi and Bulgarelli 2015; Vannier et al. 2019; de la Fuente Cantó et al. 2020). One of the most ancient symbiosis between plants and rhizospheric microorganisms is mycorrhizal symbiosis, occurring between arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF) of the Glomeromycota clade and roughly 80% of vascular plants, including Angiosperms of interest such as wheat, maize or rice (Schüβler et al. 2001; Gutjahr et al. 2008; Jung et al. 2012). The establishment of this symbiosis is mediated by a molecular dialogue between the partners, via the exudation of strigolactones by the plant and the recognition of fungal Myc factors (Mbodj et al. 2018; Ho-Plágaro and García-Garrido 2022). When in contact with the root, the hyphae changes into an adhesive structure named hyphopodia, enabling the access to the internal root cortex. It spreads via intercellular spaces and colonise cortical cells with highly branched intracellular structures named arbuscules, preferential sites of exchange with their host (Gutjahr et al. 2008, 2009; Jung et al. 2012; Liu et al. 2022). Their number and functioning in a plant root system is recognized as a marker of symbiotic compatibility (Montero et al. 2019). During these exchanges, plants retribute up to 30% of their produced photosynthates to the fungi in the form of sugars and lipids (Jung et al. 2012; Sugiura et al. 2020). In return, mycorrhizal fungi provide a multitude of beneficial effects. The mere presence of the fungi within cortical root cells can enhance shoot and root biomass, especially stimulating the lateral roots formation and development in wheat, rice or maize (Paszkowski and Boller 2002; Oláh et al. 2005; Gutjahr et al. 2009; Fiorilli et al. 2018). Its hyphal network enables it to cover a large area of soil, recovering essential and/or poorly bioavailable nutrients or water for the [SOURCE: Page_204]

185 [SOURCE: Page_204]

development of its host (Begum et al. 2019; Kadam et al. 2020). Particularly, phosphorus is a key nutrient in AMF symbiosis with plants (Smith et al. 2011). Its bioavailability (or lack thereof due to complexation with soil particulates) affects the recruitment and functioning of the fungal association with its host (Breuillin et al. 2010). Mycorrhization impacts inorganic phosphate (Pi) responsive genes expression in multiple plant species, such as rice or wheat, suppressing for instance the Pi starvation response typically occurring in low Pi soils (Yang et al. 2012; Fiorilli et al. 2018; Campo and San Segundo 2020). In addition to improving the mineral nutrition of the plant, the AMF symbiosis can help its host tolerate a wide range of stresses. It can increase its host's tolerance to a variety of abiotic stresses, from drought to excessive temperature, or reduce root uptake of heavy metals (de Andrade et al. 2015; Begum et al. 2019; Chen et al. 2019). AMF symbioses also enhance their host's tolerance to pathogen pressure at two complementary levels. Fungal root colonisation protects the host both by competing with soil pathogens for its photosynthates and colonisation sites and by triggering a local defence response (accumulation of callose, ROS, phenols and R proteins) (Schouteden et al. 2015; Gupta et al. 2017; Dowarah et al. 2021). By modulating phytohormonal pathways such as jasmonate, salicylic acid and ethylene, AMF primes its host's defence responses in the shoot via a mechanism called mycorrhiza-induced resistance (MIR) (Jung et al. 2012; Gupta et al. 2017; Fiorilli et al. 2018; Nishad et al. 2020). This MIR results in reduced foliar symptoms and control of pathogen development on a variety of plants and shoot pathogens (Liu et al. 2007; Fiorilli et al. 2018; Kadam et al. 2020). Due to their ability to improve soil fertility and plant health, AMF have great potential as plant bioinoculants in the field. This potential is as promising as AMF are generally known to have low host specificity, capable to induce growth of a multitude of different crops (Van Geel et al, 2016). However, recent metanalyses and studies highlighted that there exists different symbiotic compatibilities between AMF species and crop cultivars since different associations results in different phenotypic observations (Pérez-de-Luque et al. 2017; Campos et al. 2018; Silva et al. 2018). A meta-analysis on 115 studies showed that some specific associations between AMF genera and plant host families are more efficient for crop growth promotion, such as the Poacees family with the AMF genera Funneliformis and Rhizophagus (Van Geel et al. 2016). Crop responsiveness to mycorrhization is indeed plant genotype-dependent, as well as AMF species-dependent and is positively linked with AMF colonisation (Lehman et al. 2012). This meta-analysis explained that the relationship between crop mycorrhizal response and AMF colonisation wasn’t significant for wheat or barley, while recent studies have underlined differences in symbiotic compatibility between rice genotypes, both in terms of AMF colonisation and its effect on rice growth (Suzuki et al. 2015; Diedhiou et al. 2016; Davidson et al. 2019). [SOURCE: Page_205]

186 [SOURCE: Page_205]

Rice typical watering mode is flooding, but it has major drawbacks: monopolising a third of the world’s freshwater, high levels of methane production, soil polluting streaming of chemical inputs and negative impact on AMF development (Redeker et al. 2000; Saito et al. 2018; Chialva et al. 2020, Chandel et al. 2002). As a substitute to constant flooding, Alternate Wetting and Drying (AWD) rice management practices have been developed, reducing water use by up to 30% and methane emissions by 48% without reducing yield (Richards and Sander 2014). Rice varieties that are AMF-responsive should therefore be selected in fields that are being converted from flooded to AWD rice systems. Within these paddy fields, initial studies showed little or no colonisation of different rice varieties under flooded conditions (Lumini et al. 2011; Vallino et al. 2014), but recent ones have reported AMF colonisation in experimental and farmers’ fields around the world, especially in rainfed lowland systems (Sarkodee-Addo et al. 2020; Chialva et al. 2020; Barro et al. 2022). There are few studies on the natural occurrence of AMF communities and their diversity in rice paddy fields (Wang et al. 2015, 2021; Bernaola et al. 2018a; Zhang et al. 2020). AMF communities rely on the site and the irrigation mode and Glomerales, Archaeosporales and Diversisporales are generally the predominant orders (Lumini et al. 2011; Chialva et al. 2020; Barro et al. 2022).              Members from the Glomeraceae, Claroideoglomeraceae and Paraglomeraceae families have been found in paddy fields in China and Ghana (Wang et al. 2015; Sarkodee-Addo et al. 2020), including the well-studied Rhizophagus irregularis and Funneliformis mosseae species. Another analysis on fragrant black rice in Indian fields identified R. intraradices and F. mosseae in both field and rice root samples (Surendirakumar et al. 2021). Their global distribution in various fields, long-term storage ability, and ability to form symbiosis with a wide range of plant hosts make them excellent models for studying AMF symbiosis (Berruti et al. 2016). Under greenhouse conditions, there is evidence that rice mycorrhization can improve plant biomass, yield and tolerance to multiple abiotic and biotic stresses (Gutjahr et al. 2009; Campos-Soriano et al. 2012; Li et al. 2016; Campo et al. 2020a). These beneficial effects depend on rice developmental stage, variety and AMF genotype (Suzuki et al. 2015; Sisaphaithong et al. 2017; Wang et al. 2021). Surprisingly, studies on the global effect of AMF symbiosis on both rice’s growth and defence responses are scarce or limited to a few combinations of rice cultivars and AMF species. Studies by Campos-Soriano & San-Segundo showed that the mycorrhization of a single japonica variety, Senia, by the AMF model R. intraradices enhances its biomass and resistance to Pyricularia (P.) oryzae, both locally and systemically (Campos-Soriano et al. 2010, 2012). Two AMF species, R. irregularis and F. mosseae, inoculated on 12 japonica rice varieties showed contrasted effects on rice growth, [SOURCE: Page_206]

Pi content in leaves, and resistance to P. oryzae infection (Campo et al. 2020). A multi-AMF species inoculant on another two tropical japonica varieties also increases their growth but at the same time their susceptibility to insect attacks and Rhizoctonia solani infection (Bernaola et al. 2018b). Finally, a large study by Suzuki et al. 2015 showed a range of impact (from improvement to deterioration) of F. mosseae’s inoculation on the biomass of 64 rice genotypes. In order to develop AMF bioinoculants for rice production under AWD conditions, it is necessary to deepen our understanding of the symbiotic compatibility between rice and AMFs. This will assess which combinations are beneficial, negligible, or detrimental to plant growth and responses to environmental stresses. In this study, the symbiotic compatibility between six varieties of japonica and indica rice and three AMF species, known to interact with rice, was characterised. Model rice varieties as well as varieties with potential for AWD programs were targeted. We analysed the colonisation rate and intensity as well as the functioning of the interaction between rice and mycorrhizal fungi. We then assessed how AMF inoculation impacts rice’s growth and defence responses to a pathogenic infection by Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae (Xoo). We used a combination of phenotypic and rice gene expression studies to uncover promising compatible associations. How phenotypic responses of rice to AMF symbiosis can be linked to systemic changes in marker gene expression (ranging from growth, phytohormonal balances to defence response) was investigated in the leaves of two rice model cultivars contrasting in AMF establishment and responses. [SOURCE: Page_208]

Material and methods [SOURCE: Page_208]

Plant and fungal material Six Oryza sativa cultivars and three AMF genotypes belonging to three different species were selected and their characteristics are listed in Table 1. Two indica (IR64 and Phka Rumduol) and four japonica (Azucena, Kitaake, Nipponbare and Zhonghua 11) subspecies were selected. Seeds were obtained from IRRI and propagated at IRD except for Phka Rumduol which was provided by CIRAD. Funneliformis mosseae FR140 (FM), Rhizophagus intraradices FR121 (RIN) and Rhizophagus irregularis DAOM 197198 (RIR) were purchased from MycAgro Lab (Technopôle Agro-Environnement, Bretenière, France) in the form of individual granular inoculum (100 spores / g). Plant growth conditions Rice seeds were dehusked and surface-sterilised by immersion in 70% ethanol for 3 min, then in 3.8% sodium hypochlorite supplemented with 1% Tween 20 under agitation (180 rpm) for 30 min. Seeds were rinsed three times with sterile water, three times with 2% filtered sodium thiosulfate and three more times with sterile water. They were incubated overnight at 28°C in sterile water in the dark and then germinated on sterile-soaked sand for three days at 28°C. To ensure the absence of contaminants, 100 µL of the last rinse water and imbibition water were plated on tryptic soy agar (Sigma-Aldrich) Petri dishes. Four homogeneous rice seedlings were then transferred to anti-coiling pots (Comptoir Vert, France) filled with 150 mL of clay beads (6-18 mm, Terres & Traditions, France) and 450 mL of sterile inert substrate composed of 70% of sand, 20% sieved perlite and 10% of vermiculite (Campo & San Segundo, 2020). This substrate was inoculated with either the AMF granular inoculum, or the granular inoculum without fungal spores (control) at a volume of 5% v/v per pot. Rice plants were grown for 2.5 months in a growth chamber (12 h day/night, 28 °C day, 26 °C night, 75% humidity). The substrate was moistened for one week and then watered three times a week with a Hoagland solution reduced in phosphate (2.5 mM Ca(NO$_3$)2, 2.5 mM KNO$_3$, 1 mM MgSO$_4$, 0.25 mM (NH$_4$)2SO$_4$, 25 µM KH2PO$_4$ and trace elements, complete recipe in Supplementary Table 1), with the watering volume gradually increased according to the cultivar growth. [SOURCE: Page_210]

Rice growth phenotyping Maximum height, shoot and root fresh weights were measured for each plant (n= 20 per condition). Shoot dry weight was also measured after drying at 40 °C for one week at 48 h (n= 20 par condition). Roots were stored in 70% ethanol at 4 °C until mycorrhizal quantification (n= 5 pools of 4 root systems). [SOURCE: Page_210]

Mycorrhizal quantification The root systems of four plants from the same pot were washed in tap water, placed in 70% ethanol and stored at 4 °C until analysis. Fungal structures were stained using a blue ink-based protocol modified from Cao et al. 2013. Roots were heated to 80 °C for 45 min in 10% KOH. They were rinsed three times with ultrapure water (MilliQ) and stained with a staining solution consisting of 5% blue ink (Waterman “Bleu Sérénité”) in 5% acetic acid at room temperature for 10 min, rinced again three times with MilliQ water and fixed in 5% acetic acid overnight. Five replicates of 20 to 25 fragments from coronary roots were mounted between slide and glass and the mycorrhizal index (global mycorrhization and arbuscular intensity) were assessed as in Trouvelot et al. 1986, on a Axiozoom Zeiss microscope. [SOURCE: Page_210]

Biocontrol assays against Xanthomonas oryzae pv oryzae Infection of Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99 (Xoo) was carried out on 50-days old rice plants by leaf clipping as described in Niño-Liu et al. 2005. The extent of chlorosis and necrosis was assessed 14 days post-inoculation (dpi) on each leaf (n= 18). Mock inoculations were made with sterile water to assess that the sole clipping of the leaf doesn’t induce any disease symptoms. [SOURCE: Page_211]

RNA extraction and quantitative PCR of rice gene expression RNA was extracted from leaf samples collected during leaf-clipping (before infection, at 50 days post germination). They were ground to a fine powder using a TissueLyser II (Retsch) at 30 Hz, for 15 s twice. Each biological replicate consisted of two (Nipponbare) to three (IR64) leaf samples from the same pot, with four biological replicates for each condition. Total RNA was extracted using TriReagent (Sigma) and a DNAse (QIAgen) treatment was added in the protocol before purification with the RNA Clean & Concentrator kit (Zymo), according to the manufacturer’s instructions. The quantity and quality of total RNA was assessed using a NanoDrop 1000 spectrophotometer (ThermoFisher). Approximately 360 ng of total RNA from each biological replicate was used for retrotranscription into cDNA using SuperScript III Reverse Transcriptase (Thermo Fisher Scientific). cDNAs were diluted 5-fold and RT-qPCRs were performed using the Takyon™ Low ROX SYBR 2X MasterMix blue dTTP (Eurogentec) on a LightCycler 480 qPCR system (Roche). Plate preparation was automated using the epMotion 5070 pipetting robot (Eppendorf). Four independent biological replicates were analysed for each condition, each one analysed in triplicate. Relative gene expression is calculated by comparing each sample to the standard’s (number of cycles of EF1a, 𝛥𝐶𝑡), [SOURCE: Page_211]

and then to the control group (FM, RIN & RIR vs. CT, 𝛥𝛥𝐶𝑡). The fold change is calculated with 2−𝑚𝑒𝑎𝑛𝛥𝛥𝐶𝑡 and the logFC represents the relative expression of each marker gene as indicated in (Pfaffl 2001). The list of marker genes, their function and the primers used in this study are listed in Table 2. [SOURCE: Page_212]

Statistical analyses Statistical analyses were performed with Rstudio (version 2022.2.0.443) and R (version 4.1.3) softwares using the packages “readxl”, “tidyverse”, “ggplot^2”, “rstatix”, “ggpubr”, “multicompView”, “car”, “pcr”, “pheatmap”. As phenotypic data of mycorrhization and arbuscular intensity indexes fulfilled normality (Shapiro test, p > 0.05) and homoscedasticity (Levene test, p > 0.05) hypotheses, a two-way ANOVA was used to test the significance of rice cultivar and AMF genotype effects on these parameters, and one-way ANOVA with Tukey test were used for pairwise group comparisons. For rice growth and biocontrol traits, these data did not fulfil the normality and homoscedasticity hypotheses, thus a non-parametric ANOVA with Kruskal-Wallis test (α = 0.05) followed by pairwise comparisons with Wilcoxon tests (α = 0.05) were used for mean group comparisons of measured phenotypic traits. For the analysis of the expression of rice marker genes, the “pcr” package was used. Linear regression was used to assess statistical differences between AMF inoculation and marker gene expression. To visualise the relative expression of each marker gene as a function of rice cultivars and AMF inoculation, a heatmap of LogFC was made with the “pheatmap” package. [SOURCE: Page_212]

194 [SOURCE: Page_213]

Results [SOURCE: Page_214]

Mycorrhizal colonisation and arbuscular content in AMF-rice japonica and indica rice cultivars To assess the symbiotic compatibility between the three AMF species (RIN, RIR, FM) and the six rice cultivars (listed in Table 1), we analysed the mycorrhization rates in the 18 combinations. We focused on the global fungal colonisation rate and the percentage of visible arbuscules in the mycorrhizal roots (see Material & Methods). The use of mycorrhizal index of global mycorrhization and arbuscular intensity allowed us to quantify the interaction between AMF and rice. The mean of each mycorrhizal index for each combination are presented in Figure 1 and in Supplementary Table 2. We tested whether rice cultivar and AMF genotype have an impact on rice’s global mycorrhization and arbuscular intensity index, with a two-way ANOVA test. Both indexes are statistically significantly affected by either rice cultivar or AMF genotype (Two-Way ANOVA, F = 66.91593, p < 2*10-6; F = 4.9315, p < 0.01). [SOURCE: Page_214]

All the rice cultivars tested were root colonised by each AMF genotype (Figure 1, Supplementary Table 2). Each fungal organ (hyphal structures, spores, vesicles and arbuscules) was clearly visible on each combination as shown in Figure 2 and Supplementary Figure 1. The global intensity of mycorrhization ranged from 27.20% (Phka Rumduol with RIN) to 83.90% (Nipponbare with RIR). Independently of the fungal inoculation, indica rice varieties have the less intense symbiotic percentage, ranging from 27.20% (Phka Rumduol - RIN) to 46.8% (IR64 - RIN) (Supplementary Table 2). The percentage of mycorrhization of the japonica cultivars ranges from 43.20% (Kitaake with FM) to 83.90% (Nipponbare with RIR). Nipponbare is the most intensely mycorrhized cultivar with 79%, 77.90% and 83.90% for FM, RIN and RIR inoculation, respectively (Supplementary Table 2). The arbuscular percentage of the mycorrhizal roots ranged from 3.22% (Azucena with FM) to 49.40% (Nipponbare with RIR) (Supplementary Table 2). Japonica rice cultivars showed the highest percentage of visible arbuscules in the mycorrhized system compared to indica rice, with RIN and (Figure 1). On the other hand, each rice genotype in interaction with FM formed almost no arbuscules, with a maximum of 5.24% in Zhonghua 11 (Supplementary Table 2). [SOURCE: Page_214]

[SOURCE: Page_215]

196 [SOURCE: Page_215]

Growth response of rice cultivars to AMF colonisation The phenotypic response of rice to AMF inoculation and symbiosis establishment was assessed by growth measurements. Maximum height, fresh and dry shoot weight and fresh root weight were measured for each combination (n= 20) and are shown as boxplots in Figure 3. All the corresponding measured values are listed in Supplementary Table 2. Developmental stage of each cultivar (at 10 weeks) is shown in Supplementary Figure 2. Plants were still at developmental vegetative stage, except for Kitaake plants which flowered one week before harvest. The dataset shows that AMF inoculation can result in an increase, decrease or no significant effect on plant growth parameters. We observed a significant decrease in the height of IR64 during the interaction with FM or RIN (-18.81% and -14.12% respectively, Figure 3A and Supplementary Table 2). The RIR genotype resulted in a significant increase in both root and shoot weights for Phka Rumduol, Kitaake, Zhonghua 11 and Nipponbare. However, this effect was not statistically significant for Azucena. The effect on rice’s dry weight wasn’t as significant as on height or fresh weight, but was still a good proxy for the beneficial effect of AMF on rice growth (Figure 3). Under our growth conditions, we observed different growth rates depending on the rice cultivar. Uninoculated Kitaake was the smallest rice cultivar both in size (24.65 cm) and weight (0.19 g, 0.11 g, 0.09 g on average for fresh shoot, root and dry shoot weight, respectively; Supplementary Table 2). However, mycorrhization of Kitaake induced a clear improvement in growth: the highest dry weight among all combinations being obtained with Kitaake in association with RIN (0.30 g, Figure 3D, Supplementary Table 2). This combination showed the greatest positive effect on rice’s growth on all variables: 36% taller, 259%, 270% and 221% heavier on biomass of fresh shoots and roots, and dry shoots, respectively (Supplementary Table 2). Globally, FM inoculation affected rice growth non-significantly (Nipponbare, Phka Rumduol, Zhonghua 11) or negatively (Azucena, IR64), in both height and weight (Figure 3). The only significant positive interaction was with Kitaake: 20% taller, 163%, 177%, 125% heavier on its fresh shoot & root and dry shoot weights, respectively (Figure 3, Supplementary Table 2). The effect of RIN inoculation was contrasting: beneficial on Kitaake and Zhonghua 11, negative on IR64 and Phka Rumduol or non-significant on Azucena and Nipponbare (Figure 3). RIR was the AMF genotype that induce the most positive effects among all rice cultivars: +35% and +20% leaf height with Kitaake and Phka Rumduol, respectively (Figure 3A); +182% and 103% fresh shoot weight with Kitaake and Zhonghua 11, respectively (Figure 3B) and +196% and +149% fresh root weight with Kitaake and Phka Rumduol, respectively (Figure 3C). [SOURCE: Page_216]

198 [SOURCE: Page_217]

Our results show that the effect of AMF inoculation on rice growth depends on both rice cultivars and AMF genotypes: ranging from negative, neutral to beneficial outcomes across the 18 combinations under study. [SOURCE: Page_218]

Mycorrhiza-induced resistance The potential of each fungal inoculum to induce systemic resistance in the leaves of each rice cultivar during a shoot phytopathogen infection was investigated. Rice plants were infected by leaf-clipping with Xanthomonas oryzae pv oryzae (Xoo) strain PXO99 and the extent of chlorosis and necrosis was recorded 14 days later. The results are shown as boxplots in Figure 4 and all the corresponding measured values are listed in Supplementary Table 2. The effect of AMF inoculation on the chlorosis and necrosis symptoms of rice induced by Xoo PXO99 differed greatly between combinations. Only two combinations, both with RIN, showed a significant increase in leaf symptoms: Azucena on chlorosis (+69%) and Phka Rumduol on necrosis (+106%). Regarding the bio-protective effects of AMF, chlorosis symptoms were significantly reduced on IR64 in combination with FM (-24%) and RIR (-26%), on Zhonghua 11 in combination with FM (-44%), RIN (-28%) and RIR (-29%), and on Nipponbare with FM (-40%) and RIN (-34%) (Figure 4 and Supplementary Table 2). For necrosis, only Zhonghua 11 and Nipponbare showed significant reductions of symptoms. These reductions in the size of necrosis were observed with the three AMF genotypes: -65%, -66% and -64% for Zhonghua 11 with FM, RIN and RIR respectively; and -78%, -87% and - 64% for Nipponbare with FM, RIN and RIR, respectively (Figure 4, Supplementary Table 2). [SOURCE: Page_218]

RT-qPCR analysis of growth and immunity molecular marker genes in contrasting rice-AMF combinations We observed contrasted patterns of symbiotic compatibility among our AMF-cultivar combinations. In order to link these observed differences with the expression level of leaf marker genes, we selected two rice cultivars with contrasting AMF responses: Nipponbare and IR64. The first is a japonica model cultivar and was the most intensely mycorrhized, regardless of the AMF genotype, with the interaction having non-significant to beneficial effects on its growth and tolerance to Xoo infection (Figure 1, Figure 3 and Figure 4). The latter is an indica model cultivar, that was significantly less mycorrhized, with non-significant to negative effects of the AMF interaction on its growth, but with beneficial effects on its tolerance to Xoo infection (Figure 1, Figure 3 and Figure 4). We selected 19 markers genes of development, nutrient homeostasis, hormonal balances and defence and their expression was normalised to that of EF1a reference gene. The list of marker genes, their function and the primers used in this study are listed in Table 2. A summary of the statistical comparison of [SOURCE: Page_218]
