200 [SOURCE: Page_219]

gene expression for each combination, for both Nipponbare and IR64, is provided in Supplementary Table 3 and Supplementary Figures 3 & 4. Their expression was visualised as a heatmap in Figure 5. [SOURCE: Page_220]

The expression of two cellular growth marker genes, OsYABBY$_6$, responsible for abaxial-adaxial polarity and whose expression is needed for leaf development (Jiang et al. 2016), and OsXTH17, a xyloglucan endotransglucosylase/hydrolases involved in primary cell wall formation (Lin et al. 2019) was recorded. A non-significant induction of OsXTH17 expression was observed for Nipponbare in interaction with RIN (p= 0.055), while that of OsYABBY$_6$ is repressed with FM (p= 0.009) (Figure 5, Supplementary Table 3, Supplementary Figure 3). In IR64, their expression was not significantly affected independently of the AMF genotype (Supplementary Figure 4). To assess the effect of mycorrhization on mineral homeostasis in leaves, one iron transporter (OsIRO$_2$), one nitrate-reductase (OsNIA$_1$) and three Pi transporters, marker genes of Pi-starvation response (OsMGD$_2$, OsPAP23 and OsSPX$_3$) were selected. The expression of almost all mineral marker genes was significantly reduced in Nipponbare leaves (OsNIA$_1$, OsMGD$_2$, OsPAP23 with RIN, OsSPX$_3$ with either AMF genotype), except for a non-significant strong induction of OsIRO$_2$ expression when associated with RIN (p= 0,09) (Figure 5, Supplementary Table 3). In leaves of IR64, the expression of OsMGD$_2$ and OsPAP23 was not significantly affected. The expression of the other mineral marker genes was reduced only significantly for OsSPX$_3$ in RIN-mycorrhized leaves, and for OsNIA$_1$ and OsIRO$_2$ in FM-mycorrhized leaves (Figure 5, Supplementary Table 3, Supplementary Figures 3 & 4). Mycorrhization is known to affect the hormonal balance in mycorrhized plants. Its effect on the expression of jasmonate (JA: OsLOX$_4$, a lipoxygenase responsible for the biosynthesis of JA (Nguyen et al. 2022) and OsJAMyb & OsJAZ$_6$, both responsible for JA signalling (Yokotani et al. 2012, Lu et al. 2016), ethylene (ET: OsACS$_1$, 1-aminocyclopropane-1- carboxylate synthase responsible for ethylene biosynthesis) and salicylic acid (SA: OsNPR$_1$, mediating SA biosynthesis and responsives genes (Kumari et al. 2016) & OsWRKY45, a transcription factor mediating SA signaling) pathways was investigated. Overall, jasmonate- and ethylene-related genes expression was not significantly repressed in Nipponbare and IR64 leaves (Figure 5, Supplementary Table 3). SA-related genes were not significantly repressed, except for OsNPR$_1$ in RIN mycorrhized-IR64 leaves (Figure 5, Supplementary Table 3, Supplementary Figures 3 & 4). [SOURCE: Page_220]

The expression of defence-related genes was recorded to assess how mycorrhization affects the defence response in healthy leaves. OsPR$_5$ is a pathogenesis-related protein, [SOURCE: Page_220]

201 [SOURCE: Page_220]

OsPAL$_4$ is a broad-spectrum disease resistance-related gene and OsTGAP$_1$ & OsDXS$_3$ are responsible for phytoalexines production in rice (Deiteil et al. 2012, Petitot et al. 2017, Okada et al. 2009 & Valette et al. 2020). OsMPK10 and OsWRKY30 are responsible for early disease- mediated signalling, the latter also responsive to SA and JA treatments (Nguyen et al. 2014, Ryu et al. 2006). Globally, defence genes appeared to be more induced in mycorrhized IR64 leaves than in Nipponbare leaves (Figure 5, Supplementary Figures 3 & 4). In Nipponbare leaves, we observed i) a significant repression of OsPR$_5$ expression, irrespective of the AMF species, ii) a significant down-regulation of OsPAL$_4$ expression with FM, iii) a non-significant down-regulation with RIN (Supplementary Table 3). In leaves of IR64, phytoalexin biosynthesis-related genes (OsDXS$_3$ and OsTGAP$_1$) were not significantly induced in plant associated with AMF. When mycorrhized with RIR, the expression of OsWRKY30 is significantly induced but not the one of OsMPK10 (p-value = 0.03 and 0.07, respectively) (Figure 5, Supplementary Table 3). [SOURCE: Page_221]

Discussion [SOURCE: Page_221]

Symbiotic compatibility between six rice cultivars and three AMF species depends on the studied combination In this study we have characterised the symbiotic compatibility between multiple rice cultivars from japonica and indica subspecies in association with three AMF genotypes (F. mosseae FR140, R. intraradices FR121 and R. irregularis DAOM197198). First, we observed that all rice cultivars selected for this study were colonised by each fungal inoculum, their colonisation intensity differing between rice cultivars (Phka Rumduol being the lowest and Nipponbare the highest in terms of global mycorrhization). A similar pattern was also observed at the arbuscular level, with FM developing fewer arbuscules when interacting with rice, compared to the two Rhizophagus genotypes. We also observed an effect of AMF colonisation on rice growth that could be either beneficial, neutral, or negative. However, there was no direct relationship between the level of colonisation and the growth phenotype: both indica rice cultivars had similar AMF colonisation and arbuscular levels, but the tendency of IR64 to be negatively affected on growth wasn’t observed for Phka Rumduol (Figure 1 and Figure 3). The japonica cultivars used in this study were generally beneficially affected in their growth (either in height or weight; Figure 3). A special case was observed with the cultivar Kitaake. As stated earlier, early mortality and a general lack of growth were observed for this cultivar in our inert substrate. We hypothesised that Kitaake’s growth wasn’t optimal under these drastic conditions. However, [SOURCE: Page_221]

202 [SOURCE: Page_221]

plants that have managed to grow were well colonised by the three AMF species (43%, 69% and 71% of roots colonised by FM, RIN, and RIR respectively). The highest levels of growth improvement were recorded for this cultivar: 37% taller, 259%, 270% and 221% heavier on biomass of fresh shoots and roots and dry shoots (Supplementary Table 2). AMF symbiosis is known to enhance root anchoring and nutrient uptake in poor soils, thereby improving plant health (Paszkowski and Boller 2002; Gutjahr et al. 2009; Chiu et al. 2018). However, early growth depression following mycorrhization has also been documented in wheat, barley, and soybean (Jacott et al. 2017). Such depression was eventually overcome (or not) in the later life cycle of the crop (Jacott et al. 2017). This growth depression can be explained by the genetic variability of AMF genotypes and their symbiotic effectiveness, but the recurring hypothesis is related to the trade-off between plant photosynthates and soil nutrients recovered by AMF (Jin et al. 2017). Our differences in the phenotypic growth effects of mycorrhization among rice cultivars could be explained by an imbalance between early life stage development and the photosynthetic carbon cost for AMF establishment and function (Jin et al. 2017; Jacott et al. 2017). Our growth conditions were adapted from recent studies on wheat and rice mycorrhization, composed of a mixture of sand, sieved perlite, and vermiculite. It mimics sandy soil and is easy to sterilise. Though, this inert substrate is still poor in essential nutrients, and a modified Hoagland’s solution depleted in phosphate was used to water the plants. Several studies have shown that similar conditions allow both crop species to grow and the establishment of an efficient AMF symbiosis (Gutjahr et al. 2008; Fiorilli et al. 2018; Campo and San Segundo 2020; Campo et al. 2020; Guo et al. 2022). [SOURCE: Page_222]

Japonica cultivars respond better to mycorrhization than indica cultivars Under our growing conditions, japonica rice appeared to be more intensely colonised than indica rice. (Figure 1, Supplementary Table 2). The result of upland japonica rice cultivars being more colonised and responding more to AMF colonisation than flooded indica rice cultivars is shared by several studies, considering their different root architecture and the co- evolution between AMF and upland rice in aerobic selective condition, optimizing their interaction (Diedhiou et al. 2016; Davidson et al. 2019). It is important to note that we selected two indica rice cultivars compared to four japonica, depending on the valuable factors listed in Table 1. This study should be extended to more rice genotypes from both subspecies to understand if this pattern can be generalised. The global understanding of how rice mycorrhization is affected by host genotype is currently limited. In a published report, the mycorrhizal growth response (MGR (%), corresponding to the ratio between the dry weight of mycorrhized and control plants) was measured for 64 rice genotypes from different subspecies 4 weeks after inoculation with F. [SOURCE: Page_222]

203 [SOURCE: Page_222]

mosseae. Differences between genotypes were observed but not related to indica or japonica origin (Suzuki et al. 2015). A possible explanation is that variations in AMF recognition receptors between host genotypes affect their symbiotic compatibility. OsCERK$_1$, a LysM receptor-like kinase that is essential for AMF recognition and activation of the symbiosis pathway, is also responsible for root branching in rice (Chiu et al. 2018). It has been proposed that natural variation between allelic variants of the OsCERK$_1$ gene in different rice cultivars may affect their symbiotic compatibility with R. irregularis DAOM 197198 (Huang et al. 2020). A higher level of AMF colonisation, 14 days post-inoculation, was reported for eleven indica rice cultivars, compared to eight japonica. The difference was proposed to be related to an OsCERK$_1$ haplotype, absent in japonica rice and present in their selected indica cultivars (Lefebvre 2022). In our study, we obtained opposite results, but we chose to harvest the rice after two months of growth, which, in addition to the different substrate used, may explain the difference. Several reports have shown that root colonisation of Nipponbare becomes clearly visible after 14 days post-inoculation, and arbuscules after 3 to 4 weeks (Gutjahr et al. 2015; Liu et al. 2022; Guo et al. 2022). It would be interesting to assess whether rice cultivar also affects the kinetics of AMF establishment and functioning in the long term. [SOURCE: Page_223]

Beneficial effects on resistance to Xoo are highly dependent on rice cultivar and AMF genotypes As plant colonisation by AMF has often been shown to be associated with a better protection against pathogens, we conducted biocontrol trials against Xoo. We observed a general tendency for symptoms to decrease, which was statistically significant for some AMF- rice cultivar combinations (Figure 4, Supplementary Table 2). Only two among 18 showed an increase in the leaf symptoms when associated with an AMF (Phka Rumduol and Azucena associated with RIN). Almost all well-colonised japonica rice species showed a significant reduction of at least one Xoo-induced symptoms thanks to MIR, particularly with Zhonghua 11 and Nipponbare cultivars. The most-colonised rice cultivar, Nipponbare, see chlorosis and necrosis symptoms reduced in both FM and RIN conditions. With the most compatible one, this reduction of symptoms can be noticed but isn’t statistically significant. This reduction of symptoms becomes statistically significant in a repeated study on 20 non-mycorrhizal Nipponbare and 20 associated with RIR (Additionnal file 6: Fig S$_3$). An induction of 39%, 107% and 187% of Nipponbare’s maximum height, shoot and root dry weight, respectively was shown, linked with a reduction of chlorosis symptoms by 32% at 14 days post-clipping. It is noteworthy that IR64, a rice variety three times less intensively mycorrhized, also shows reduced chlorosis symptoms when mycorrhized with FM or RIR. A possible explanation could reside in the differences between rice genotypes themselves, being more or less sensitive to [SOURCE: Page_223]

204 [SOURCE: Page_223]

AMF colonisation and their established interaction, having then important or little to no effects on rice growth phenotype, but notable impacts on rice nutrient physiology and defence responses. Our results highlight here that symbiotic compatibility between AMF and rice species states for a sufficient amount of colonisation allowing significant nutrient starvation response inhibition and phytopathogen-induced symptoms reduction. The potential of biological control by AMF symbiosis is well documented in the literature, although pathogen-dependent. Mycorrhization of japonica rice cultivars by AMF genotypes such as R. intraradices, R. irregularis or F. mosseae showed both a local and a systemic defence response against Pyricularia oryzae, sometimes associated with an increase in shoot biomass (Campos-Soriano et al. 2010, 2012; Campo et al. 2020). In Triticum aestivum cv. Chinese Spring associated with F. mosseae, the AMF symbiosis confers both a positive effect on growth and resistance to X. translucens (Fiorilli et al. 2018). Two American rice cultivars inoculated with a mixture of AMF genotypes showed increased susceptibility to two insects and Rhizoctonia solani infections, but without growth defects or nutrient losses, suggesting an effect of symbiosis on defence vs growth trade-off (Bernaola et al. 2018b). [SOURCE: Page_224]

Oryza sativa cv japonica Nipponbare is the best AMF-responsive rice cultivar Many research studies focus on the association between R. irregularis and Nipponbare (Gutjahr et al. 2009, 2015; Campo et al. 2020; Liu et al. 2022; Guo et al. 2022). Since they are both models (sequenced genomes, easy to grow, transform and store, controlled lifecycle), deepening our knowledge of their interaction at different life stages of both organisms may shed light on how rice interacts with and benefits from AMF and vice-versa. Under our conditions, Nipponbare was the rice cultivar with the best level of mycorrhization, regardless of the AMF inoculated (Figure 1, Supplementary Table 2). There is no significant negative effect of mycorrhization on its growth, with a tendency of AMF symbiosis to increase both height and weight (Figure 3). After Xoo infection, a significant reduction of symptoms was observed with FM and RIN (Figure 4). To further confirm this, the experiment was repeated (n= 20) for Nipponbare in combination with R. irregularis DAOM197198 and showed then a clear positive effect on growth promotion (height, root and leaf weight) and on biocontrol against Xoo (Supplementary Figure 5). At the molecular level, RT-qPCR analysis showed that the expression of two chosen marker genes of cell growth was induced by mycorrhization, even after two months of growth (Figure 5, Supplementary Table 3). Under these conditions, the overall expression of nutrient transporter and defence genes was down-regulated. As these genes are known to be induced upon starvation, Nipponbare in symbiosis with AMF could then be considered to be in a healthy state. [SOURCE: Page_224]

205 [SOURCE: Page_224]

Our results are consistent with previous work. On Nipponbare, Glomus intraradices, which could be classified as R. irregularis according to the current AMF phylogeny, colonises more than 60% of the large lateral roots and increases both dry weight and coronary root length (Gutjahr et al. 2009). Colonisation kinetics of R. irregularis on this cultivar showed high colonisation rates (up to 87% of root length, of which 59% contained arbuscules) and the presence of viable arbuscules and vesicles up to six weeks after inoculation (Gutjahr et al. 2015; Liu et al. 2022). Here we show that even after ten weeks, arbuscules and vesicles were still clearly visible and in larger numbers. A study on the effect of pre-inoculation of F. mosseae on Nipponbare before transplanting in the field showed a root colonisation rate of 65%, an arbuscule content of 1.3% and a vesicle content of 6% (Sisaphaithong et al. 2017), consistent with our results. Growing conditions and practices may have an impact on this low arbuscule content. After 10 weeks of association with F. mosseae, eight temperate japonica cultivars used in Spanish or Italian fields had between 20 and 60% of arbuscules in their mycorrhized roots, with non-significant to beneficial effects on both rice growth and P. oryzae tolerance (Campo et al. 2020). The geographical origin and genotype of the host may determine its symbiotic compatibility with different AMF genotypes. [SOURCE: Page_225]

Defence potential imprint revealed by systemic molecular analyses The description of the phenotypic responses (rice growth and tolerance to Xoo) of different rice cultivars to inoculation with each AMF genotype allowed us to assess the symbiotic compatibility between them. To understand how AMF symbiosis affects the molecular functioning in two rice cultivars with contrasting phenotypic responses to AMF, the leaf molecular responses of Nipponbare and IR64 after six weeks of interaction were studied. Local molecular responses to mycorrhization have been well studied in rice roots (Güimil et al. 2005; Gutjahr et al. 2009; Campos-Soriano et al. 2010), but the systemic effects on the responses of rice leaves are still poorly described. A panel of rice marker genes involved in development, nutrient status, phytohormone signalling and defence responses (listed in Table 2, detailed in the results) was selected, and their expression analysed in healthy 50-day old leaves of Nipponbare and IR64. First of all, the response of Nipponbare leaves to root mycorrhization was consistent with our phenotypic results. We observed a modulation of developmental gene expression, coupled with a reduced starvation response (repression of Pi starvation’s marker genes and a nitrate reductase marker gene, Figure 5, Supplementary Table 3). Defence related genes expression was not significantly to negatively affected by AMF symbiosis in Nipponbare, suggesting that mycorrhization doesn’t induce a systemic defence response under healthy conditions. Overall, mycorrhization of the japonica rice Nipponbare highlights an improvement [SOURCE: Page_225]

206 [SOURCE: Page_225]

of rice’s development and nutritional status, to be linked with the reported increase in growth in this most intensely mycorrhized rice. This imprint on rice’s expression pattern hints for a better overall state and tolerance against abiotic as well as biotic stressors. The study of the effect of either one of them on mycorrhized Nipponbare is interesting to assess which specific systemic mechanisms will act on the trade-off between growth and tolerance. Mycorrhized IR64 plants showed a different response to AMF symbiosis. A non- significant repression of developmental and nutrient starvation response marker genes was observed, coupled with a non-significant induction of the defence response (Figure 5, Supplementary Table 3). Under our growth conditions, mycorrhization of IR64 doesn’t seem to be as beneficial as of Nipponbare and this is still to be linked with phenotypic results (i.e. a non-significant to negative impact of mycorrhization on IR64’s growth with a less mycorrhized cultivar). The nutritional status of both our rice cultivar were investigated thanks to iron, phosphate and nitrate-related genes expression. There is a strong trend of induction of OsIRO$_2$ expression in leaves of Nipponbare interacting with RIN (Figure 5, Supplementary Table 3, Supplementary Figure 3). This transcription factor is induced under Fe deficiency, modulating key genes involved in iron uptake in rice (OsNAS$_1$, OsTOM$_1$ or OsYSL15), with IRO$_2$-overexpressing rice showing improved Fe-deficiency tolerance compared to the non- transgenic lines (Ogo et al. 2006, 2011). Mycorrhization may affect iron homeostasis, via its effect on IRO$_2$ expression, affecting Fe uptake and translocation to shoots and grains (Ogo et al. 2011) but little research has been done on this subject. Leaves of the japonica rice Senia show an increase of IRO$_2$ expression during mycorrhization with RIR, while wheat symbiosis with FM triggers an accumulation of Fe-uptake related proteins in roots, linked with a translocation of iron from roots to shoots, suggesting both a beneficial effect of mycorrhization on iron homeostasis in mycorrhized hosts (Campos-Soriano et al. 2012). AMF have been demonstrated to increase the bioavailability of essential nutrients such as nitrogen and phosphate to their host roots. This capacity can be linked with the repression of OsNIA$_1$ and Pi-starvation-related genes expression in the leaves of our two studied rice cultivars. (Figure 5, Supplementary Table 3, Supplementary Figures 3 & 4). These results are in line with previous reports, and specifically results showing the repression of OsSPX$_3$, OsPAP23 and OsMGD$_2$ expression in Loto rice leaves in association with FM (Liu et al. 2007; Gutjahr et al. 2008; Fiorilli et al. 2018; Campo and San Segundo 2020; Vannini et al. 2021). The expression of marker genes for phytohormones biosynthesis or signalling in leaves was not significantly affected by AMF symbiosis, except for a repression of the SA perception gene (OsNPR$_1$) in leaves of IR64 (Figure 5, Supplementary Table 3, Supplementary Figure 4). Recent literature reports contradictory results on the effect of mycorrhization on SA pathways in wheat or rice: the first isn’t affected on any SA-related [SOURCE: Page_226]

207 [SOURCE: Page_226]

pathways during FM mycorrhization, while OsNPR$_1$ is induced in some japonica rice cultivars (Campos-Soriano et al. 2012; Fiorilli et al. 2018; Tian et al. 2019). Mycorrhization effect on SA-related pathways may be plant species-dependent (Campo and San Segundo 2020). Under our conditions, ethylene and jasmonate related genes were not significantly repressed in both cultivars (Figure 5, Supplementary Table 3). Ethylene biosynthesis is known to be induced in tomato and wheat and repressed in rice leaves under mycorrhization, not significantly in our case (Fiorilli et al. 2018; Campo and San Segundo 2020). Jasmonate- related genes are previously reported to be modulated by mycorrhization in different rice cultivars, both on its biosynthesis or signalling (Campos-Soriano et al. 2012, Campo and San Segundo, 2020). MIR is reported to occur via JA-related pathways, leading to reduced symptoms and pathogen load in multiple host-pathogen interactions (Dowarah et al. 2021). The molecular responses to Xoo infection may allow us to understand if the reduction of symptoms occurring in both our rice cultivars are linked with MIR, a primed induction of defence responses, responding faster and more efficiently than non-mycorrhized controls, or by an overall better rice health state (related to both growth and nutritional status improvement). If the MIR hypothesis appears to be true, deeper transcriptional analyses are to be conducted to understand by which mechanisms MIR occurs. Overall, the systemic response of rice to AMF symbiosis is dependent on rice cultivar and AMF species but can be linked to an overall improvement in rice health. [SOURCE: Page_227]

208 [SOURCE: Page_227]

Conclusion In this study we showed that the establishment of the AMF symbiosis and its effects on rice depends on both the rice cultivar and the AMF genotype for each variable studied. We found that mycorrhizal growth enhancement and induced resistance to Xoo strongly depends on both rice variety and AMF species. In our study and under our conditions, japonica rice chosen subspecies tend to be more colonised and have more benefits on growth and defence responses than indica ones. In the model rice cultivars Nipponbare and IR64, root colonisation rate, growth enhancement or shortage in both shoot and roots can be associated with a systemic modification of molecular pathways in leaves. These differences in rice response raise the question of how beneficial the AMF symbioses really are. In some cases, AMF interactions are detrimental to the growth of the plant host or its response to the environment (Jin et al. 2017; Bernaola et al. 2018b). The assumption that AMF symbiosis can be viewed as an equilibrium between mutualism and parasitism, with symbiont considered as more or less efficient and cooperative partners (Kiers et al. 2011), may be closer to biological reality. In our study, we have identified rice-AMF combinations that are able to develop into a functional symbiosis with positive effects on both rice growth and tolerance to phytopathogens. These combinations should now be tested in unflooded rice field conditions with low Pi to unravel their true potential. [SOURCE: Page_228]

226 [SOURCE: Page_245]

Complementary results: Mycorrhization-induced benefits on rice growth and tolerance to phytopathogen depends on the growth substrate In this work, the symbiotic compatibility between six cultivated rice genotypes and three AMF species was investigated. An inert substrate composed of sand, sieved perlite and vermiculite was used to mimic a poor sandy soil composition found in fields. Under these conditions, the association between the japonica rice Nipponbare and the AMF Rhizophagus irregularis showed a clear symbiotic association, with high percentages of root mycorrhization (83.9% of mycorrhizal intensity, 49.4% of arbuscular content), an increase in rice growth (52% of dry shoot weight per plant) and a decrease in symptoms (-5% and -65% of chlorosis and necrosis spread respectively) caused by infection with the leaf phytopathogen Xanthomonas oryzae pv oryzae. In this interaction, the environmental factor wasn't studied and was fixed under these poor soil conditions. There is some evidence that the state of soil nutrient bioavailability affects the interactions between crops and their microbiota, especially at high concentrations of nitrate and inorganic phosphate (Igiehon & Babalola, 2017). Under high phosphate conditions, the expression of strigolactone and carotenoid biosynthesis is low in the roots of Petunia hybrida, preventing the long-term establishment of the AMF symbiosis (Breuillin et al., 2018). We hypothesised that rice mycorrhization and its benefits would be affected by a change in environmental conditions and substrate. To assess the effects of this type of modification, a greenhouse potting soil experiment was conducted. Five Nipponbare seeds were planted in potting soil (GO M$_2$ growing medium, Jiffy, composition in Complementary Table 3.1.) in 3 L pots, with 10 pots per condition (n= 50). During planting, 2 g of either the AMF granular inoculum without fungal spores (uninoculated control) or the R. irregularis granular inoculum was placed under each seed (10 g per 3 L of soil). Rice seeds were allowed to grow for eight weeks before inoculation with Xoo by leaf cutting, and symptoms of chlorosis and rice growth phenotype (maximum height and fresh mass of shoots) were assessed eleven days after cutting. Roots were harvested and stored in 70% ethanol before staining and counting mycorrhizal fragments (as explained in Materials and Methods). As shown in the Complementary Figure 3.1., there is no significant effect of mycorrhization by RIR under greenhouse and potting soil conditions on either the rice growth phenotype or the chlorosis spread on infected leaves. In terms of mycorrhizal index, both global intensity and arbuscular content were lower than those obtained in the inert substrate (21.5% compared to 83.9% and 6.6% compared to 49.4%, respectively). [SOURCE: Page_246]

227 [SOURCE: Page_246]

228 [SOURCE: Page_247]

These overall results highlight the fact that mycorrhizal capacity and intensity depend on the AMF species, the rice genotype and the environment in which they occur (especially the substrate used). [SOURCE: Page_248]

Chapter                5:       Co-inoculation                    of       Rhizophagus irregularis and Paraburkholderia kururiensis on rice roots leads to different rice responses in terms of growth,               physiology                 and          induced              systemic resistance in leaves [SOURCE: Page_250]

Ludivine Guigard^1, Léa Jobert, Hans Rios^1, Lionel Moulin^1, Pierre Czernic^1 [SOURCE: Page_250]

1 PHIM Plant Health Institute, Univ Montpellier, IRD, CIRAD, INRAE, Institut Agro, Montpellier, France [SOURCE: Page_250]

Ce chapitre est rédigé sous la forme d’un article, rédigé en anglais. Il vise à définir les réponses du riz, communes ou spécifiques, phénotypiques (croissance et tolérance à un stress biotique) ou moléculaires (RT-qPCR et RNAseq en cours), à son interaction avec une rhizobactérie endophyte bénéfique (Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130, chapitre 2), un champignon mycorhizien à arbusucles (Rhizophagus irregularis DAOM1197198, chapitre 4) ou à leur co-inoculation. En résultats complémentaires de cette étude est présenté un screen d’inoculation bactérienne sur des racines mycorhizées de Nipponbare, avec deux espèces fongiques de compatibilité symbiotique différente (chapitre 4). L’effet sur la croissance du riz d’une co- inoculation bactérie – champignon bénéfiques a été investiguée. [SOURCE: Page_250]

231 [SOURCE: Page_250]

Résumé du chapitre 5 [SOURCE: Page_251]

Le dernier chapitre de résultats expérimentaux de cette thèse vise à caractériser les réponses communes et spécifiques du riz en interaction avec un champignon mycorhizien à arbuscules (AMF) tel que décrit dans le chapitre 4, une rhizobactérie phytobénéfique (PGPR, chapitre 2) et leur co-inoculation. Les signaux moléculaires communs et spécifiques de l’induction de la résistance systémique induite (ISR, induite par des PGPR) et / ou de la résistance induite par la mycorhization (MIR, par des AMF) sont également investigués. Un pan de la littérature actuelle met en lumière les bénéfices additionnels voire synergiques qu’apportent des inoculums à deux voire plusieurs espèces microbiennes différentes. Ces bénéfices peuvent cibler à la fois une amélioration de croissance de la plante d’intérêt (Kavadia et al., 2020), une amélioration de sa tolérance à des stress abiotiques (stress salins, pollutions des sols au cadmium ou autres métaux lourds (Chen et al., 2022) ou sur sa résistance face à des agents phytopathogènes (Vannini et al., 2021). Ces effets synergiques dépendent des espèces microbiennes choisies, ainsi que de la variété végétale étudiée et des conditions environnementales mises en place. En effet, en résultats complémentaires de cette étude, six bactéries inoculées sur des racines pré-mycorhizées par deux partenaires fongiques différents ont été testées sur le phénotype de croissance du riz. Ces résultats de screen bactérien mettent en lumière des tendances très différentes suivant le champignon mycorhizien considéré, démontrant ainsi que l’effet bénéfique des inoculations racinaires dépendent des espèces inoculées et également des microorganismes déjà établis. Des conditions de croissance en substrat inerte et de faibles apports en nutriments sont connues pour favoriser l’établissement d’une interaction mutualisme entre plantes et microorganismes. En respectant de telles conditions, l’étude des réponses phénotypiques et moléculaires communes et spécifiques du riz Nipponbare, à un PGPR (Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 (Pk), chapitre 2), un AMF (Rhizophagus irregularis DAOM197198, (RIR) chapitre 4), tous deux capables d’induire une résistance systémique dans les feuilles de riz infectés par un Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99 (Xoo), ainsi qu’à leur co-inoculation a été conduite. Les paramètres de croissance (hauteur des plants, poids secs des parties aériennes et racinaires), de mycorhization (intensité globale et contenu en arbuscules), ainsi que la mesure des symptômes induits par un agent phytopathogène (chloroses induites par Xoo) ont été comparés entre les trois conditions. Pour aller plus loin, les réponses moléculaires systémiques à chaque inoculation (RT-qPCR sur feuilles, RNAseq en cours) ont été analysées en conditions saines ou après 24h d’infiltration par Xoo. L’inoculation de Pk a un impact sur la colonisation racinaire fongique, en réduisant l’intensité de mycorhization de 32 % et le contenu en arbuscule de 77 %. Toutefois, que ce [SOURCE: Page_251]

232 [SOURCE: Page_251]

soit pour les riz mycorhizés avec RIR ou en co-inoculation avec Pk, tous les organes fongiques fonctionnels étaient observables (hyphes extra ou intercellulaires, arbuscules et vésicules). En inoculation seule, la bactérie n’a pas d’effet sur les paramètres de croissance étudiés, alors que la mycorhization du riz par RIR entraîne une augmentation significative de la hauteur et du poids secs des parties aériennes, mais pas racinaires. La co-inoculation induit les mêmes effets, mais d’une manière plus ténue. Les deux inoculations simples ont permis une réduction significative des symptômes de chloroses induite par une infection de Xoo (de 33 % pour Pk et de 62 % pour RIR). En co-inoculation, une réduction des chloroses de 41 % est observée, mais elle s’avère être statistiquement non significative. Sous nos conditions de culture, Nipponbare co-inoculé avec ces deux microorganismes a donc un taux de mycorhization et une croissance moins élevés qu’en inoculation mycorhizienne seule ainsi qu’une réduction de symptômes induits par Xoo moins significative. Les signaux moléculaires systémiques induits par nos trois conditions ont été testés sous deux conditions : en situation saine ou après 24h d’infiltration foliaire avec Xoo. L’impact de l’inoculation par Pk sur les réponses moléculaires du riz ont été détaillées dans le chapitre 2. Nous y détaillions que le phénotype d’ISR n’était peut-être pas visible après 24h d’infiltration, ou par des mécanismes qui seront élucidés par l’analyse transcriptomique plus large (RNAseq) qui est en cours. Ici, l’expression de gènes marqueurs de la santé de la plante sont tous réprimés par la mycorhization simple en situation de stress biotique, quelle que soit la voie étudiée (croissance, nutrition, phytohormones ou défense). En co-inoculation, on observe la même tendance mais avec une tendance d’induction des gènes reliés à la voie de l’acide salicylique, potentiellement liée à une induction des défenses du riz face à une infection par un phytopathogène biotrophe (Campos-Soriano et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_252]

Pour conclure, cette étude a permis de mettre en lumière l’importance du choix des espèces microbiennes inoculées sur une plante d’intérêt, mais également la nécessité de la compréhension de leurs interactions in planta. Bien que Pk et RIR aient été reportés comme promouvant la croissance du riz et induisant l’ISR, leur co-inoculation induit des effets antagonistes pour sa croissance et sa tolérance à un agent phytopathogène. L’analyse transcriptomique à plus large spectre (RNAseq) de la réponse systémique du riz à l’interaction racinaire avec un AMF, une rhizobactérie bénéfique et leur co-inoculation permettra de déchiffrer plus précisément les voies moléculaires communes ou spécifiques à leur interaction, ainsi que de déterminer si leur co-inoculation induit des effets additifs, antagonistes ou synergiques de ces mêmes voies. L’analyse est en cours de séquençage. [SOURCE: Page_252]

233 [SOURCE: Page_252]

234 [SOURCE: Page_253]

Co-inoculation of Rhizophagus irregularis and Paraburkholderia kururiensis on rice roots leads to different rice responses in terms of growth, physiology and induced systemic resistance in leaves [SOURCE: Page_254]

Ludivine Guigard^1, Léa Jobert, Hans Rios^1, Lionel Moulin^1, Pierre Czernic^1 [SOURCE: Page_254]

1 PHIM Plant Health Institute, Univ Montpellier, IRD, CIRAD, INRAE, Institut Agro, Montpellier, France [SOURCE: Page_254]

Introduction [SOURCE: Page_254]

To address current concerns about how crop production can feed an ever-increasing population while relying on organic solutions to eliminate environmentally harmful inputs, plant microbiome studies are becoming increasingly popular in the scientific literature. They provide an overview of the microorganisms living around the main organs of plants (phyllobiome, soil or root microbiome, etc.), their composition and diversity between microbial domains (Edwards et al., 2015, Stone et al., 2018, Wang et al., 2020). Microbiomes inhabiting specific plant tissues influence the health of their hosts through their effects on soil fertility, plant biomass production, and abiotic and biotic stress tolerance (Vannier et al., 2019, Bakker et al., 2020, Babalola et al., 2021). Microbiome studies have highlighted both the existence of a core microbial pool of microorganisms and the connectivity between taxa, with some microbial keystone taxa being drivers of communities (Finkel et al., 2020, Banerjee et al., 2018, Michl et al., 2023). The beneficial effects of resident microorganisms on crop health make beneficial microbiome engineering a powerful potential way to create bioinoculants that replace harmful or ineffective inputs (Busby et al., 2017). Studying the transcriptomic responses of crops to these beneficial bioinoculations or microbiome modifications will highlight specific induced responses that represent useful genetic targets for crop growth, defence or yield enhancement strategies (Bakker et al., 2020). Among the beneficial microorganisms living in the root microbiota, one of the most diverse and studied pools of microorganisms correspond to the plant growth promoting [SOURCE: Page_254]

235 [SOURCE: Page_254]

rhizobacteria (PGPR) (Edwards et al., 2015, Trivedi et al., 2020). The mechanisms of induction of their host's growth have been well studied in a variety of genera and crop species. Nitrogen- fixing rhizobacteria are the most studied, both with nodulating rhizobia or free-living genera such as Azospirillum, Herbaspirillum, Azotobacter or Bradyrhizobium, increasing the nitrogen bioavailable to their hosts (Baldani et al., 1986, Backer et al., 2018, Tonelli et al., 2020). Based on the same mechanism with inorganic phosphate, P-solubilising bacteria are reported to be PGPR, with species such as Bacillus sp. Pseudomonas striata and Paraburkholderia tropica (Backer et al., 2018, Tian et al., 2021). In addition to increasing the nutritional status of their host, PGPR can directly enhance the root growth of their host through the production of phytohormones in their vicinity, such as the production of auxin by Azospirillum or Paraburkholderia kururiensis (Mattos et al., 2008, Vacheron et al., 2013). Their role in abiotic and biotic stress tolerance of their hosts is well studied, with some strains being able to do both: P. phytofirmans has been reported to induce growth and salt tolerance strategies in Arabidopsis, helping it to cope with a virulent Pseudomonas syringae strain (Ledger et al., 2016, Timmermann et al., 2017). Studies on the symbiotic compatibility between AMF and rice have highlighted the role of rice genotype in its mycorrhizal responsiveness. Upland japonica rice seems to be more extensively and efficiently mycorrhized than flooded indica rice (Diedhiou et al., 2016, Guigard et al., (in Front. Plant Sci., in press)). From a more global perspective, AMF has been reported to have a variety of beneficial effects on rice health: increasing shoot and root biomass, mineral uptake, and abiotic and biotic stress tolerance (Gutjahr et al., 2009, Begum et al., 2019, Campo & San Segundo et al., 2020, Guigard et al., (in Front. Plant Sci., in press)). Transcriptomic responses of rice to AMF symbiosis are visible at both the local and systemic scales. Upon establishment of this symbiosis and recognition of specific chitin- derived Myc factors via OsCERK$_1$ and other receptor kinases, calcium oscillations occur in root epidermal cells that are typical of the common symbiosis signalling pathways, inducing a transient local defence response that is rapidly repressed (Zipfel et al., 2017, Huang et al., 2020). The formation and function of arbuscules in cortical root cells then drive sugar, fatty acid, and phosphate and ammonium transporter-related genes locally (Mbodj et al., 2018). This modulation of nutrient transporters is often associated with a localised and specific (i.e. different for each root type) repression of nutrient starvation responses on iron, phosphate and ammonium pathways, which is also visible in leaves (Gutjahr et al., 2015, Campo & San Segundo, 2020, Guigard et al., (in Front. Plant Sci., in press)). Mycorrhization has been reported to have a biological control effect on some associations, as interactions with F. mosseae or R. intraradices reduce the pressure of nematodes and other soil phytopathogens on tomato or maize (Schouteden et al., 2015, Poveda et al., 2020). There are also reports of systemic induction of resistance mediated by [SOURCE: Page_255]

236 [SOURCE: Page_255]

root mycorrhization in Medicago truncatula (70% less Xanthomonas campestris-induced symptoms with R. intraradices, Liu et al., 2007), wheat (F. mosseae against Xanthomonas translucens, Fiorilli et al., 2018) and rice. Indeed, wild or cultivated rice mycorrhized with R. intraradices, R. irregularis or F. mosseae are more tolerant to Pyricularia oryzae infection due to changes in their secondary metabolism and modulation of phytohormonal balances (Tian et al., 2019, Campo et al., 2020). Direct competition for colonisation sites, soil nutrients and local induction of host defence responses are proposed to be responsible for the local biological control potential of AMF (Schouteden et al., 2015, Nishad et al., 2020). Mycorrhiza- induced resistance (MIR) is a systemic phenomenon associated with a priming of host defence responses in leaves (Liu et al., 2007, Fiorilli et al., 2018, Dowarah et al., 2021). It proceeds through a modulation of the phytohormonal balance, starting with the early inhibition of SA- related genes in roots to ensure mycorrhizal colonisation and the activation of JA-related pathways (Poveda et al., 2020, Nishad et al., 2020). Once the interaction is established, there is a long-lasting priming of SA-dependent genes (similar to a systemic acquired resistance (SAR)-type response) and induction of JA-dependent and cell wall thickening defence responses (Jacott et al., 2017). The leaf marker genes of MIR remain to be discovered. The term 'priming' has been described as the acquisition of a memory with a low fitness cost, more robust defence response and better performance in challenged plants, representing a type of immunological memory (Tonelli et al., 2020). This priming of the host defence response is induced by either beneficial rhizobacteria or AMF, but no specific study has been conducted to understand if and in what way ISR and MIR priming pathways are common or specific to the microorganism involved. Since both beneficial rhizobacteria and fungi can improve rice growth and defence against foliar phytopathogens through two induced systemic resistance responses, the hypothesis that their co-inoculation has interesting effects on their host's health is relevant. In particular, it is this hypothesis that new bio-inoculation formulation engineers are investigating: choosing the association between PGPR and AMF depending on their roles and benefits to the plants, testing whether they are additive, synergistic or, in the worst case, detrimental (Begum et al., 2021). However, the effects of co-inoculation depend on the plant host, the symbiotic interaction and the phenotypic trait observed. A study of co-inoculation of an indigenous AMF consortium and two Pseudomonas (P. jessenii & P. synxantha) showed a 41% induction of wheat yield, an increase in both grain nutritional value and an increase in nitrogen and phosphorus assimilation and allocation due to synergistic effects between the symbionts. These effects weren't significant in black gram or rice cultures (Mäder et al., 2011). The tolerance of two Iranian rice cultivars to salt stress was enhanced by the co-occurrence of P. putida, P. fluorescens and R. irregularis, which increased leaf chlorophyll content, catalase activity and proline accumulation, and improved rice growth and crop quality (N [SOURCE: Page_256]

237 [SOURCE: Page_256]

content, yield) (Norouzinia et al., 2020). Co-inoculation between R. intraradices and Pseudomonas synergistically increased the bioavailability of phosphorus and potassium in the soil near rice roots, while their content in rice leaves was also increased, associated with a general induction of biomass parameters (plant height, root length, dry weight) (Chen et al., 2023). While the association of F. mosseae and Paraburkholderia graminis on wheat roots enables its growth and reduces symptoms induced by X. translucens infection, the association of the same AMF species with Azospirillum brasilense induces the opposite effect on symptoms extent (Vannini et al., 2021). While Paraburkholderia strains have been shown to induce plant defence responses against a wide range of phytopathogens, A. brasilense is not a typical biological control agent, but rather a beneficial bacterium that induces plant growth (Esmaeel et al., 2018, Vannini et al., 2021). These results highlight the importance of the choice of the PGPR in terms of its role and the benefits it provides to the plant. Another interesting hypothesis regarding the synergistic effects of AMF and PGPR co- inoculation lies in the benefits they can exchange with each other. Mycorrhizal helper bacteria are strains that either assist in the establishment or functioning of the fungal-root symbiosis through their effects on hyphal germination rate, development and ramification, or through direct plant growth-promoting capabilities (Frey-Klett et al., 2007, Sangwan & Prasanna, 2021). In turn, AMF may help PGPR colonise their host microbiota by exuding specific metabolites or by influencing their host exudation in the rhizosphere (Artursson et al., 2006, Cameron et al., 2013). Nevertheless, an important aspect to consider when dealing with co- inoculation is the effect of PGPR inoculation on AMF root colonisation. The colonisation frequency of a native AMF species on wheat and rice is reduced when inoculated with Pseudomonas (Mäder et al., 2011). In Mercato wheat, the number of arbuscules of R. irregularis, a marker of AMF symbiosis functioning, is decreased when co-inoculated with P. putida (Pérez-de-Luque et al., 2017). Co-inoculation studies between AMF and PGPR in rice are scarce, but they suggest that the symbiotic compatibility between AMF and rice, as well as the selected PGPR, are important factors. There is little interest in the phenotypic and molecular response of their host during this tripartite interaction, either under healthy conditions or under attack by a phytopathogen. How co-inoculation between two microorganisms known to induce ISR or MIR can affect the response of rice to a leaf bacterial phytopathogen is of interest to evaluate its potential value as a bioinoculant. [SOURCE: Page_257]

In this study, the common and specific pathways associated with two induced systemic resistances, originating from either a beneficial endophytic rhizobacteria (ISR) or an AMF species (MIR), were investigated. Analyses were performed on the phenotypic and molecular responses of rice to either Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130, Rhizophagus irregularis [SOURCE: Page_257]

238 [SOURCE: Page_257]

DAOM197198 or their co-inoculation. The effect of bacterial inoculation on the mycorrhizal index and the effects of bacterial, fungal or both inoculations on rice growth parameters and defence against a bacterial leaf phytopathogen, Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae (Xoo), were recorded ten weeks after sowing. Molecular analyses of the transcriptomic responses of rice to the three types of interactions were performed using RNAseq and qRT-PCR on a panel of marker genes for growth, nutrient transport, hormonal pathways and defence responses in leaves with or without Xoo infiltration. [SOURCE: Page_258]

Material and Methods [SOURCE: Page_258]

Plant and microorganism’s material preparation Rhizophagus irregularis DAOM197198 (RIR) was purchased from MycAgro Lab (Technopôle Agro-Environnement, Bretenière, France) in the form of individual granular inoculums (100 spores.g-1). Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 (Pk) is cultured as follows: glycerol stock (20%, -80°C) is plated onto low salt Luria broth agar (LBm) and incubated for 72h at 28°C. Cells are then scraped with 2 mL of sterile osmotic water and diluted to 20 mL in a 50 mL falcon tube, then centrifuged for 10 min at 2,500 g and resuspended again in sterile osmotic water, before diluting it to 108 bacterial cells per mL for inoculation. Oryza sativa ssp. japonica cv. Nipponbare was chosen for this study and prepared as follows. Rice seeds were dehusked and surface-disinfected as explained in Guigard et al. (submitted), by immersion in 70% ethanol for 3 min, then in 3.8% sodium hypochlorite supplemented with 1% Tween 20 under agitation (180 rpm) for 30 min. Seeds were rinsed three times with sterile water, three times with 2% filter-sterilised sodium thiosulfate and three more times with sterile water. They were incubated overnight at 28 °C in sterile water in the dark and then allowed to germinated on sterile-soaked sand in the growth chamber (12 h day/night, 28 °C day, 26 °C night, 75% humidity). To confirm surface disinfection, 100 µL of the last rinse and imbibition waters were plated on tryptic soy agar (Sigma-Aldrich) Petri dishes. After three days of germination, four homogeneous rice seedlings were transferred as explained in Guigard et al., (in Front. Plant Sci., in press) in anti-coiling pots filled with 150 mL of clay beads and 450 mL of sterile inert substrate composed of 70% of sand, 20% sieved perlite and 10% of vermiculite. This substrate was inoculated with RIR granular inoculum, or the granular inoculum without fungal spores (control, CT) at a volume of 5% per pot. Rice seedlings were planted and inoculated with RIR as explained above, in the same growth chamber with the same parameters. After one week of sowing, Pk was inoculated at a rate of 108 bacterial cells per plantlet (control plants were inoculated with 1 mL of sterile water). The substrate was moistened for one week and then watered three times a week with a modified [SOURCE: Page_258]

Hoagland solution, volumes increased as needed. Since non-mycorrhized plants (controls or only with the bacteria) are too weak to properly grow in this substrate (data not shown), we divided this experiment into two subparts. Rice inoculated with the granular inoculum without fungal spores and sterile osmotic water or Pk inoculated were watered with a Hoagland solution containing P (0,5 mM KH2PO$_4$), while mycorrhized rice with or without Pk (and their associated control) were watered with a Hoagland solution with low P source (25 µM KH2PO$_4$). The complete recipe depending on the conducted experiment is available in Supplementary Table 1. [SOURCE: Page_260]

Mycorrhizal quantification Mycorrhizal index quantification is recorded as explained in Guigard et al (submitted). Briefly, root systems of four plants from the same pot were washed in tap water, then placed in 70% ethanol at 4 °C until analysis. Ethanol is replaced with 10% KOH then heated to 80 °C for 45 min. Roots are rinsed three times with ultrapure water (MilliQ) and stained with a 5% blue ink (Waterman “Bleu Sérénité”) in 5% acetic acid at room temperature for 10 min. They are then rinsed three times with ultrapure water and fixed in 5% acetic acid overnight. Five replicates of 20 to 25 fragments from coronary roots were mounted between slide and glass and the mycorrhizal index (global mycorrhization and arbuscular intensity, n= 5) were assessed as in Trouvelot et al., 1986, on an Axiozoom Zeiss microscope. [SOURCE: Page_260]

Phenotypic and transcriptomic analysis of rice biomass and Xoo tolerance assays Rice plants were left to grow for eight weeks before one part was infected with Xoo by leaf-clipping as explained in Guigard et al (submitted). The extent of chlorosis was assessed 14 days post-inoculation (dpi) on each leaf (n= 20). The second part was infiltrated by syringe- pressing against the last fully-developed leaf on three centimeters (DO = 0.5). 24 hours later, 12 healthy and 12 infiltrated leaves per condition were harvested in 2 mL tubes containing 2 metal beads and snap-frozen in liquid nitrogen and stored at -80°C. Leaf-clipping induced symptoms as well as maximum height of plants were recorded 14 days post clipping and rice was left to dry at 46°C. One week later, dry mass was recorded. RNA was extracted from leaf samples collected (healthy or infiltrated by Xoo) and prepared for RNAseq and qPCR analyses. They were ground to a fine powder using a TissueLyser II (Retsch) at 30 Hz, for 15 s twice. Each biological replicate consisted of three leaf samples, with four biological replicates for each condition. Total RNA was extracted using TriReagent (Sigma) and a DNAse (QIAgen) treatment was added in the protocol before purification with the RNA Clean & Concentrator kit (Zymo), according to the manufacturer’s [SOURCE: Page_260]

instructions. The quantity and quality of total RNA was assessed using a NanoDrop 1000 spectrophotometer (ThermoFisher). Approximately 120 ng of total RNA from each biological replicate was used for retro- transcription into cDNA using SuperScript III Reverse Transcriptase (Thermo Fisher Scientific). cDNAs were diluted 6-fold and RT-qPCRs were performed using the Takyon™ Low ROX SYBR 2X MasterMix blue dTTP (Eurogentec) on a LightCycler 480 qPCR system (Roche). Plate preparation was automated using the epMotion 5070 pipetting robot (Eppendorf). Four independent biological replicates were analysed for each condition, each one analysed in triplicate. Relative gene expression is calculated by comparing each sample to the standard’s (number of cycles of EF1a, ΔCt), and then to the control group (Kuru P+ to CT P+; RIR and RIR Kuru to CT P-, ΔΔCt). The fold change is calculated with 2-meanΔΔCt and the logFC represents the relative expression of each marker gene as indicated in Pfaffl, 2001. The list of marker genes, their function and the primers used in this study are presented in Table 1. [SOURCE: Page_262]

Statistical analyses Statistical analyses were performed using Rstudio (version 2022.2.0.443) and R (version 4.1.3) software, using the packages "readxl", "tidyverse", "ggplot^2", "rstatix", "ggpubr", "multicompView", "car", "pcr", "pheatmap". For the statistical comparison of the effect of Pk inoculation on rice long-term growth and biocontrol traits, and comparison of mycorrhizal indexes (global intensity and arbuscule content) between RIR and RIR-Pk inoculation depending on whether the variables fulfilled the hypotheses of normality (Shapiro test, p > 0.05) and homoscedasticity (Levene test, p > 0.05) (maximum leaf height, chlorosis spread) or not (dry shoot and root weights), a Student t-test or a non-parametric Wilcoxon test was performed (α = 0.05). For the statistical comparison of the effect of RIR and RIR-Pk inoculation on rice long-term growth and biocontrol trait, depending on whether the variables fulfilled the normality and homoscedasticity hypotheses (maximum leaf height) or not (dry shoot and root weights, chlorosis spread), a one-way ANOVA with Tukey test for pairwise group comparisons (α = 0. 05) or non-parametric ANOVA with Kruskal-Wallis test (α = 0.05) followed by pairwise comparisons with Wilcoxon tests (α = 0.05) were used for mean group comparisons of measured phenotypic traits. The "pcr" package was used to analyse the expression of rice marker genes. Student t-tests were used to assess statistical differences between bacterial inoculation and marker gene expression. To visualise the relative expression of each marker gene as a function of rice genotype and AMF inoculation, a heat map of LogFC was generated using the "pheatmap" package. [SOURCE: Page_262]

Results [SOURCE: Page_264]

Co-inoculation          of    Rhizophagus            irregularis      DAOM197198              with Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 affects rice mycorrhization Colonisation pattern and rate of the single inoculation of Rhizophagus irregularis DAOM197198 (RIR) or the co-inoculation with the wild-type Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 (Pk) are visible in Figure 1 and Table 2. As already observed (Guigard et al., in prep), Pk is capable of colonising rice roots efficiently during five weeks in an inert substrate. In terms of mycorrhization index, RIR also colonises efficiently rice roots, with a 47.42% of global colonisation intensity, 13.63% of it containing arbuscules (Figure 1A, Table 2). It doesn’t seem to have any severe cross contamination since no non-inoculated rice roots were mycorrhized and only very few hyphal fragments were visible in two P. kururiensis-inoculated roots replicates (global intensity << 0.1%, Table 2). The co-inoculation of RIR with Pk decreases both indices, in a non-statistically significant manner (32,34% of global intensity, with 3% of arbuscular content, p-value = 0,151; 0,0952 respectively, Table 2). All mycorrhizal organs were observed in both conditions, i.e. hyphal structures, vesicles and arbuscules (Figures 1B & 1C). [SOURCE: Page_264]

Long term interaction with two beneficial microorganisms: effect on rice growth and defence against a leaf phytopathogen phenotypes To assess how rice growth is affected by each type of inoculation, maximum height and both shoot and root dry weights were recorded after eight weeks. Results are visible in Figure 2 and Table 3. There is no significant effect of Pk inoculation on rice growth phenotypic traits (Figure 2A, C, E). The inoculation of RIR alone increases rice maximum leaf height and dry shoot weight by 17,6% (27,4 cm compared with 23,3 cm) and 31,6% (0,200 g compared with 0,152 g) respectively (Table 3). The co-inoculation of RIR and Pk significantly increases rice growth too, by 15,8% and 30,9% respectively (Figure 2 and Table 3). Chlorosis spread on the infected leaf is significantly reduced in both single inoculations: Pk reduces Xoo-induced chlorosis symptoms by 33% (3,30 cm compared with 4,36 cm) and RIR by 62% (5,78 cm compared with 15,3 cm) (Table 3). The co inoculation reduces by 41% chlorosis spread on the leaf but without statistical significance (p-value = 0.11). [SOURCE: Page_264]
