Impact of AMF and bacterial inoculation on rice systemic transcriptomics To further understand the impact of mycorrhization and its co-inoculation with a beneficial bacteria on rice health, the expression of 24 marker genes related to development, nutritional status, phytohormonal balance, defence and microorganisms (listed in Table 1) was investigated in healthy or diseased leaves. Figure 3 shows a heatmap of the relative expression of rice health and development marker genes in leaves of plants inoculated with R. irregularis alone or co-inoculated with P. kururiensis, in a healthy state or 24 h after infiltration with Xoo. They are compared with the response of their respective control (non-Pk inoculated plants infiltrated with Xoo). Their absolute level compared to the respective control (plants not inoculated with R. irregularis and Pk) is shown in Supplementary Figures 1, 2 & 3. [SOURCE: Page_266]

The expression of AMF and bacteria-related marker genes was investigated using OsWRKY$_7$, OsSHR$_5$ and OsPT11. The two first are associated with Pyricularia oryzae tolerance and interaction with diazotrophic beneficial bacteria respectively (Tun et al., 2023, King et al., 2019), while the third, an inorganic phosphate transporter known to be induced in mycorrhizal roots, has been shown to be modulated in Nipponbare leaves by AMF symbiosis (Gutjahr et al., 2008, Guigard et al., (in Front. Plant Sci., in press)). Microorganism-modulated genes were not significantly induced in leaves of both RIR and RIR-Pk healthy plants, whereas they were repressed in Xoo-infiltrated leaves (significant in RIR rice, Supplementary Figures 1 & 2). The inorganic phosphate transporter OsPT11 was significantly repressed in both RIR and RIR-Pk diseased rice leaves (Figure 3). All three genes expression was significantly induced by Xoo infiltration in control plants (Figure 3, Supplementary Figure 3). [SOURCE: Page_266]

The growth induction of rice in both interacting conditions was investigated by examining the expression of two cellular growth marker genes. OsYABBY$_6$ is responsible for leaf cell growth (Jiang et al., 2016) and OsXTH17 is involved in primary cell wall formation (Lin et al., 2019). Under healthy conditions, their expression wasn't affected by inoculation with either RIR or RIR-Pk. However, after infiltration with Xoo, their expression was repressed in both conditions (only significantly in RIR rice leaves, Figure 3 and Supplementary Figures 1 & 2). In control plants infected by Xoo, significant modulation of growth-related genes expression were noted (up-regulation for OsYABBY$_6$, down-regulation for OsXTH17) (Supplementary Figure 3). [SOURCE: Page_266]

The nutrient status of nitrogen, phosphorus and iron elements was studied thanks to the respective transporters or regulators: OsNIA$_1$; OsMGD$_2$, OsPAP23 and OsSPX$_3$; OsIRO$_2$ (Tang et al., 2012, Secco et al., 2013, Ogo et al., 2006). In RIR-associated healthy rice leaves, [SOURCE: Page_266]

247 [SOURCE: Page_266]

Figure 3: Effect of the interaction with R. irregularis alone or associated with P. kururiensis on the relative expression of rice marker genes related to defence, hormonal balances, nutrition, development and microorganism’s presence in Nipponbare leaves, before and after a Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae infection. R. irregularis, R. irregularis + P. kururiensis healthy and Xoo-infiltrated uninoculated rice leaves were compared to healthy uninoculated rice leaves gene expression. Diseased R. irregularis, R. irregularis + P. kururiensis were compared with diseased uninoculated rice leaves gene expression. Transcript levels were normalised to that of the reference gene EF1a. Log^2 fold change values are displayed in green (negative), grey (-0.5 < x < 0.5) and red (positive) (n= 3). SA = salicylic acid. ET = ethylene. JA = jasmonic acid [SOURCE: Page_267]

248 [SOURCE: Page_267]

the expression of all genes was not significantly induced, but was statistically repressed during Xoo infection (Figure 3, Supplementary Figure 2). In rice leaves co-inoculated with the AMF and the beneficial bacteria, the same pattern can be seen, although to a lesser extent (significant repression of only OsNIA$_1$’s expression). In infected control plants, the expression of OsSPX$_3$ was significantly induced while the other nutrient-related ones weren’t significantly affected (Figure 3, Supplementary Figure 3). [SOURCE: Page_268]

Both mycorrhization and association with a beneficial bacterium have been reported to modulate rice phytohormone balances, particularly jasmonate, ethylene and salicylic acid. OsAOS$_1$, related to JA biosynthesis, OsJAZ$_6$ and OsJamyb, reported in JA signalling (Yokotani et al., 2012, Lu et al., 2016, King et al, 2019), OsACS$_1$, the 1-aminocyclopropane- 1-carboxylate synthase responsible for ET biosynthesis (Petitot et al., 2017), and OsNPR$_1$ and OsWRKY45, which mediate SA biosynthesis and signalling respectively (Kumari et al., 2016) were chosen. OsNPR$_1$ was the only marker gene that was significantly repressed by both RIR and RIR-Pk inoculations in healthy rice leaves. In Xoo diseased leaves of plants inoculated with RIR, OsJAZ$_6$, OsMYC$_2$, OsAOS$_1$ and OsNPR$_1$ were repressed, whereas no significant modulation was visible in the RIR-Pk diseased condition (Figure 3). Although not statistically significant, two switches were interesting: the strong induction of OsJamyb switched to repression in diseased leaves of RIR rice, whereas the repression of OsNPR$_1$ and OsWRKY45 in healthy leaves of RIR-Pk switched to strong induction of both gene expression when Xoo was infiltrated (Figure 3, Supplementary Figures 1 & 2). In Xoo infected plants, a modulation of jasmonate pathway (up-regulation of OsMYC$_2$ and down-regulation of OsJamyb expressions) with an up-regulation of SA-associated genes expression was noted. [SOURCE: Page_268]

In terms of defence expression, the effect of RIR and RIR-Pk inoculation was investigated using seven marker genes. OsWRKY30 and OsMPK10 are responsible for early disease-mediated signalling (Ryu et al., 2006, Nguyen et al., 2014). OsPBZ$_1$, OsPR1a and OsPR$_5$ are pathogenesis-related proteins, while OsPAL$_4$ and OsDXS$_3$ are responsible for broad-spectrum disease resistance and phytoalexin production in rice, respectively (Deiteil et al., 2012, Petitot et al., 2017, Okada et al., 2009, Valette et al., 2020). OsPR$_5$ was significantly induced in healthy leaves of RIR-associated rice, whereas all defence marker genes were significantly repressed in Xoo-infected leaves of RIR rice, except OsDXS$_3$. In healthy leaves of co-inoculated rice, both OsDXS$_3$ and OsPAL$_4$ were significantly downregulated, whereas in diseased leaves almost all marker genes were repressed (although not statistically significantly) (Figure 3, Supplementary Figures 1 & 2). Finally, in diseased leaves of control plants, a modulation of defence was seen (down-regulation of OsPR1a with an up-regulation of OsWRKY30 and OsPR$_5$ expressions) (Supplementary Figure 3). [SOURCE: Page_268]

249 [SOURCE: Page_268]

Discussion [SOURCE: Page_269]

Rice interaction with R. irregularis alone has more beneficial effects on its growth and tolerance to Xoo than when co-inoculated with Pk In this study, the effect of two beneficial rice endophytes, Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 and Rhizophagus irregularis DAOM197198, or their co-inoculation on Nipponbare roots was characterised on both phenotypic and molecular traits. The inoculation of Pk on RIR early mycorrhized rice roots leads to a reduced mycorrhizal index (global intensity and arbuscular content) (Figure 1A). The sole inoculation of RIR induces a significant improvement in plant height and shoot dry weight on the observed traits, whereas the inoculation of Pk doesn't (Figure 2). Co-inoculation with Pk and RIR also increases rice height and shoot dry weight, but to a lesser extent than RIR alone (Figure 2B & D). A clear reduction in Xoo-induced symptoms is induced by both single inoculations, while the RIR-Pk condition also reduces chlorosis, although not statistically significantly (Figure 2G, H; Table 3). Recent literature showing interest in co-inoculation between known PGPR and AMF species on a variety of crop hosts has highlighted their additive or synergistic beneficial effects. Wheat growth and mycorrhization index were both induced when co-inoculated with beneficial native Pseudomonas, associated with better yield and grain content parameters (Roesti et al., 2006, Mäder et al., 2011). Chen et al, 2023, when investigating the beneficial additive effects of different bacterial inoculations on rice mycorrhization, highlighted their potential role in improving soil fertility through their activity on soil pH and organic matter decomposition, leading to synergistic effects on rice growth and yield parameters. Kavadia et al., 2021, explained that only co-inoculation between a Sinorhizobium meliloti and three different AMF species addressed N deficiency in cowpea. It has been reported that AMF hyphae recruit phosphate solubilising bacteria (PSB) as part of their mechanisms to improve the P status of their plant host (Zhang et al., 2018). It is not surprising that PSB such as Bacillus subtilis co- inoculated with AMF improve P uptake, soil fertility, and growth and yield of maize, Medicago sativa, Artemisa annua or even Solanum lycopersicum (Nanjundappa et al., 2019, Wahid et al., 2020). Co-inoculation between PGPR and AMF helps their host to tolerate abiotic stresses such as salinity or heavy metal contamination. Rhizophagus irregularis has already been reported to recruit cadmium (Cd)-absorbing Arthrobacter in the rice rhizosphere (Chen et al., 2019). In a study on the tripartite interaction between alfalfa, S. meliloti and Glomus mosseae in Cd-contaminated soil, co-inoculation helped the host to mitigate Cd-induced lipid peroxidation and reactive oxygen species (ROS) stress by increasing the activities of antioxidant enzymes (superoxide dismutase, catalase and peroxidase) and enriching the [SOURCE: Page_269]

250 [SOURCE: Page_269]

rhizosphere microbial community for certain beneficial bacterial taxa (Wang et al., 2021). In two rice cultivars under salt stress, co-inoculation with Pseudomonas putida, P. fluorescens and R. irregularis increased antioxidant enzyme activities and proline accumulation, while improving rice growth and yield (Norouzinia et al., 2020). These results were also observed in maize inoculated with the same AMF species in contact with a salt stress-associated bacteriome (Chen et al., 2022). In tobacco under drought stress, the same mechanisms were synergistically induced with co-inoculation of G. versiforme and Bacillus methylotrophicus (greater maintenance of tobacco mycorrhization, growth, nutrient uptake, photosynthesis, antioxidant activities associated with reduced lipid peroxidation and electrolyte leakage, Begum et al., 2022). Finally, Xun et al. 2015 reported that in petroleum-contaminated soils, co-inoculation of Avena sativa with a petroleum-native Acinetobacter sp. and a G. intraradices induced better oat biomass production and tolerance (increased antioxidant enzyme activities, decreased proline and malondialdehyde levels), while increasing soil enzymatic activity and petroleum degradation rate. Co-inoculation between PGPR and AMF has also been reported to increase their host tolerance to biotic stresses. Oil palm seedlings inoculated with native AMF species and B. subtilis showed reduced disease severity index against Ganoderma boninense, an important fungal pathogen of this crop species (Bakhtiar et al., 2012). Co-inoculation of black gram with G. intraradices and Rhizobium sp. BMBS increases its tolerance to Scodoptera litura attack, most likely due to their improved phenolic content and defensive responses in the leaves (Selvaraj et al., 2020). The phenolic compounds present in soybean exudates, increased by their interaction with Bradyrhizobium sp. and G. mosseae, reduced the severity and incidence of Cylindrocladium parasiticum (Gao et al., 2012). However, these beneficial effects on host health are mostly dependent on the host and inoculant species: Mäder et al., 2011 explained that the beneficial effects of co-inoculation with wheat-associated bacteria and AMF weren't as beneficial for black gram and rice growth. Co-inoculation of cowpea with S. meliloti and three AMF species (Dominikia disticha, Claroideoglomus etunicatum and/or R. irregularis) increases plant biomass but depends on the fungal species, with some combinations having no significant effect when combined with the bacteria (Kavadia et al., 2021). As seen in our study, bacterial inoculation had a significant effect on mycorrhizal colonisation, depending on the combination, e.g. the multi-AMF species inoculant reduced total colonisation. The same study found no correlation between plant colonisation rate and synergistic effects of co-inoculation on plant growth traits. While co- inoculation with F. mosseae and either Paraburkholderia graminis or Azospirillum brasilense showed similar improvement in wheat biomass as F. mosseae inoculated plants, but increased compared to bacterial inoculation alone, both co-inoculations don't induce the same wheat response to Xanthomonas translucens infection. While P. graminis - F. mosseae plants [SOURCE: Page_270]

251 [SOURCE: Page_270]

achieved a similar reduction as plants inoculated with AMF alone, A. brasilense co-inoculated plants showed no significant beneficial effect on symptom reduction (Vannini et al., 2021). Overall, this lack of predictability of co-inoculation outcomes highlights the need to understand the mechanisms involved in each microorganism's ability to promote plant growth and how they interact with each other in a defined set-up as well as under field conditions. [SOURCE: Page_271]

Interactions between Pk and RIR: competition or antagonism? The result of bacterial inoculation on mycorrhizal roots affecting global intensity and arbuscular content can be found in the literature. Mäder et al., 2011 state that co-inoculation reduces fungal colonisation of rice roots by 13% compared to AMF alone. The co-inoculation of G. versiforme, Azotobacter chrococcum and P. fluorescens induces the best mycorrhizal colonisation of roots, associated with the best improvement in potato biomass production. Potatoes inoculated only with G. versiforme also have higher biomass, but co-inoculation between AMF and one of the PGPRs leads to a significantly lower effect on this trait (Kavatagi & Lakshman, 2014). Chen et al., 2022 explained that the addition of salt-stress adapted bacteriomes to R. irregularis mycorrhized maize roots induced a higher degree of total colonisation intensity on the roots, with a lower arbuscule content, possibly explained by the increase in nutrient availability induced by the bacterial inoculation or a change in energy allocation to the fungi. The colonisation of Nipponbare roots by R. irregularis is significantly reduced by P. fluorescens and B. subtilis, but their association triggers a better reduction of symptoms induced by P. oryzae infection, although both bacterial inoculations have the best effect on this particular trait (Ashnei, 2019). This reduction in mycorrhizal colonisation and the beneficial effects on rice growth and tolerance to Xoo infection of the co-inoculation between Pk and RIR could be due to specific competition between the two beneficial microorganisms for rice colonisation sites (both are endophytic microorganisms) or nutrients in the substrate. As explained earlier, the substrate used is nutrient poor to enhance the recruitment of beneficial fungi and bacteria from rice roots. However, while these conditions are sufficient to establish symbiosis when inoculated singly, they may trigger their competition for nutrients when inoculated together. In addition to this, Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 is known to have the ability to produce several antimicrobial substances, such as putrescine and 4-hydroxybenzoate (Dias et al., 2019). These antifungal and antibacterial substances could be responsible for the reduction of AMF colonisation in rice roots, in a competition between them. On the other hand, the AMF could also prevent the growth and development of bacterial endophytes in rice roots through its own exudation, filtering its own associated microbiota, or through its interaction and effects on its host exudation, instead of recruiting them as suggested in synergistic co-inoculations [SOURCE: Page_271]

252 [SOURCE: Page_271]

(Artursson et al., 2006, Cameron et al., 2013, Basiru et al., 2023). It would be interesting to assess bacterial colonisation rates on rice roots with and without pre-inoculation with R. irregularis, using fluorescent bacterial strains (as in King et al., 2019, Guigard et al., in prep). Different patterns and intensities of bacterial colonisation could help us to understand the nature of the interaction between the two micro-organisms. [SOURCE: Page_272]

Transcriptomic changes in rice physiology upon mycorrhization or co- inoculation with a beneficial endophyte In this study, another goal was to understand the common and specific molecular responses triggers of MIR and ISR (reported in Guigard et al., (in Front. Plant Sci., in press) & in prep) verified in Figure 2. The molecular systemic response of Nipponbare to RIR and RIR-Pk inoculations revealed interesting expression patterns. Rice marker genes for growth, nutrient status, hormonal balance and defence responses weren't significantly affected by either inoculation condition. In RIR-inoculated healthy rice leaves, only a repression of the SA- related gene OsNPR$_1$ and an induction of the expression of the pathogenesis-related gene OsPR$_5$ were observed. In the co-inoculated healthy rice leaves, the repression of OsNPR$_1$ is still notable, coupled with a repression of the expression of OsPAL$_4$ and OsDXS$_3$, which are required for the broad-spectrum disease resistance response and phytoalexin production in rice (Figure 3). In the case of RIR, there is a clear but non-significant induction of JA and ET- related genes (in particular OsJamyb), which isn't the case in the co-inoculated rice leaves (Figure 3). Campos-Soriano et al. (2012) found an up-regulation of the expression of OsNPR$_1$, OsJamyb and OsAOC in rice leaves inoculated with G. intraradices. Upon AMF recognition in roots, defence and SA-related pathways are reported to be induced and rapidly repressed during mycorrhizal establishment to ensure proper mycorrhization (Jung et al., 2012, Kadam et al., 2020). JA- and ET-related pathways are also reported to play an important role in the functioning and maintenance of the AMF symbiosis, as well as it's associated systemic induction of defence against secondary infection by pathogens (Jung et al., 2012). Looking at the molecular changes in leaves when inoculated with a Xoo, almost all the selected marker genes were significantly repressed in leaves of RIR-associated rice, whereas only the expression of OsPT11 and OsNIA$_1$, a phosphate and nitrogen transporter related gene, was repressed in co-inoculated rice (Figure 3). There is no clear effect of primed defence against Xoo, which could be responsible for the mycorrhiza-induced resistance (MIR) phenotype seen in Figure 2. MIR is known to be associated with pathogenesis-related and defence-related genes, such as in tomato or Medicago truncatula against A. solani or X. campestris (Liu et al., 2007, Kadam et al., 2020). Nevertheless, a non-significant induction of OsNPR$_1$, OsWRKY30 and OsWRKY45 expressions, which are related to SA biosynthesis [SOURCE: Page_272]

253 [SOURCE: Page_272]

and signalling as well as early defence response signalling, was observed in our data. It is generally believed that biotrophic pathogens (such as Xoo) are more susceptible to SA-related defence induction, which could be induced by co-inoculation of RIR and Pk (Campos-Soriano et al., 2012). It should be noted that these analyses were performed after 24 h of Xoo challenge, whereas the observed MIR phenotypes were recorded at 14 d post-inoculation (Figure 2). For Gao et al. (2012), visible effects of mycorrhization on the expression of pathogenesis-related proteins, and in particular PAL expression, were observed mainly after 3 days of C. parasiticum infection on co-inoculated soybean. Similarly, the expression of OsPR1a and OsPR$_5$ was repressed after 24 h of P. oryzae infection, but strongly induced after 3 days (Campos-Soriano et al., 2012). The induced mechanisms may not yet be observable due to the single time point we chose. A more thorough analysis of the kinetics of rice response to Xoo infiltration in both RIR and RIR-Pk conditions should be performed. On the other hand, an RNAseq analysis is currently being performed to evaluate unknown transcriptomic mechanisms that couldn't be predicted and are actually induced by RIR or RIR- Pk inoculations. [SOURCE: Page_273]

Conclusion In this work, the phenotypic and molecular responses of rice to inoculation with two beneficial endophytes, in single or co-inoculation, were investigated. We have highlighted that R. irregularis provides the best benefits in terms of Nipponbare growth compared to co- inoculation with P. kururiensis or bacterial inoculation alone. This could be explained by the fact that Pk inoculation reduces RIR mycorrhizal colonisation on rice roots, both in intensity and function. More work should be done to clearly understand their interaction in planta, which is suggested to be antagonistic. The phenotypes of both MIR and ISR are clearly visible in the reduction of chlorosis symptoms of Xoo infection on rice leaves. The co-inoculation of RIR and Pk shows signs of ISR, as it reduces the extent of Xoo chlorosis on infected leaves, but in a non-statistically significant manner. In order to understand the molecular mechanisms involved, early defence responses were examined 24 h after Xoo infiltration. In healthy leaves, phytohormonal modulations of RIR-inoculated leaves indicated the establishment and functioning of the AMF symbiosis. However, the expected clear priming of defence responses in Xoo-inoculated leaves of RIR and RIR-Pk inoculated rice was not observed. Instead, RIR interaction leads to a general down-regulation of our selected marker genes whereas co-induction leads to a non-significant SA-related pathway induction. This time point gives us an indication to go deeper into this topic with a broad and kinetic approach. This will unravel in details the common and specific molecular strategies involved in MIR and ISR. [SOURCE: Page_273]

254 [SOURCE: Page_273]

265 [SOURCE: Page_284]

Complementary results: Tendencies of bacterial inoculation to interfere with mycorrhized rice growth phenotype To complement this study, a compatibility screen was carried out between six bacterial strains, one rice cultivar (Nipponbare) and two arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi with different mycorrhizal colonisation intensities on this particular rice. We selected Funneliformis mosseae FR140 (FM), an AMF species reported to be a weak partner with the japonica rice Nipponbare, and its highly compatible partner Rhizophagus irregularis DAOM197198 (RIR) (Guigard et al., 2023). We inoculated the substrate with spores of one of the two AMF species (or granular inoculum for the control), allowed the rice to grow for one week and then inoculated the bacterial strains. We selected the known rice growth-promoting bacteria Pseudomonas defensor WCS374r (ISR inducer in rice, de Vleesschauwer et al., 2008), Bradyrhizobium ORS278, Azospirillum sp. B510, Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 and P. phytofirmans PsJN (PGPR capabilities, including on rice, Chaintreuil et al., 2000, Isawa et al., 2010, King et al., 2019 & 2023) and the rice root phytopathogen Burkholderia plantarii ATC43733 (Solis et al., 2006). After eight weeks of growth, rice height (cm) was measured and plants were harvested. Dry shoot weight per plant (g) was recorded after one week of drying at 46°C. Phenotypic results can be seen on Complementary Figure 4.1. [SOURCE: Page_285]

Complementary Figure 4.1.: Bacterial screen and their effect on mycorrhized rice’s phenotypic traits. Comparison of the impact of bacterial inoculation of five strains of known PGPRs and one phytopathogenic strain on mycorrhization effect by Funneliformis mosseae (A, C) and Rhizophagus irregularis (B, D) of Nipponbare rice roots on phenotypic traits (maximum leaf height (A, B) in cm, dry shoot weight (C, D) in g). Letters above boxplots indicate statistical groups according to the results of the Kruskal Wallis coupled with a Dunn test (p value < 0.05) (n= 24 minimum) [SOURCE: Page_285]

266 [SOURCE: Page_285]

Here, we faced both AMF species during their symbiosis with Nipponbare to a diversity of bacteria to see how their host growth would be affected by their co-inoculation. Due to the high number of replicates and conditions, there are no statistically significant differences between inoculation types for rice height or dry shoot weight (g) (Complementary Figure 4.1.). However, interesting trends can still be seen. First, we can still observe that FM doesn't induce rice growth (in agreement with results in Guigard et al, 2023), while the height and weight of rice is greater in RIR-mycorrhized than in controls (Complementary Figure 4.1). Inoculation with Azospirillum sp. B510 seems to induce even more growth in RIR-mycorrhized rice, while reducing that associated with FM. The opposite is observed with other known PGPRs, namely Pseudomonas defensor, Paraburkholderia phytofirmans and P. kururiensis (Complementary Figure 4.1.). On the other hand, the association of FM with the phytopathogen seems to increase the mean phenotypic characteristics of its host even more. Our bacterial inoculation screen highlights a rather interesting phenotype. The reported ISR inducer P. kururiensis strain seems to be (non-significantly) detrimental to rice growth when co-inoculated with the highly compatible RIR, but beneficial with the incompatible FM. These results highlight the fact that tripartite or, on a larger scale, multipartite interactions can have quite unexpected results depending on the microbial association inoculated on rice roots. [SOURCE: Page_286]

Chapter 6: General discussion Résumé de la discussion [SOURCE: Page_288]

Dans ce dernier chapitre, les principaux résultats de ma thèse sont discutés. Dans ce manuscrit, je voulais mettre en lumière les réponses, communes et spécifiques, phénotypiques et moléculaires, du riz au cours d’interaction avec des microorganismes bénéfiques. Au global, nous avons pu observer que les changements phénotypiques induits par l’interaction avec une bactérie rhizobénéfique (PGPR) ou un champignon mycorhizien à arbuscules (AMF) dépendait à la fois du mode de culture de ces derniers (chapitre 2), du génotype du riz et de l’espèce microbienne utilisée (chapitres 3 et 4), ainsi que des interactions qu’ils entretiennent entre eux ou avec leur milieu (chapitre 5 et résultats complémentaires des chapitres 2 et 4). Les bactéries les plus adaptées à la colonisation du riz induisent des réponses transcriptionnelles plus fortes que E. coli et S. maltophilia, allant jusqu’à induire une résistance systémique induite (ISR) contre une attaque par une bactérie phytopathogène (chapitres 2 et 3). La compatibilité symbiotique entre plusieurs génotypes de riz et d’AMF détermine les effets bénéfiques observés sur la croissance du riz, ses changements physiologiques et sa tolérance à des stress biotiques (chapitre 4). Cependant, ces effets bénéfiques peuvent être diminués par l’interaction de l’AMF avec les microorganismes environnants les racines de riz, comme c’est le cas avec la bactérie endophytique Paraburkholderia kururiensis dans le cas de l’association symbiotique entre Rhizophagus irregularis et Nipponbare (chapitre 5). Les facteurs déterminants de ces compatibilités symbiotiques ne sont pour l’instant que faiblement étudiés et compris. Il serait intéressant d’étudier les réponses réciproques de microorganismes à la présence du riz et les systèmes génétiques régissant leur interaction, comme il a été fait pour Pk par Coutinho et al. (2015) ou sur plusieurs modèles de Burkholderia par Wallner et al. (2023). En effet, ces études ont mis en lumière les différents mécanismes moléculaires nécessaires à la colonisation du riz, ainsi que les mécanismes bénéfiques potentiellement impliqués lors de leur interaction. La combinaison des études transcriptomiques du riz avec ses partenaires bactériens ou fongiques, par des approches de dual RNAseq pourra nous donner une vision plus globale des interactions plante-microorganisme bénéfique. Dans nos analyses transcriptomiques (RNAseq en chapitre 3, RT qPCR en chapitres 2, 4 et 5), les réponses du riz à plusieurs types d’interaction ont été décryptées. De ces analyses nous pouvons tirer un état transcriptomique, local ou systémique, des réponses du riz à une interaction bénéfique. Quel(s) que soi(en)t le(s) microorganisme(s) impliqué(s), des phénomènes d’induction de croissance et de répression de réponses de carence en [SOURCE: Page_288]

269 [SOURCE: Page_288]

nutriments et de défenses sont observées (chapitre 2, 4 et 5). Cependant, le chapitre 3 met en lumière le fait que ces réponses globales ne représentent qu’une petite partie des gènes impliqués dans les réponses du riz, comme précédemment retrouvé dans la littérature (Drogue et al., 2014, Valette et al., 2020, King et al., 2023). Ainsi, nous avons pu mettre en lumière que cet état transcriptionnel global de réponse à un microorganisme bénéfique passe par des mécanismes moléculaires spécifiques à chaque microorganisme (homéostasie du fer et de l’azote pour les feuilles de riz inoculés avec Azospirillum sp. B510, croissance et homéostasie du phosphate dans les racines de riz en association avec Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278 ou encore les différences notables d’expression des gènes marqueurs sélectionnés dans les chapitres 2, 4 et 5 entre Pk, RIR ou leur co-inoculation). Dans cette thèse, tous les microorganismes bénéfiques étudiés sont à même d’induire une résistance systémique (ISR) chez le riz, contre l’infection foliaire de Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99 (ISR dans les chapitres 2 3 et 5, résistance induite par la mycorhization, MIR dans les chapitres 4 et 5). Les mécanismes moléculaires impliqués dans cette ISR ou cette MIR ont été investigués à 24 heures post-infiltration. Aucune modification significative des voies reportées de l’ISR (jasmonate et éthylène, ainsi que les réponses de défenses) n’a été observées dans ces échantillons (chapitres 2 et 5). Cette temporalité n’est donc pas adéquate pour l’étude de la mise en place de la résistance systémique induite par un microorganisme bénéfique chez le riz, du moins dans nos conditions. Une étude complémentaire en cinétique plus fine pourrait permettre de déceler le timing et les mécanismes généraux ou spécifiques de l’ISR ou d’une MIR. [SOURCE: Page_289]

270 [SOURCE: Page_289]

My thesis is in line with current agronomic concerns about how to reduce the use of chemical and environmentally damaging inputs with the addition of sustainable, resilient approaches. In my bibliographic review, I highlighted the beneficial effects of bioinoculation, where some microbes are able to stimulate plant growth and/or control phytopathogens. It is well known that symbiotic associations are highly dependent on crop variety, microbial species and the surrounding environment. In order to increase the durability of the bioinoculant in the soil and the specificity of the interaction with its host, more studies should be considered on the specificity of the interaction between the crops of interest and the candidates, both on phenotypic and molecular markers, during single or co-inoculation. In this thesis, the main focus was on the phenotypic and molecular responses of rice during its interaction with a variety of beneficial microorganisms. I wanted to correlate the beneficial effects on rice growth or tolerance to biotic stress, induced by root inoculation, with systemic molecular markers on rice leaves. Several different types of interaction were tested, from bacteria to fungi, from single associations to co-inoculation. My first study was on the assessment of the effect of a rice-isolated beneficial endophyte on rice phenotypes of growth and tolerance to a phytopathogen infection, depending on the type of bacterial inoculation. We selected Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130, previously reported in our laboratory to affect rice transcriptomics and yield (King et al., 2019 & 2023). In preliminary results in our lab, Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99 (Xoo) was reported to be less virulent on rice leaves if cultured in a liquid culture rather than in solid media. This was also reported for Pseudomonas aeruginosa strains (Granato et al., 2018). Xoo virulence, as other phytopathogens such as Xyllela fastidiosa, partly passes through the production of exopolysaccharides (EPS), more abundant in solid media than liquid cultures (Niño-Liu et al., 2006, Killiny et al., 2013). We wondered if this effect on pathogenic strains capacity to colonise and persist in rice depending on their method of culture was shared with beneficial bacteria. Doing so, we investigated Pk ‘s impact on rice phenotypic traits depending on the type of growth culture of the bacteria (from a solid medium or liquid culture). Recent studies in our laboratory have shown that three phylogenetically close Burkholderia sensu lato, reported for their PGPR abilities, modulate distinctly rice transcriptomic signatures, in both roots and shoots (King et al., 2019 & 2023). However, transcriptomic responses of rice to a diversity of bacteria are still lacking in the literature. To decipher common and specific rice transcriptomic responses during PGPR interaction, we performed a fine RNAseq analysis on roots and shoots of rice plants initially inoculated with a variety of bacteria, depending on their degree of adaptation to plants, and to rice in particular. We also assessed their individual effects on rice growth, yield and tolerance to phytopathogen infection after several weeks of interaction in complementary results. [SOURCE: Page_290]

271 [SOURCE: Page_290]

Still on the topic of specific interaction with a diversity of beneficial microbes, the symbiotic compatibility between six rice cultivars and three AMF species was studied on rice root colonisation, phenotypic parameters and molecular marker gene expression. From this study, the best rice-AMF pair was selected in terms of rice growth induction and biotic stress reduction potential: the japonica rice Nipponbare mycorrhized with Rhizophagus irregularis DAOM197198. This pair was used in the last chapter of my thesis to try to decipher the common and specific transcriptomic responses of rice in interaction with a single bacterial (Pk); fungal association (AMF); or in co-inoculation. [SOURCE: Page_291]

Interplay between rice root colonisation, inoculant type and effects on rice phenotype From all my experimental chapters, one main conclusion can be drawn: the outcome of the interaction between rice and a bacterial or fungal beneficial microbe depends on the cultivar and/or microbial species involved, the method of inoculation and the symbiotic compatibility between the partners. In Chapter 2, we found that neither solid-media nor liquid- cultured Paraburkholderia kururiensis M130 had a significant effect on the growth and yield parameters tested on Nipponbare, while exhibiting similar colonisation behaviour on rice roots. However, only rice inoculated with the solid-media-grown Pk showed a clear significant reduction in Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99 infection compared to the control plants. Our main hypothesis for these results is that the bacterial growth method has a strong influence on Pk development, metabolism and enzymatic activities. Pk grown in solid media doesn't lead to a better colonisation of rice roots compared to liquid culture cells, but rather establishes a specific interaction with rice roots capable of triggering an induced systemic resistance (ISR) in its host leaves infected by a biotrophic phytopathogen. There are some specific pathways that can be enriched in solid-grown media compared to liquid-cultured ones and vice versa (Zeroual et al., 1994, Marra et al., 2011 & 2012). P. aeruginosa strains were reported to be less virulent when originating from unstructured media compared with structured media ones (Granato et al., 2018). The production of EPS by Xyllela fastidiosa and Xoo, which is increased in solid growth media, is responsible for biofilm formation and virulence in susceptible plants (Killiny et al., 2013, Ham & Kim, 2018). Nevertheless, there are reports that EPS-producing bacteria are PGPRs, thanks to their assistance in nutrient homeostasis or maintenance of soil moisture under drought conditions (Nassem et al., 2018). The more abundant EPS production in solid media could alter the MAMP associated with the metabolism of bacteria (pathogens or PGPR), triggering a different response. Further analysis of these rice-associated PGPR activities, at a transcriptomic or metabolic level, comparing Pk [SOURCE: Page_291]

272 [SOURCE: Page_291]

from liquid or solid cultures with rice extracts, should be carried out to understand the effect of the growth culture type on Pk mutualism. In the complementary results from Chapter 3, when looking at the phenotypic effects of our bacterial diversity on various rice health parameters, there was no clear correlation between the degree of plant adaptation and growth induction. In fact, Escherichia coli and Stenotrophomonas maltophilia rice roots colonisation ranges at a similar level than plant- adapted bacteria at seven days post inoculation, before being among the three highest colonising strains at 14 dpi. However, they did not have significative effects on the local and systemic transcriptomics of rice, both in terms of the number of DEGs and the strength of modulation. They were selected for their less obvious plant adaptation compared with the other strains but do have a significant effect on rice growth and yield parameters. E. coli reduced both the height and weight of eight-week-old Nipponbare's shoots, while increasing the mean grain weight of four-month-old rice, while S. maltophilia induced growth parameters only at the first time point. All the different selected PGPR had a beneficial effect on at least one growth parameter on eight-weeks old rice, while having no effect (or a negative effect for Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278) two months later. However, only the selected PGPR inoculations triggers ISR by effectively reducing the extent of Xoo-induced chlorosis and necrosis compared to the non-adapted or the pathogenic bacteria inoculated plants and the control plants. Our interpretation of these results is that, under our conditions, the bacterial presence on rice roots alone may be sufficient to stimulate rice growth, either by inducing molecular root physiological changes upon bacterial recognition or by their impact on soil nutrients bioavailability, thanks to their colonisation of the rhizosphere and their own metabolism. Only the recognition of a beneficial bacterium on rice roots, which rewires systemic transcriptomic pathways, can lead to an ISR and effectively prepare its host against phytopathogen infection. This is independent of the degree of adaptation of the bacteria to rice, as the root phytopathogen does not induce a systemic acquired resistance-type response. It doesn't reduce rice growth either, perhaps our chosen bacterial dose or environmental conditions are not adapted to induce the pathogenicity of the pathogen. Its virulence is based on the production of tropolone and the functioning of the BCC-related quorum sensing system, inducing clear symptoms after 21 days post inoculation (with a starting inoculant of 10 mL OD 0.1, which is much more concentrated than the load we used) (Solis et al., 2006). Recent preliminary results done in our laboratory showed clear symptoms of Burkholderia plantarii ATCC43733 after 7- to 14- days of inoculation, under sterile hydroponic conditions (E. King, personal communication). The substrate used in our study was not sterilised prior to sowing, it is therefore possible that local microbiota may have dampened the phytopathogen inoculation. It could also explain why reported PGPR, such as Azospirillum sp. B510, did not show clear signs of rice growth enhancement after several [SOURCE: Page_292]

273 [SOURCE: Page_292]

months of growth (Complementary Results of Chapter 3). The bacterial load used to inoculate our strains may be too low and is diluted or dampened by the pre-established soil microbiota, explaining the discrepancies between theory and practice in our greenhouse experiments. The study of symbiotic compatibility between six rice cultivars and three AMF species allows us to understand that mycorrhizal colonisation of rice, and the associated beneficial effects on its growth and responses to phytopathogens, are dependent on the species of both organisms. We found that our four japonica varieties were more heavily colonised by both AMF than the two indica varieties. Overall, the japonica seems to benefit more from AMF symbiosis than the indica varieties, with better help on growth parameters. This was confirmed by molecular analysis of several rice health marker gene expression and Xoo tolerance. Current knowledge doesn't allow us to explain precisely this result, as previous studies have shown opposite results. It has already been reported that upland japonica rice cultivars have a higher degree of AMF colonisation and responsiveness than submerged indica rice, explained by their different root architectures and the co-evolutionary relationship between AMF and upland rice under aerobic selective conditions, increasing the efficiency of their interaction (Diedhiou et al., 2016, Davidson et al., 2019). A study involving 64 rice cultivars from all subspecies showed no clear separation in their mycorrhizal growth responses based on this particular parameter (Suzuki et al., 2015). Although it wasn’t clearly under consideration in their article. Huang et al. (2020) found that 14-day-old indica rice was more colonised than japonica, thanks to a natural variation in the AMF recognition receptor OsCERK$_1$. A likely explanation for the differences in symbiotic compatibility between rice and AMF could be variations in AMF recognition receptors between rice genotypes (Lefebvre, 2022). It should be noted that all these studies focused on mycorrhization at different time points: while Huang et al, 2020 looked at 14-day-old plants, we observed mycorrhization ten weeks after inoculation. There may be differences in colonisation rates over time for the different rice varieties, which should be investigated. To answer the question of the causes of this highly variable symbiotic compatibility between rice genotypes and AMF species, a larger panel of rice cultivars from indica and japonica subspecies, from all different ecological backgrounds (used in flooded fields, irrigated fields, etc.), should be established. Parameters such as mycorrhizal colonisation index, root development and branching, as well as effects on rice shoot growth, should be recorded in a time course experiment. With extensive research focused on understanding the mechanisms involved in the establishment and maintenance of AMF symbiosis, the changes in gene expression in the involved root cells have revealed important marker genes associated with AMF establishment and function (Chiu et al., 2018, Choi et al., 2018). Transcriptomic studies of these specific marker genes should be considered when comparing the mycorrhizal growth response of different rice subspecies. In a study recently done in our laboratory about Burkholderia vietnamiensis and Pk transcriptomic [SOURCE: Page_293]

274 [SOURCE: Page_293]

adaptation to two rice varieties colonisation, it was reported that their fitness and colonisation rates depends on more differentially expressed genes when colonising an indica than a japonica rice (Wallner et al., 2023). This suggest differences of host pressure between the two, a phenomenon that could also occur in AMF symbiosis with these two subspecies. It could be interesting to study the mechanisms implied in AMF establishment of a more or less symbiotic association depending on a diversity of plant hosts (species or cultivars) (Kiers et al., 2011). In the last results’ chapter, we observed that the bacterial inoculation of Pk on the early Rhizophagus irregularis - Nipponbare interaction had an effect on rice phenotype. Both single associations induced beneficial effects on this particular rice variety. Pk was reported to induce rice growth and yield parameters after several weeks of interaction (Mattos et al., 2008, King et al., 2023), as well as ISR (Chapter 2). The effects of RIR symbiosis on Nipponbare growth, systemic transcriptomic response and Xoo tolerance were reviewed in detail in Chapter 4 and highlighted RIR as both a biostimulator and biological control agent. The association of rice with the two microbes leads to reduced mycorrhizal colonisation without reducing the previously reported RIR-induced growth improvement. This could be explained by both microorganisms competing for phosphorus for their metabolism, as the substrate used was depleted of this key nutrient to promote mycorrhization. Competition for rice root colonisation sites is another hypothesis, which should be tested by microscopy analyses. Another possible explanation could be antagonism between the two microorganisms, but not to an extent that would be detrimental to their host, since growth is still induced in the co-inoculated rice shoots. This reduction in the benefits given up by RIR mycorrhization alone could be mitigated by Pk's own soil activities in the vicinity of rice roots (nitrogen fixation, ACC deaminase activities, etc.) (Mattos et al., 2008, Dias et al., 2019). However, while Pk induces ISR in a single association, it significantly reduced the RIR-induced mycorrhiza-induced resistance (MIR) phenotype onto the extent of Xoo chlorosis (Chapter 5). Further analysis on the specific pathways modulated by either the bacterial or fungal inoculation, or both, would give us clues to understand what are the common and specific molecular triggers of ISR and MIR. This was investigated using two complementary transcriptomic analyses: a targeted approach studying the expression of more than twenty marker genes of rice health status (growth, hormone balance, nutrient deficiency, defence responses), and a broader approach using RNAseq, which is currently in progress. [SOURCE: Page_294]

275 [SOURCE: Page_294]

Transcriptomic changes induced by the interaction with a beneficial microorganism depend on its degree of adaptation and the biotic environment Overall, we have examined the transcriptomic responses of rice under different types of conditions: under an early timepoint of interaction with different bacteria (Chapter 3), or after several weeks of growth, focusing on the systemic modulation of rice health marker genes (Chapters 2, 4 and 5). Our results highlight both a specific adaptation to each microorganism that rice encounters, as well as a very broad physiological and transcriptomic adaptation of rice, depending on the type of interaction we focus on. For example, the pool of differentially expressed genes (DEGs) modulated by all the PGPR selected in Chapter 3 represents only a small percentage of the total pool of DEGs modulated by at least one of them, highlighting the fact that plants modulate their diverse and complex molecular physiology specifically for each bacterium encountered. This is in concordance with previous studies on the transcriptomic regulations commonly modulated by multiple PGPR inoculations (Drogue et al., 2014, Valette et al., 2020, King et al., 2023). In this transcriptomic analysis, the 7 days post-inoculation timepoint was selected to avoid the initial plant defence burst due to bacterial inoculation (up to 1 dpi) and to allow for an advanced root colonisation state, without any visible phenotypic effect on rice development (King et al., 2019). At 7 days post-inoculation, PGPR induce a general increase in the expression of growth and stress-related genes, while repressing the expression of defence- related pathways (receptor recognition activity as well as pathogenesis-related proteins). In chapter 2, after inoculation with Pk and interaction for six weeks, only the expression of phosphate starvation and jasmonate-related genes were significantly affected. In Nipponbare leaves of plants mycorrhized with either Funneliformis mosseae, Rhizophagus intraradices or R. irregularis, a general pattern of expression of the different marker genes selected can be observed, with a clear repression of genes related to nutrient starvation or the PR$_5$ defence- related gene (Chapter 4). In Chapter 5, both RIR and RIR-Pk inoculated plants showed repression of SA-related pathways, with the expression of generalist defence-related marker genes being repressed in rice leaves. This comparison between our data should be taken with caution, as transcriptomic analyses were based on a thorough RNAseq analysis was performed in Chapter 3, while marker genes expression (RT-qPCR) was investiguated in chapters 2, 4 and 5. A complementary RNAseq is pending to dive deeper into the molecular responses approached in Chapters 2 and 5. Time is also an important parameter to consider in our comparison. Chapter 3 focused on local and systemic transcriptomic modulation in an early interaction, while Chapters 2, 4 and 5 considered the systemic overall modification of [SOURCE: Page_295]

276 [SOURCE: Page_295]

rice health related marker genes after several weeks of interaction. The RNAseq analyses of how rice responds to Pk, RIR, RIR - Pk, in a healthy state or in Xoo-infected leaves will give us more clues about the specificity of each type of interaction. [SOURCE: Page_296]

Transcriptional state of rice in interaction with a beneficial microbe Our results can be used to extrapolate the transcriptomic state of rice during interaction with beneficial microbes. First, in roots inoculated with either Azospirillum sp. B510, Pseudomonas defensor WCS374r or Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278 (Chapter 3), the expression of genes related to nitrogen sensing and cell growth are up-regulated, while those related to biotic and abiotic stress responses and recognition receptors are repressed. This is in line with results from previous analyses, which reported that nitrogen transporters and auxin-related genes are induced, while defence-related genes are repressed upon A. brasilense Sp^245 or Herbaspirillum seropedicae SmR$_1$ inoculation (Thomas et al., 2019, Brusamarello-Santos et al., 2012 & 2019). The systemic transcriptional response of rice to bacterial inoculation involves the upregulation of the expression of genes associated with cell rearrangement processes, while several transcription factors and defence responses to abiotic and biotic stimuli are repressed, marking the shift towards growth induction in the growth-defence trade- off (Chapter 3). This is consistent with our previous results (King et al. 2023), where several WRKY and PR genes were commonly down-regulated in rice leaves of plants inoculated with three phylogenetically close beneficial Burkholderia s.l. In Chapters 2 and 4, root association with Pk or an AMF species induces a clear repression of shoot nutrient starvation responses, highlighting the beneficial effects of these two microbes on nutrient bioavailability. In such, beneficial interactions with either a bacterium or a fungus shift the growth- defence trade-off, in a local and systemic way, beginning at the establishment of the interaction and still visible when the mutualism is several weeks old. [SOURCE: Page_296]

Induced systemic resistance transcriptomic pathways are influenced by the microbial type of the inducer and the selected timepoint Another stated aim of this thesis was to characterise the molecular triggers of ISR of both bacterial and fungal origin (MIR). I wanted to unravel the similarities and differences between the two induced systemic immune responses. It is generally reported that ISR establishment passes through the activation of jasmonate and ethylene-related pathways (Pieterse et al., 2014). This leads to a stronger and faster defence response upon pathogen [SOURCE: Page_296]

277 [SOURCE: Page_296]

infection recognition, depending on both JA-ET and SA related defence responses (van Wees et al., 2000, Tonelli et al., 2020). MIR occurs after inhibition of the SA-related defence response required for proper establishment of the AMF symbiosis and also relies on jasmonate and ethylene-related pathways for systemic signalling (Gupta et al., 2017, Dowarah et al., 2021). Both induced resistances lead to callose deposition, cell wall thickening, ROS scavenging, calcium influx and induction of PR gene expression (Pieterse et al., 2014, Dowarah et al., 2021). However, to our knowledge, no comparison of signals commonly or specifically involved in these interactions has been performed in the literature. This is what we proposed in my Ph.D. thesis, with a pending RNAseq analysis, but also hints with marker genes expression in Chapters 2, 4 and 5. Phenotypes of ISR and MIR were reported in all experimental chapters. Pk, P. defensor, Bradyrhizobium sp. ORS278, Azospirillum sp. B510 were all able to significantly reduce the chlorosis spread of the biotrophic phytopathogen Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae PXO99. In different rice varieties, Funneliformis mosseae, Rhizophagus intraradices and Rhizophagus irregularis induce the same phenotype. However, co-inoculation of Pk with RIR abolished this reduction in symptoms. After 24 h of infiltration with the same phytopathogen, the systemic transcriptional response of rice was assessed for each type of interaction. However, no clear modulation of ISR- or MIR-related pathways was observed in the different data sets. Overall, OsJAZ$_6$ in Pk- inoculated rice, in addition to OsNPR$_1$, OsMYC$_2$ and OsAOS$_1$ and several defence-related genes in RIR-inoculated rice leaves, are down-regulated upon Xoo infiltration, highlighting a repression of the JA and SA pathways at this time point. Initial reports of the modulation of JA, ET and defence-related pathways were seen as early as 24 h post-inoculation, we based our analysis on this timepoint to assess ISR as early as possible (Shoresh et al., 2005). Transcriptomic delays have already been reported in the literature, e.g. the modulation of PR proteins, repressed at 24 h and induced at 3 days post-inoculation of two fungal pathogens on mycorrhized rice or soybean (Campos-Soriano et al, 2012, Gao et al., 2012). To our knowledge, the comparison between ISR and MIR in a time course experiment has not yet been investigated. This could be interesting for two main reasons. Firstly, as Xoo is a biotrophic phytopathogen and its symptoms on rice only become visible 14 days after inoculation under our conditions, our chosen time point may not be suitable to investigate the true responses of induced resistance to this pathogen. In Chapters 2 and 5, signatures of defence down-regulation were seen in Xoo-challenged, beneficially inoculated rice, with a growth/defence trade-off favouring growth induction over Xoo-responsive effects at 24h post- inoculation. A kinetic approach could then be used to compare the similarities and differences of the ISR and MIR pathways at several timepoints and could highlight delays in the induction of the same physiological pathway modulations between the two induced mechanisms. [SOURCE: Page_297]

278 [SOURCE: Page_297]

To go further, a RNAseq approach linked to chapters 2 and 5 is on-going in the team. Instead of focusing on common marker genes, we will be able to decipher the transcriptional states of rice during a beneficial bacterial or fungal interaction or co-interaction. We will therefore be able to identify common or specific pathways for these two types of beneficial interaction. Finally, these RNAseq will allow us to further decipher the presence of an ISR at this time point, as well as the common or specific pathways modulated upon a bacterial or fungal induced systemic resistance. [SOURCE: Page_298]

Conclusion and perspectives This thesis focused on the phenotypic and molecular responses of rice to beneficial microbial interactions, elucidating the mechanisms of specificity of rice interaction with bacterial and fungal beneficial endophytes. This led to the understanding that rice responds specifically to each beneficial bacterium it encounters, which could be based on the beneficial role they play in the vicinity of its roots, by their presence and metabolism or direct interaction. Beneficial interactions shift the growth-defence trade-off at both local and systemic levels. However, relationships between inoculants and pre-existing soil microbiota are still difficult to predict, as exemplified during my work with a beneficial endophyte bacterium inhibiting a systemic resistance phenotype induced by mycorrhization. To deepen the discovery of the mechanisms of specificity that make a microbial partner compatible with its target host, approaches combining phenotypic and transcriptomic studies should be carried out in time-course experiments. Among the various aims of my PhD thesis, it would have given us more evidence on the physiological mechanisms that take place during the establishment of a beneficial interaction, as well as the beneficial effects taking place at a systemic level (Chapters 2, 3 and 4). It would allow us to understand if there are rice transcriptomic switches or delays in expression of the same physiological pathways upon PGPR or phytopathogenic bacteria interaction. It would enable us to link the rice roots colonisation potential of AMF with biomass production and rice health marker genes. Finally, the underlying molecular switches that induce systemic resistance during bacterial or fungal mutualism will reveal the common and specific modulation of ISR and MIR. Harnessing the mechanisms of specificity of interaction in mutualism with one or more microbial species will contribute to the exploration of new sustainable bioinoculation methods. Knowledge of the drivers of symbiotic associations between multiple microbial species will help in the making of new formulations dedicated to specific crops and their microbiota, and enhance their resilience in the environment. [SOURCE: Page_298]

Résumé de la thèse La transition de l'utilisation conventionnelle d'engrais chimiques et de pesticides vers des pratiques agricoles biologiques durables, sans perte de rendement, est une préoccupation majeure en agronomie. Cette transition nécessite l'étude du microbiote associé aux racines des cultures, car il détient la clé de la formulation de stratégies efficaces d'inoculation biologique. Le microbiote racinaire, qui comprend une diversité de microorganismes, joue un rôle crucial dans le cycle des nutriments du sol, le développement des racines et de la plante entière, et la médiation de la pression des pathogènes. Parmi ces microorganismes bénéfiques, les rhizobactéries favorisant la croissance des plantes (PGPR) et les champignons mycorhiziens à arbuscules (AMF) sont particulièrement intéressants, mais leur efficacité en champ dépend des conditions du sol et des espèces végétales inoculées. Ma thèse se concentre sur le riz, qui revêt une importance économique particulière en Asie de l'Est et du Sud. Sa production, principalement dans des champs inondés, consomme une part importante des ressources mondiales en eau douce et contribue aux émissions de gaz à effet de serre. La transition vers des méthodes d'irrigation économes en eau est une alternative satisfaisante, permettant l'utilisation de bioinoculants contenant des PGPR et des AMF, mais elle modifiera de manière significative les interactions entre le riz et les microorganismes naturellement présents. Les formulations actuelles de bioinoculants manquent de résilience et de spécificité, ce qui souligne la nécessité d'identifier et de tester des candidats naturellement issus de champs de culture. Les études de co-inoculation avec des PGPR et des AMF montrent différents effets sur la croissance des plantes, allant de la synergie à l'antagonisme. Il est essentiel de comprendre les mécanismes sous-jacents de ces interactions pour prédire avec précision les phénotypes des plantes. La perception par les plantes de la diversité microbienne du sol implique des dialogues moléculaires définis entre les deux partenaires. Cependant, les mécanismes qui sous-tendent la discrimination des plantes entre les micro- organismes bénéfiques et pathogènes et l'activation des voies symbiotiques ou immunitaires ne sont pas encore clairs. Il est important de définir les facteurs qui conduisent au mutualisme ou au parasitisme dans une variété d'interactions riz-microbe, car ils déterminent l'établissement d'associations symbiotiques. Des études approfondies des interactions entre le riz et différentes espèces bactériennes et fongiques découvriront les réseaux moléculaires impliqués pour obtenir des informations sur les interactions entre le riz et les microbes. Cette thèse étudie les réponses phénotypiques et moléculaires du riz au cours d’interactions avec les microorganismes du sol. Plusieurs espèces de PGPR et d'AMF, basées sur une littérature récente revendiquant leurs bénéfices pour la santé du riz, ont été étudiées. Ce travail vise à découvrir les réponses communes et spécifiques du riz aux microorganismes bénéfiques, d'origine bactérienne ou fongique, afin d'établir des liens entre l'amélioration de la croissance, la tolérance aux stress biotiques et la régulation transcriptionnelle des voies physiologiques. [SOURCE: Page_303]
